===== page 3 =====
1. How to judge a tax system, and
Hawaiʻi’s today
This report examines a series of tax reforms, some of which would raise revenue and others
reduce it. A proposal that does not say where the revenue comes from, or where it goes, is
incomplete. Chapter 10 describes paired changes to the general excise tax (GET), income tax
and food/excise tax credit, which the final report will estimate. We call the second change in
each pair the financing change, or offset.
Except for user or benefit charges, the source of tax revenue does not ordinarily determine how
it should be spent. We should spend the revenue where it does the most good for residents,
taking account of the cost. That need not be on the people or activities it came from. We
therefore consider each tax together with what it pays for. Who would gain, who would lose,
and how would their responses change those gains and losses and the revenue available to
the state? Where the answers depend on assumptions, we explain what we have assumed and
why it matters.
Take a grocery exemption from the general excise tax (GET), Hawaiʻi’s broad tax on sales.
Before allowing for changes in spending, we estimate that exempting groceries would cost
the state and counties $267.4 million. If stores pass the full tax reduction through to prices,
residents would save $214.1 million and visitors $53.3 million. Suppose residents’ income
taxes go up by enough to make up their $214.1 million. Residents as a group would pay as
much in additional income tax as they save on groceries. Individual households could still gain
or lose, because their grocery purchases and income-tax payments differ. Visitors keep their
$53.3 million and pay no more income tax, so the state and counties are still $53.3 million
short.
One of the pairs in Chapter 10 is a grocery GET exemption with higher income-tax rates to
replace the State General Fund revenue loss. If residents fund that offset, they also pay for
the visitors’ share of the General Fund loss.
Why should it matter which tax raises the revenue? Different taxes fall on different people,
and behavior changes when taxes change. When we tax people, we can reduce their value
to the tax system, because they might work less, they might earn less, or they may be more
likely to turn down a raise, and they can do this either by changing how much they actually
earn or by recharacterizing their income. They might even remove themselves from the tax
system entirely, via migration or labor force participation decisions.1
1 Saez, Slemrod and Giertz (2012).
1

===== page 4 =====
Hawaiʻi Tax Review Commission · Draft report 1. How to judge a tax system
By contrast, the supply of land is fixed.2 Taxing land can lower its market value because buyers
account for the taxes they will pay, but it does not reduce the amount of land. Buildings are
different: taxing new construction and improvements can discourage investment.
We care about how responsive a tax base (whatever the tax is levied on) is, meaning how
easily it can shrink, move or hide, and we care about spillover effects, meaning the effects a
tax has on people other than the taxpayer, from the workers whose pay depends on a business
to the collections of other taxes. The cost of raising a dollar depends on both: it is higher when
the base can move or hide, and higher again when the response spills over onto other people.3
When people ask whether a state’s tax system is competitive, they are mostly asking how easily
its tax bases can move somewhere else. This shows up in several ways. Residents can move
to another state and take their future earnings with them, and people deciding where to live
and work can choose not to come. Businesses can make new investments in another state, or
report their profits there while the work stays here. Visitors can choose another destination,
or buy at home what they would otherwise have bought here. Owners of wealth can move
it, or themselves, to where it is taxed less. Section 1.3 takes up people, business capital (the
buildings, equipment and other assets businesses use to produce goods and services) and
wealth, section 1.4 takes up visitors, and section 1.6 asks how much taxes matter for these
choices compared with everything else that makes a place attractive.
Not every response is about competitiveness. People can also change when they do things,
such as when they sell an asset and realize a gain, or how they label their income, such as
reporting wages as business profits. These responses shrink the base too, but they usually do
less economic harm than people working less, businesses investing less because taxes leave
them with less of the return, or people leaving the state.
We draw on evidence from other places to assess how Hawaiʻi’s tax bases respond. We give
the population, place and period for each of these estimates because Hawaiʻi could be more or
less responsive than the place it comes from (Appendix B gives the standards, and Appendix
D the estimates). We report gains and losses to Hawaiʻi residents, and separately the gains
and losses when we count everyone, including visitors and nonresidents (section 1.2).
The pairs in Chapter 10 are set to hold State General Fund revenue constant in 2027. A reform
can leave the General Fund unchanged while reducing county revenue.
1.1 Hawaiʻi’s tax mix
The GET and the individual income tax account for most of the tax revenue the state collects.
Including taxes collected by counties, we find that Hawaiʻi gets much more of its revenue from
sales taxes (broadly conceived) and much less from property taxes than the United States as
a whole. This mix reflects in part the fact that only the counties are allowed to tax property
but fiscal responsibility for education lies with the state, limiting counties’ need for high rates

with higher implicit taxes on land have faster growth in population density and business establish-
ments. Their measure captures differences between how tax assessors and buyers value land relative
to buildings (pp. 22, 28).

to finance education spending. It also reflects the taxation of the spending of a large volume
of nonresidents, who pay the GET on almost all purchases of goods and services.
In fiscal year 2025 (FY2025), total tax collections tracked by the Department of Taxation
(DOTAX) were $11.46 billion, up from $11.08 billion in FY2024.4 Fiscal years (FY) are gov-
ernment budget years; Hawaiʻi’s fiscal year ends on June 30. Tax years (TY) are the years
for which income is taxed. Of that revenue, $4.63 billion came from the GET and use tax
(40.4 percent of the total). The individual income tax brought in $3.29 billion (28.7 percent).
Together, they account for 69.1 percent of the total.
The next largest source of revenue is the transient accommodations tax (TAT) on hotel rooms
and vacation rentals at $816.2 million. The corporate income tax accounts for a relatively
small share: $400.4 million after subtracting refunds (3.5 percent of DOTAX’s total). Table

allows.6 A comparison of state governments leaves out Hawaiʻi’s property tax, which appears
only in a comparison of state and local taxes.
Property taxes are reserved for financing the counties. Each county sets its own rates and
exemptions and values the property. We include them here because this report has a lot of
discussion of property taxes, especially in chapter 5. For FY2025, the four counties levied
$3.06 billion in real property taxes, $1.73 billion of it in Honolulu.7 Changes to the property
tax are decided by the county councils and show up in county budgets.
On a state-and-local basis, Hawaiʻi collects a smaller share of its taxes from property than the
US does, and a larger share from sales and excise taxes. In FY2024, property taxes made up
18.9 percent of Hawaiʻi’s state and local taxes, against 29.4 percent nationally. General and
selective sales taxes together made up 49.6 percent, against 35.2 percent, while the individual
income tax was close to the national share, at 22.4 percent against 21.4 percent (Figure 1.1).8
Among state governments alone, general sales taxes were 45.3 percent of Hawaiʻi’s collections,
against 31.6 percent for all states, and the corporate income tax 4.9 percent, against 11.2
percent.9
By the Tax Foundation’s measure, the GET has the broadest base among U.S. general sales
taxes. This broad base dates from before statehood: the GET already covered services and
transactions between businesses. It taxes most services and many business purchases, and in
FY2024 its implied base equaled 116 percent of the state’s personal income (the total income
of its residents), against a median of 36 percent among the 45 states with a general sales tax.
This ratio overstates how broadly the tax falls on residents, though, because nonresidents’
spending and business purchases are in the base, while personal income counts only what
residents earn.10 Because the GET taxes business-to-business sales as well as final sales, it
can cascade through a chain of production, which is known as tax pyramiding, and it gives
businesses an incentive to bring more stages of production into the same business. Chapter 3

tion). Hawaiʻi Revised Statutes (HRS) §237D-2.5(a) caps county TATs at 3 percent and gives county
finance directors the collection powers. All four counties adopted a 3 percent rate in 2021: Hon-

Comparisons like these need care, because collections, tax burdens and rankings of tax codes
measure different things. DOTAX and the Census Bureau report what governments collect. The
Tax Foundation and the Tax Policy Center explain and compare tax structures and revenue
sources. Estimates of who bears a tax ask how prices, wages and returns to ownership adjust.
The person legally responsible for a tax need not be the person who ultimately bears its cost
(section 1.2).11
Even with the same tax rates as everyone else, a state that has a lot of visitors and little heavy
industry would get more of its revenue from consumption. A state government will also appear
to collect more if it pays for things paid for by counties or school districts in other states.
Hawaiʻi’s state and county governments together collect more tax per person than those of al-
most any other state. In FY2024 they collected $10,210 per person, against $7,246 for the US
as a whole, which puts Hawaiʻi second among the 50 states, just above California at $10,177

Census data. Its general-sales category excludes selective sales taxes. Tax Policy Center, “What are
the sources of revenue for state and local governments?” and “How do state and local general sales
and gross receipts taxes work?” explain the categories. General revenue also includes charges and
intergovernmental transfers, so its shares are not shares of tax collections.

Both figures include taxes paid by visitors and by owners who live somewhere else (section
1.2). The share of personal income divides those taxes by what residents alone earn, so it
overstates what residents pay. Neither figure allows for the cost of living here.13
The state government collects most of the revenue, 72.17 percent of Hawaiʻi’s state and local
taxes in FY2024, against 59.75 percent for state governments in the US as a whole. This is in
part because the state pays for the public schools and there are no school districts with taxes
of their own, while only the counties tax property.14
The other states in the table show how differently states raise their revenue. California col-
lects almost as much per person as Hawaiʻi but gets 30.73 percent of it from the individual
income tax and 18.85 percent from general sales taxes. Washington and Nevada get almost
nothing from individual income taxes and 49.03 and 44.77 percent from general sales and
gross receipts taxes. Oregon has no general retail sales tax and gets 34.25 percent from the
income tax. Hawaiʻi falls in between on both taxes, and it gets a smaller share from property
than any of them.15

1.2 Residents, nonresidents and who bears a tax
Who hands over the revenue tells us little about who pays the tax in practice. The public stud-
ies summarized by PFM Group Consulting estimated that nonresidents paid roughly a fifth to
nearly a third of Hawaiʻi’s state and county taxes.16 Just because the law says a business is
legally responsible for a tax, or has to remit it (send in the payment), does not mean the busi-
ness actually pays. Taxes change prices, wages, rents and returns, and the loss of purchasing
power can fall on someone else. These are the people who bear the tax.
The law requires the business to pay the GET but allows it to add up to 4.712 percent to the
16 PFM Group (2017), p. 58; DOTAX (2006), p. 12. The estimates cover different historical periods.
8

===== page 11 =====
Hawaiʻi Tax Review Commission · Draft report 1. How to judge a tax system
price on the receipt in counties with a surcharge (now every county).17
The tax may lead to higher prices for customers or lower incomes for the owners, workers and
landlord, depending on how easily they can change what they do. If a customer is willing to
go to another business as soon as prices rise, they will not pay much of the tax. On the other
hand, if a building cannot be used for anything else, its landlord may end up paying a lot. In
general, the tax is paid to a greater extent by those who find it harder to change what they
do, so if we tax something that cannot move, the owner tends to pay.
Nonresidents’ share differs a great deal from one tax to another. DOTAX estimated that non-
residents paid about 64 percent of the TAT.1 8 Past estimates put their share of the GET at
around 30 percent,19 and DOTAX’s estimates for each tax are shown in Figure 1.3.2 0

Hawaiʻi Tax Review Commission · Draft report 1. How to judge a tax system
Some nonresidents own property or businesses in Hawaiʻi, which helps explain why they pay
such a large share of the corporate income tax and the tax on non-residential property. They
are not all visitors. Visitors alone have been estimated to pay about 19 to 25 percent of GET
collections, less than nonresidents as a whole.21
A tax with a large nonresident share is not necessarily a good tax to increase. If a visitor tax
discourages travel, tourism jobs and wages can fall; with a large enough response, total tax
revenue could fall too. Residents work in those jobs, so we have a substantial stake in their
well-being. Lower incomes and less visitor spending also reduce revenue from other taxes.
Whether a reform looks beneficial depends in part on whose gains and losses we count. A
dollar paid by a nonresident is a dollar a resident does not have to pay, but someone still
pays it. To give readers all the information they might want, we report the gains and losses
to Hawaiʻi residents, and also the totals when we count everyone, visitors and nonresidents
included.22
1.3 Responsiveness: whether a tax base can shrink, move or hide
Other things equal, taxes do the least damage when the base cannot easily shrink, move or
hide. Hawaiʻi has land, which cannot move, and at the other end of the range, wealth that
can be hard even to see. People can reduce the amount of activity we tax by working less,
selling fewer homes or investing less. They can move the activity to another state, move there
themselves, or report their income there. Or they can avoid reporting it, report a value below
what it is worth, or describe it as something taxed at a lower rate. The more ways people have
to respond, the less revenue we get from a tax increase, and the greater the cost per dollar
raised to people who change what they do to avoid the tax.
Enforcement matters because a tax base is easiest to hide when the government cannot mea-
sure it. Property is difficult to hide, making property taxes difficult to evade, and every taxable
parcel of land has to be valued by the county for the property tax.23 When income is reported
by someone else to the tax authority and tax is withheld, there is rarely much error in how
much income is reported. In U.S. estimates of income omitted or misreported on tax returns
for tax years 2014-2016, the net misreporting of wages and salaries subject to withholding
and third-party reports was about 1 percent; for income with little or no third-party reporting,
such as income from self-employed businesses outside farming, it was about 55 percent.24
21 PFM Group (2017), pp. 52–54: previous studies put visitors’ share of GET collections at about 19
to 25 percent; visitors are a narrower group than all nonresidents.
22 It is not our role to decide how much weight each of these deserves. That is for the people of Hawaiʻi,
the Commissioners and policymakers.
23 Slack (2010), PDF p. 3.
24 Internal Revenue Service (IRS, 2022), printed pp. 13–14, tax years 2014-2016: the net misreporting
percentage is 1 percent for income “subject to substantial information reporting and withholding”,
6 percent for income with substantial information reporting but no withholding, and 55 percent for
income with little or no information reporting. The percentages measure the net amount of income
reported in error; they do not measure the share of taxpayers who misreport. Slemrod (2018),
p. 22, calls this variation “stark and compelling evidence for the primary importance of deterrence”.
A randomized audit study in Denmark finds the same pattern: evasion of 0.23 percent for positive
third-party-reported income and 17.1 percent for positive self-reported income (Kleven et al. 2011,
pp. 670–671).

DOTAX’s own enforcement is substantial. In FY2025 it issued $214 million in assessments,
conducted 27,709 audits and collected $284 million in delinquent taxes. DOTAX reported
$36.3 million in impact revenue from its Special Enforcement Section, including $5.0 million
in TAT.2 5 Each tax chapter discusses how enforcement shapes its own base.26
Figure 1.4 gives a rough ordering of Hawaiʻi’s tax bases from the least responsive to the most:
land, property sales, people and their paychecks, capital gains, business capital, and wealth.
The ordering is illustrative, with no measured rankings or magnitudes, and can change with
a tax’s design and enforcement. A property tax where values are assessed frequently and
accurately would stay near the top, while an income tax with preferences that encourage
people to change how they classify income would move toward the bottom.
Figure 1.4. A rough ordering of responsiveness
Note: Illustrative. Rates are current law as of October 1, 2026. Source: UHERO, with the statutes cited in section
1.3.
At the top is land. Land does not move. A tax can reduce its value but cannot make the land
disappear, so the burden falls on whoever owns it when the tax is announced. Hawaiʻi’s county
property taxes fall on buildings as well as land. Buildings can respond: tax them more heavily
and there will be less building (chapter 5).27

Hawaiʻi Tax Review Commission · Draft report 1. How to judge a tax system
Property sales come next. Property transactions can be moved in time, so Hawaiʻi’s conveyance
tax can have a lock-in effect: people may respond by delaying the sale of their home. The tax is
0.10 to 1.25 percent of the full price depending on the price band and the type of sale.28 And
because the rate applies to the whole price, the tax jumps at every band boundary. Transactions
can respond strongly to taxes like this: a temporary removal of a 1 percent transaction tax in
the United Kingdom raised housing market activity by 20 percent in the short run. In New
York and New Jersey, sales bunch just below the $1 million threshold of a 1 percent “mansion
tax” on the full price.29
People and their paychecks are more responsive. On top of working or earning less, people
can move away, which land cannot. A resident who simply works less still pays Hawaiʻi tax
on whatever she earns. If she moves away for good, the state gets none of her earnings from
elsewhere, although her Hawaiʻi-source income (wages for work done here or the gain from
selling Hawaiʻi real estate) remains taxable here.30
Spending more than half the year away is not enough to end residency, which depends on
domicile: roughly where a person has a permanent home and means to return.31 Figure 1.5
compares land, a salaried worker and a self-employed owner. Most people rarely move across
state lines. Section 1.6 looks at the evidence on how often taxes cause them to.
Figure 1.5. Can the tax base shrink, move or hide?

from 0.10 percent on properties under $600,000 to 1.00 percent at $10 million or more; sales of
condominiums and single-family homes to buyers who are not eligible for a county homeowner’s
exemption pay 0.15 to 1.25 percent.
29 Best and Kleven (2018), p. 157 (activity rose “by 20% in the short run”, and less than half of the

Note: Illustrative. Source: UHERO, with the rules cited in section 1.3.
Capital gains move more easily than people do, and their owners can choose when to pay tax
on them. A capital gain is the profit an owner makes when she sells an asset for more than she
paid for it. The tax is due when the owner sells, so she can choose a year in which her income
is low or the tax rate has been cut. With some exceptions, she can avoid the tax entirely by
leaving the asset to her heirs: inherited assets are valued as of the date of death (the “step-up
in basis”), so there is no income tax on the gain up to that point.32 Hawaiʻi’s top tax rate on
qualifying long-term gains is 7.25 percent.
If an owner actually moves out of the state, her future gains on stocks and other securities
can go with her. Gains on Hawaiʻi real estate remain taxable here (chapter 2).33
Business capital is easier still to move. New investment can be made in another state, and
a business can report its profits in a different state even if the work stays here. Hawaiʻi’s
corporate income tax rates are 4.4, 5.4 and 6.4 percent.34 In US states, a percentage point
increase in the corporate tax rate reduces employment by about 0.4 percent in the short run
at existing establishments of corporations that operate in more than one state. Roughly half of
that response comes from moving activity to establishments of the same corporations in other
states.35 For a hotel or a utility, the customers are here, which ties some activity to Hawaiʻi.
How much businesses respond depends on which ones are taxed (chapter 6).
Wealth is the base with the most ways to respond. In a sense, through the counties’ property
taxes, Hawaiʻi is already taxing the part of wealth that is easy to tax. Private-company shares
and art can be hard to value, and wealth held in trusts can be hard to attribute.36 A tax on
this wealth gives people an incentive to move or to change how they report it.
When Swiss cantons cut their annual wealth tax rates, reported taxable wealth rose by at
least 43 percent after six years for each percentage point of the cut. In two cantons where
the records give more detail, about a quarter of the increase came from taxpayers moving
between cantons and about a fifth from the increase in house prices. The authors find that
little of the rest can be explained by saving. They interpret this as evidence of sizable evasion,
since no third party reports financial wealth to the tax authority.37
Twelve countries in the Organisation for Economic Co-operation and Development (OECD)
taxed individuals’ net wealth every year in 1990, and four still did in 2017.38 An estate tax
32 HRS §235-51(f) caps the tax on net capital gain at 7.25 percent; 26 U.S.C. §1014(a)(1) sets the
basis of inherited property at its “fair market value ... at the date of the decedent’s death”. Saez
(2017), p. 80: “Taxpayers choose when to realize capital gains.” The exceptions to step-up include
income in respect of a decedent.
33 HRS §§235-1 and 235-4(b).
34 HRS §235-71(a): 4.4 percent on taxable income up to $25,000, 5.4 percent to $100,000, and 6.4

firm already operates. The 0.4 percent is a change in employment per percentage point of tax rate.

avoids valuing wealth every year, because it measures wealth only once, at death. Hawaiʻi’s
estate tax exclusion is $5.49 million, with rates of 10 to 20 percent above that.39 Chapter 7
discusses a wealth tax and the estate tax.
1.4 Spillovers to workers, customers and other taxes
When a tax or spending policy changes behavior, it can affect other people’s incomes and
revenue from other taxes. If a business gets smaller or leaves, the people who work for it, buy
from it or sell to it can lose income, and less revenue is collected from other taxes. This can
be quite costly. Other things equal, raising a dollar costs residents more when the response
means lower wages, less spending on local goods or less revenue from other taxes. Some of this
happens in a few years, as when a business has fewer workers, and some takes a generation
(section 1.5).
The corporate income tax is one place where the spillovers may be large, although how large
is uncertain. The tax on corporations that pay income tax separately from their owners, called
C corporations, leaves investors with less of the return from a new building or piece of equip-
ment. An investment must therefore earn more before tax to be worthwhile, which can lead
a business to invest less or to invest somewhere else. Workers may then have fewer jobs or
less productive equipment to work with, and landowners may have fewer businesses looking
to rent their property.40
One influential estimate for US states assigns roughly 40 percent of the burden of state cor-
porate taxes to firm owners, 30 to 35 percent to workers and 25 to 30 percent to landowners,
although these shares are uncertain.41 In Germany, about half of the burden of a local business
tax fell on the workers of firms that paid it, in the form of lower wages.42
If business investment also makes other workers more productive and better paid, taxing it
can impose costs beyond those borne by the firm itself. For a state, a corporate tax cut can
even raise total revenue once the effects on other taxes are counted, because businesses can
move between states much more easily than between countries. Suárez Serrato and Zidar’s
model calculation, using 2010 Hawaiʻi data and national estimates of how businesses respond,
suggests this for Hawaiʻi.43 Chapter 6 looks at this evidence.
Who gains from a cut is a separate question. After the 2017 federal corporate tax cut, 87 per-

Hawaiʻi Tax Review Commission · Draft report 1. How to judge a tax system
cent of the short-run private income gains went to the top tenth of the income distribution.44
S corporations and partnerships generally pay no federal income tax themselves, and their
profits pass through to their owners’ returns.45 Chapter 6 discusses these pass-through busi-
nesses.
Visitor taxes involve both responsiveness and spillovers. Once visitors are here, they have to
sleep somewhere, and studies find lodging demand fairly insensitive to hotel taxes. A designer
handbag, on the other hand, can be bought at home.46 If that is right, taxing hotels more
heavily than handbags raises the same revenue with less lost visitor spending, as long as
visitors do not shorten their stays. On the other hand, a visitor tax heavy enough to discourage
trips to Hawaiʻi reduces jobs and wages in the tourism industry. It costs residents income as
well as revenue (chapter 4).
A change in one tax can also affect revenue from other taxes. If we raise the TAT and there is
no change in the rooms people book or the prices they pay, we get the full increase in revenue.
But if the higher TAT starts discouraging people from coming or from staying as long, the
state also loses the GET on what they would have spent outside the hotel and loses income
tax revenue as tourism jobs and wages fall.
Revenue from the counties’ own TATs and the GET surcharge also changes when receipts from
rooms and other spending change (Figure 1.6). Federal revenue estimates already allow for
these kinds of changes in behavior, holding the size of the economy constant.47

One caveat is separate from these spillovers, because it comes from what the revenue pays
for. Public spending can also change tax bases and the need for other spending. For example,
spending on schools may increase what students earn later in life and what nearby homes are
worth. The state pays for Hawaiʻi’s public schools, but any additional property tax revenue
goes to the counties. Higher property values expand the potential tax base, and how much
revenue they bring depends on assessments and the rates counties set.48 Section 1.5 takes up
payback of this kind, which can take a generation.
1.5 Budget payback across a generation
Changes caused by either taxes or public spending can return some of a policy’s cost to the
budget, or add to it. A credit might lead a parent to enter the labor force, in which case that
parent would contribute something to income tax revenue. That can offset some of the cost
of the credit in the first place, an effect we call budget payback. It can be partial or slow, and
48 Black (1999), “Do Better Schools Matter? Parental Valuation of Elementary Education,” uses atten-
dance boundaries within school districts to estimate how school quality is reflected in house prices.
This supports a possible link, not an estimate of Hawaiʻi’s fiscal return. The state/county division is
described in section 1.1.
16

===== page 19 =====
Hawaiʻi Tax Review Commission · Draft report 1. How to judge a tax system
it can even be negative, as when a tax increase drives taxpayers away and reduces revenue
from other taxes too.
Payback through children takes the longest. When the benefits of a policy go to families with
children, it is possible that much of the return comes from the children themselves, who are
more likely as adults to work steady jobs and less likely to rely on public assistance. The
budget sees that return only once those children are adults.
Children who grew up after their counties gained access to food stamps, which spread across
the US between 1961 and 1975, were less likely to have metabolic syndrome as adults, and
the women among them became more economically self-sufficient.49 Children whose families
gained more from the earned income tax credit (EITC), a credit that adds to the earnings
of low-income working families, during their teenage years were more likely to finish high
school and college, more likely to work as young adults, and earned more.50
Across a wide range of US policies, direct spending on low-income children’s health and edu-
cation has had some of the highest benefits per dollar of net cost (the cost after subtracting the
revenue the policy brings back), and many such policies have paid for themselves through the
taxes their beneficiaries later paid and the assistance they no longer needed (Figure 1.7).51
Figure 1.7. A slower spillover: US evidence on support for children and their adult outcomes
49 Hoynes, Schanzenbach and Almond (2016), abstract. The outcomes include metabolic syndrome;
the authors describe “effects decades after initial exposure”.
50 Bastian and Michelmore (2018), abstract: an additional $1,000 of EITC exposure at ages 13-18
raises high school completion by 1.3 percent, college completion by 4.2 percent, employment as a
young adult by 1.0 percent and earnings by 2.2 percent.
51 Hendren and Sprung-Keyser (2020), pp. 1209–1210: “Many such policies have paid for themselves
as governments recouped the cost of their initial expenditures through additional taxes collected
and reduced transfers.” Their measure of benefits per dollar of net cost is the “marginal value of
public funds”; for direct investments in low-income children’s health and education it has averaged
more than 5.

Hawaiʻi’s own credits could pay for themselves.52 Bastian and Jones look at the federal and
state budgets together and find that EITC expansions come close to paying for themselves.
Mothers who got more from the credit worked more, paid more payroll and sales taxes and
needed less public assistance, and this offset about 83 percent of the cost. Once the longer-run
effects are counted, the credits could pay for themselves in full. Some of the return goes to the
federal budget rather than the state’s: childhood Medicaid, for example, raised beneficiaries’
federal income and payroll taxes as adults and reduced their EITC payments.53
A high benefit per dollar of net cost does not mean the government gets that much back in
revenue, because some benefits go to families without ever returning to the budget, and a
child who grows up here may live and pay taxes elsewhere as an adult. The size and timing
of these returns are uncertain, so revenue neutrality in this report refers only to the State
General Fund in 2027.54
1.6 Competitiveness and fairness
Whether the tax system is competitive and fair depends on more than the tax rules themselves.
Judgments about fairness also depend on what we value.
Rankings based on top tax rates and the breadth of tax bases leave out much of the cost of
living and investing in Hawaiʻi. There are many things that make a place attractive to live
and invest in, and Hawaiʻi is far from other places. For a family in Honolulu, paying less tax
by leaving would mean moving across the ocean to new jobs away from family and friends.
For a business deciding where to expand, the location of its customers matters too. For many
Hawaiʻi businesses those customers are here.
Of the ways competitiveness shows up, the movement of people is probably the biggest open
question in Hawaiʻi tax policy. How much do people move in response to state taxes? Evidence
from elsewhere suggests that people do respond, mostly in a few groups.
US millionaires move between states slightly less often than everyone else (2.4 percent a year,
against 2.9 percent), and while taxes do influence where they go, the moves are too few to
52 HRS §§235-55.75 and 235-55.85.
53 Bastian and Jones (2021) treat the federal and state governments as one budget. They count pay-
roll, sales and unemployment-insurance taxes, and public assistance that includes cash assistance,
the Supplemental Nutrition Assistance Program (SNAP, federal food assistance) and public hous-
ing. Brown, Kowalski and Lurie measure federal tax payments and EITC receipt following childhood
Medicaid coverage. Bastian and Michelmore estimate the effects on children’s education, employ-
ment and earnings as adults. These studies establish several routes for fiscal payback.
54 For example, children whose families received more from the EITC were less likely as adults to have
earnings below the poverty line (McInnis, Michelmore and Pilkauskas 2026). Another paper finds
a similar effect on earnings before taxes and benefits. After taxes and benefits, there is no clear
change in poverty, in part because the benefits people get as adults make up much of the difference
(Parolin, Glasner, Mincy and Wimer 2025, abstract).

Hawaiʻi Tax Review Commission · Draft report 1. How to judge a tax system
change the top of a state’s tax base very much.55 Rauh and Shyu look at California’s 2012
increase in the top tax rate. They estimate that within the first year, about 45 percent of the
additional revenue was lost to behavioral responses, mostly because people who stayed in
California reported less income.56
Star scientists move between states in response to tax differences, and foreign superstar in-
ventors respond strongly to countries’ top tax rates, though the number of inventors working
in their home country barely changes.57 Over the twentieth century, higher state taxes in the
United States also meant fewer patents in a state, partly because inventors moved to other
states.58 When US states introduced an income tax after World War II, middle- and high-
earning households moved out to states without one, although introducing a new tax is a
much larger change than adjusting an existing rate.59 The response depends on the group
being studied and on the size of the jurisdiction, so an estimate from one group or place may
not hold for another.60 Where businesses locate is the subject of chapter 6, and chapter 11
discusses how Hawaiʻi could measure moves.
Many things besides taxes affect growth, and the evidence on taxes is mixed. In one study of
US states, the relationship of tax revenue and top income tax rates to growth and employment
was not stable across states or over time.61
Housing constraints can be a large drag on growth. In one model-based estimate, limits on
building in the most productive US cities reduced growth for the country as a whole because
workers could not move to where they would be most productive.62 There is also what the
tax revenue pays for and the natural amenities that attract people to Hawaiʻi in the first place.
With all of these things to consider, a tax change could improve Hawaiʻi’s ranking and still do
little to help residents if housing remains scarce.

Whether a tax is regressive depends on what we measure (annual income, spending or life-
time income) and the period we use. A tax is regressive if lower-income households pay a
greater share of their resources than higher-income households. Measured against annual in-
come, Hawaiʻi’s state and local taxes take about the same share from the bottom four-fifths of
non-elderly families and a smaller share from those at the top, a pattern the Institute on Tax-
ation and Economic Policy (ITEP) labels regressive. Under 2024 law at 2023 incomes, ITEP
estimates that the poorest fifth paid 14.1 percent of their income in state and local taxes, the
middle fifth 14.2 percent, and the top 1 percent 10.1 percent.63
Lower-income households spend more of their annual income, which makes a sales tax regres-
sive when measured against income. Measured against spending, a better guide to lifetime
resources, it is less regressive (chapter 3). And while a tax viewed by itself might in some
sense be regressive, no part of the tax system should be viewed in isolation, because the rev-
enue from it can fund a tax credit or a program for lower-income households that more than
offsets it.
The final report will estimate the effects of the paired changes to the GET, income tax and
food/excise tax credit in Chapter 10, and of other tax changes, on revenue and households, in-
cluding migration responses. It may also discuss revenue stability.

2. Individual income tax
Hawaiʻi is going through a period of change in its income tax, and some of the choices about
what comes next have dates attached. One of the reforms the state could make is to stop the
new top marginal rate of 13 percent before it takes effect in tax year 2027 (TY2027).1 The
expansions of the refundable credits made in 2023 end after TY2027.2 And under the status
quo, the plan for the future is that there will not be automatic adjustments for inflation: once
the last scheduled steps take effect, in TY2029 for the brackets and TY2031 for the standard
deduction, the numbers are fixed again, which is a way of gradually increasing the tax burden
through inaction as prices rise.3
The income tax can make higher-income households pay a larger share of their income, a fea-
ture called progressivity. It also taxes a different base from the GET: income rather than con-
sumption. Consumption is smoother than income: households absorb most temporary swings
in income and part of lasting ones (Blundell, Pistaferri and Preston 2008). A progressive in-
come tax can cushion a household’s loss of spending power when its income falls, because its
tax bill falls too.4 It is also a mechanism for redistribution, and in principle it can be a more
efficient way of raising revenue than the GET, whose rate on a purchase generally does not
vary with the buyer’s income.5 That depends on how high the rates are.6
How far progressivity can go is limited in three ways. People respond, in different ways at
the bottom and the top,7 and research has not settled how large those responses are (section
2.3).8 The income tax reaches more than wages, including capital gains, business income and
retirement income (sections 2.5 and 2.6). And it raises revenue almost only from residents,
while the TAT and the GET also reach visitors.9 Refundable credits can get money back to
residents, but they do so imperfectly. UHERO calculations show that a family of four with one
earner at $40,000 of wages loses about three quarters of its next dollar, most of it to federal
programs (section 2.4).
Throughout the chapter, current law going forward is the starting point, with Acts 46 and

Hawaiʻi Tax Review Commission · Draft report 2. Individual income tax
24 as scheduled and the 2023 credit expansions ending after TY2027. Income taxes affect
the income people report and, for some, where they live. The revenue estimates in the final
report will include changes in reported income, using a range of responses estimated in other
places (chapter 11). Results show the gains and losses to Hawaiʻi residents, and also the totals
counting everyone, including visitors and nonresidents (chapter 1). The goal is to be agnostic
with respect to values, but also realistic with respect to revenue, and to make it clear what
value judgments are embedded in each policy change.
2.1 The rate schedule under Acts 46 and 24
Over the next several years, going out to 2031, the income tax code is going to look quite
different, in the numbers, not the big picture. The big picture is that there are still a bunch of
tax brackets, and there is still a personal exemption and a standard deduction and so forth.
The tables are going to change a little bit every year, as a result of legislation passed during
the 2024 legislative session, Act 46, and again in 2026, Act 24.10
The brackets apply to taxable income. To get there, a filer first calculates federal adjusted gross
income (AGI): income included under federal tax rules, less specified deductions, before sub-
tracting the standard deduction or itemized deductions. Hawaiʻi AGI starts with that federal
amount and makes the additions and subtractions required by state law. Among the largest dif-
ferences are the exclusion of Social Security benefits and qualifying employer-funded pension
income that the federal government taxes. Hawaiʻi also excludes interest on US government
obligations and adds interest on most other states’ municipal bonds.11 The standard deduc-
tion, or itemized deductions, and personal exemptions are then subtracted from Hawaiʻi AGI
to reach taxable income.
For example, a single filer under age 65 with $50,000 of wages, no other income and no
adjustments has $50,000 of both federal and Hawaiʻi AGI. In 2026, subtracting the $8,000
standard deduction and one $1,144 personal exemption leaves $40,856 of taxable income.
The bracket thresholds apply to that $40,856. Neither AGI nor taxable income measures all
the resources available to a household: excluded pension income, for example, is still available
to spend.
The tax bracket thresholds are going to be increased, and the standard deduction is higher.
Instead of being in the second bracket, a filer may be in the first bracket. Instead of being
in the third bracket, maybe the second, and so forth. Filers are pushed down into lower tax
brackets, which means a lower marginal tax rate at most incomes, and everyone’s definition
of taxable income subtracts a little bit more.
10 Act 46 (SLH 2024), §1 (standard deduction, pp. 94-95) and §2 (rate schedules, pp. 95-101); Act 24
(SLH 2026), approved May 21, 2026, §2 (rate schedules from TY2027: single filers pp. 29-36, joint
filers pp. 12-21, heads of household pp. 22-28) and §9 (start date). Joint thresholds are twice the
single ones, and those for heads of household one and a half times (twelve brackets in TY2027-28,
eleven from TY2029). The online §235-51 still shows the future schedules from before Act 24; the
signed act controls.

Hawaiʻi Tax Review Commission · Draft report 2. Individual income tax
It helps to read these changes through two rates. The marginal rate is the tax on the next
dollar of taxable income: the rate goes up after taxable income crosses each threshold. The
average rate divides the tax bill by income. The figures below identify the income measure
used in that calculation. A change in the rate in one bracket changes the marginal rate for
people in that bracket and the average rate for everyone above it, since they also have income
in that bracket. So in some cases the state is changing taxes for everyone, and in others only
for people above a particular value of income.
Table 2.1 starts from the law before either act. In TY2023, a single filer subtracted a $2,200
standard deduction, where it had been since TY2013, and a $1,144 personal exemption. Tax-
able income was then taxed at 1.4 percent on the first $2,400, 3.2 percent from $2,400 and
5.5 percent from $4,800, rising through twelve brackets to 8.25 percent from $48,000 and 11
percent above $200,000. Joint filers’ amounts were twice these.12 Act 46 works on the stan-
dard deduction and the brackets. The standard deduction doubled to $4,400 in TY2024, is
$8,000 in TY2026 and TY2027, and rises in steps to $12,000 from TY2031 on (joint filers get
twice these amounts, so $24,000 at the end).13 The bracket thresholds widened in TY2025,
and the lower thresholds widen again in TY2027 and TY2029, the last scheduled change to
the brackets.14
By TY2026, the first $9,600 of a single filer’s taxable income is taxed at 1.4 percent, against
$2,400 before, and the 8.25 percent rate begins at $175,000 instead of $48,000.15 From
TY2029, the first $19,200 of taxable income is taxed at 1.4 percent, and the rate does not
reach 6.8 percent until $48,000.16 The Council on Revenues estimates that Act 46 reduces
General Fund revenue by $596.6 million in FY2026, rising to $1.45 billion in FY2032. Like
all forecasts, these numbers are preliminary.17
Act 24, signed in May 2026, changes the rates, starting in TY2027. It also keeps the 8.25, 9,
10 and 11 percent brackets at their TY2026 thresholds instead of the higher thresholds Act
46 scheduled for TY2027 and TY2029.18 It lowers the second rate from 3.2 to 2.5 percent
and the third from 5.5 to 5 percent, and it adds a 13 percent rate on taxable income above
$500,000 for single filers, $750,000 for heads of household and $1 million for joint filers.19

The 13 percent rate applies to the part of taxable income that falls into the top bracket, so a
single filer with $600,000 of taxable income pays it on $100,000.
Act 24 does both. Its lower second rate cuts tax for everyone above $14,400 of taxable income,
and its 13 percent rate reaches only a single filer’s taxable income above $500,000. In TY2027
the second rate applies between $14,400 and $19,200 of taxable income for a single filer,
so everyone above $14,400 has some income taxed at it.20 But keeping the upper-bracket
thresholds where they were raises tax at some incomes below that level, compared with Act
46 alone. The 13 percent rate does not take effect until TY2027, so the legislature could
still stop it. The final report will include an estimate of stopping it. The Council on Revenues
estimates that Act 24 as a whole (including its credit changes) raises revenue by $145.2 million
in FY2028, rising to $308.9 million in FY2033.21
If we compare with TY2023, Act 46 mostly worked through the standard deduction and the
thresholds, and then Act 24 changed the rates (Table 2.1). For a single filer with $50,000
of wages and no other income, the tax before credits falls from about $3,108 in TY2023 to
$2,025 in TY2026, $1,660 in TY2027 and $1,044 in TY2031, or from 6.2 to 2.1 percent of
wages. At the top, a single filer paid 11 percent on taxable income above $200,000 in TY2023.
From TY2027 the 11 percent rate starts at $325,000 and the 13 percent rate at $500,000.22

Hawaiʻi’s brackets and standard deduction are not indexed to inflation, and the lack of index-
ing has been a recurring reason for changing them. Unlike the federal standard deduction,
which is adjusted for inflation every year, Hawaiʻi’s is a fixed dollar amount that changes only
when a new law changes it. It was raised in 1987, 1989, 2007 and 2013, and Act 46 dou-
bled it in TY2024, eleven years after the last change.23 The 2020-22 Tax Review Commission,
reporting that nearly every earlier commission had recommended modernizing the standard
deduction and the personal exemption, recommended indexing the deduction, the exemption
and the brackets.24
Because the state held these numbers fixed for years at a time, we really should think of
them as something that was shrinking every year, because of inflation. What we care about
is what people can buy with their money, and if wages go up with the cost of living while the
thresholds stay the same, people end up in a higher tax bracket even though the purchasing
23 26 U.S.C. §63(c)(4) and (c)(7)(B)(ii) (federal inflation adjustments); HRS §235-2.4(a)(2) (Hawaiʻi
standard deduction). DOTAX, Hawaiʻi Individual Income Tax Statistics, Tax Year 2022, pp. 18-19 and
Table 9. The single filer’s deduction was $800 in 1982, $1,000 in 1987, $1,500 in 1989, $2,000 in
2007 and $2,200 from 2013 through 2023. Rates have also moved through occasional laws: the
9, 10 and 11 percent brackets applied in TY2009-15 under Act 60 (SLH 2009) and returned from
TY2018 under Act 107 (SLH 2017).

===== page 34 =====
Hawaiʻi Tax Review Commission · Draft report 2. Individual income tax
power of their income has not changed. Why is it that we had these really high marginal rates
on low-income people, with 5.5 percent from $4,800 of taxable income for a single filer? Part
of the explanation is the lack of inflation indexing.25
Inflation indexing is merely a decision to hold the tax system constant. By updating the tax
code to reflect inflation, the state can ensure that a household whose income has only risen by
the same amount as the prices it pays for goods and services will not end up paying a higher
share of its income in taxes.26 By contrast, a decision not to index the tax system is a decision to
increase taxes every year in a somewhat arbitrary, and sometimes even unpredictable, fashion.
Under the status quo, the schedule stops moving after the last steps, in TY2029 for the brackets
and TY2031 for the standard deduction, and the $1,144 personal exemption is not scheduled
to change at all.27 Adding indexing would decrease future tax revenues, and so if the state is
trying to hold future tax revenues constant, it needs to come up with some offset. We should
not present indexing as if it is free. But not indexing is a way of raising taxes that depends on
what inflation is, as opposed to something more deliberate.
We may be able to estimate the cost of indexing in the final report.
Different types of income are taxed differently, and someone with a very high income is not
necessarily taxed at the top rate. The tax on long-term capital gains is capped at 7.25 percent,
so a high-income filer whose income is partly gains pays less than the top rate on that part.28
In the other direction, some people have very little taxable income but substantial cash income.
Gains on assets they have not sold are not taxed until they sell, and qualifying employer-
funded pensions and Social Security are excluded from Hawaiʻi AGI, which took $7.1 billion
out of residents’ federally taxed income in TY2022.29 Section 2.5 takes up capital gains, and
section 2.6 pensions.
2.2 How Hawaiʻi’s income tax compares with other states
People often ask whether Hawaiʻi has one of the highest income taxes in the country, and with-
out saying compared with what, the claim means little. The answer depends on the household,
its income and the year. According to the PolicyEngine tax calculator, Hawaiʻi’s income tax is
among the lowest for a low-income family with two children, because its refundable credits
pay the family more than it owes. For a single person, it is above the typical state at every
income shown from $40,000 up. In 2027, a single person earning $1 million pays more state
income tax in Hawaiʻi than in any other state.

Hawaiʻi’s schedule has more brackets than any other state’s, and its top rate is second only to
California’s. In the 2026 survey of state income tax rates by the Tax Foundation, a national
tax policy advocacy group, 41 states tax wages, 15 of them at a single rate, and Hawaiʻi has
twelve brackets.31 Its 11 percent top rate in 2026 is second to California’s 13.3 percent. The
13 percent rate from TY2027 stays below California’s top rate and is above the 2026 top rates
of New York, New Jersey, Oregon, Minnesota and Massachusetts. The top rates begin at very
different incomes, though. Hawaiʻi’s 13 percent applies to taxable income above $500,000 for
a single filer, while California’s 13.3 percent applies only above $1 million.
Per resident, Hawaiʻi collected more individual income tax in FY2025 than all but seven states:
$2,294, about half again the national figure of $1,517. The individual income tax was 29.8
percent of state-government taxes, against 33.7 percent nationally.32 Collections per person
depend on incomes as well as on tax rates, and they include tax paid by nonresidents.
DOTAX’s own comparison, made before Act 46, put a family of four with $88,005 of income
second highest among the 41 states and DC with broad income taxes, at $5,086 of Hawaiʻi
income tax. DOTAX projected that Act 46 would bring the same family to the fourth lowest
by 2031, against other states’ laws at the time.33
None of these comparisons ranks the whole tax burden. They leave out the GET and other
taxes, what the revenue pays for and the cost of living, and section 1.6 explains why rankings
of this kind can mislead. They also say nothing about whether Hawaiʻi’s rates are too high or
too low. That depends on what the tax is for and how people respond, which is the subject of
section 2.3.

What progressivity buys
Progressivity is a mechanism for redistribution. In principle, and up to a point, a schedule
with different rates at different incomes can raise a given amount of revenue at lower cost
than one rate on everyone. The top rate that raises the most revenue depends on how people
respond and how much income lies above the top-bracket threshold.37 Whether that makes
the income tax a cheaper source of revenue than the GET depends on the two bases, on how
people respond, and on who bears each tax (Appendix D).
The income tax is also paid almost entirely by residents: DOTAX estimates that residents pay
37 The top rate beyond which a higher rate loses revenue depends on how much reported income
responds and on how far the incomes of people in the top bracket lie above its threshold (Saez 2001,
section 3). It is an outer limit on revenue, not a measure of efficiency, and it is not a computed rate
for Hawaiʻi: moves, other tax bases and federal taxes all change it, and how much the state values
a dollar to people at the top changes the rate it would choose. Appendix D sets out the formula.
36

===== page 39 =====
Hawaiʻi Tax Review Commission · Draft report 2. Individual income tax
92.9 percent of it.38 That raises the question of what justifies a tax raised almost only from
residents, instead of taxes that also raise revenue from visitors. One answer is that those taxes
cannot vary with a household’s income, so the income tax is where the state decides how the
burden is shared among residents. Another is that refundable credits can get money back
into residents’ hands if a tax aimed at visitors ends up falling on them, although section 2.4
explains why that mechanism is imperfect.
Small changes and gradual responses
It is tempting to ask at what point a rate becomes so high that people start to respond or to
move. There is no one rate that is too high, or one rate at which responses begin. A small
increase can tip some people’s decisions at the margin and a small decrease can tip others the
other way. Income can shift between tax years in response to a tax change.39
There are people making decisions constantly, there are many factors that contribute to those
decisions, and taxes are just one of them. When taxes go up, it just makes it a little harder to
justify staying, and that is enough to push some people over the edge.
At the top, moving is one of the responses, although high-income people do not move between
states more often than others overall.40 A rate change in one bracket raises the tax bill of
everyone above its threshold, and whether to stay is a decision for all of them, while the
incentive to earn and report one more dollar changes only for people inside the bracket.
Responses at the top
High-income residents facing a higher rate have several options. They might move. They might
relabel some of their income as long-term capital gains, which are taxed at no more than 7.25
percent, against 13 percent on ordinary taxable income above $500,000 for a single filer from
TY2027 (section 2.5).
They might also leave income in the business and then use the business to purchase things
that are basically consumption, and when those purchases are knowingly deducted as business
expenses, that is a form of tax evasion.41
Many of these options run through businesses. In the US, top income taxpayers receive a lot
of their income from pass-through businesses, S corporations and partnerships, whose profits
generally pass through to their owners’ tax returns, so one of the main ways that Hawaiʻi is
taxing business income is actually through the individual income tax. The IRS groups partner-
ship and S corporation income with other income that has some third-party reporting. That

Researchers use past tax reforms to estimate how reported income responds to tax changes.
Most estimates come from the United States and Scandinavia.43 Saez, Slemrod and Giertz
(2012) put the best available estimates of the long-run response between 0.12 and 0.40: when
the share of each extra dollar that a person keeps goes up by 1 percent, reported income goes
up by 0.12 to 0.40 percent.44 In California, most of the revenue lost to responses after the 2012
increase came from people who stayed and reported less (section 1.6)45 , and Rauh and Shyu
estimate that additional departures removed 0.8 percent of top-bracket income in 2013.46
We do not know if the responses from other places will generalize to Hawaiʻi. If something
makes it harder for people to leave, that is an opportunity for the state to tax them, and for
most residents, leaving Hawaiʻi means moving across an ocean, away from family and friends.
We have really good reason to suspect that migration responses should be different from one
state to another, because the reason that people live in a place has to do with all of the different
amenities of living in that place.47 For California, it would not be so crazy to use the estimates
from its own 2012 change to ask what its top tax rate should be. Hawaiʻi’s geography and
amenities can lead to different migration responses.48
When the state taxes people, they might work less, they might earn less, they may be more
likely to turn down a raise. But people can also do something land cannot: they can move
away. Then the state does not just collect less on the income they earn elsewhere. It collects
nothing. Hawaiʻi loses the income that it was taxing, but also potentially some amount of

business investment, if we are talking about local small businesses.49
If the state raises the top rate, it could go do some calculation based on assuming no one
changes their behavior. But some people might leave the state, some people might reduce
their income, and so it is not going to get 100 percent of the revenue it expects when it raises
a tax like that.50
Our household model gives a sense of the size. Raising the top rate from 13 to 14 percent
in 2027, for every filing status, would bring in about $46.0 million if nobody changed their
behavior. In this calculation, changes in reported income reduce the gain by about $3.4 million.
Lower household spending reduces GET revenue by another $0.8 million, leaving about $41.8
million, or 9 percent less than the gain without responses.51
We treat the $41.8 million as an upper bound on the revenue gain. The calculation uses an elas-
ticity of reported ordinary income of 0.3: reported income rises by 0.3 percent when the share
of each additional dollar people keep after tax rises by 1 percent. This is the lowest response
in the range we plan to consider. We expect people at the top to be more responsive, which
would reduce the gain further. The model also allows changes in whether people work, though
those responses are small at these incomes. Most importantly, the calculation leaves out mi-
gration responses. It also leaves out spillover effects on investment, employment and other
tax revenue from taxing business income. The final report will incorporate the wider range of
income responses, labor-force participation, migration and business-income spillovers to the
extent possible.
The final report will also estimate stopping the 13 percent rate and a one-point change in the 11
percent rate.
2.4 Refundable credits, effective marginal rates and claiming
A refundable credit can pay households that owe no income tax. A rate cut can help households
that owe tax, but cannot by itself provide a payment to those who owe none.52 This makes
refundable credits a way to compensate residents or direct more assistance to people with low

incomes, though the income tax system does both imperfectly.
As a family earns more, it loses its credits along with federal benefits: in UHERO calculations
for a family of four with one earner, the effective marginal rate, which counts taxes plus the
credits and benefits withdrawn, is about 76 cents of the next dollar at $40,000 of wages and
about 80 cents at $60,000. Some credits end in steps rather than gradually. Many people who
are eligible never claim: about $15.3 million of about $42 million in food/excise credits went
unclaimed in TY2021, most of it by people who did not file a return. And the credits are paid
once a year.
The credits
Hawaiʻi has four refundable credits aimed at lower-income residents. The earned income tax
credit (EITC) is 40 percent of the federal credit from TY2023 through TY2027. The food/excise
credit pays a fixed amount per person that falls as federal adjusted gross income rises, up to
$220 per person below $15,000, ending at $40,000 for single filers and at $60,000 for others,
and it can be claimed with no income at all. The dependent care credit pays 25 to 15 percent
of child care and other dependent care expenses, depending on Hawaiʻi AGI. The renters’
credit is for households with Hawaiʻi adjusted gross income below $30,000, a limit that has
not changed since 1989.53 In TY2023, filers claimed $77.1 million of EITC, $64.0 million of
food/excise credit, $14.8 million of dependent care credit and $1.6 million of renters’ credit.54
Several of these amounts are scheduled to fall. These cuts come from the end of Act 163 of
2023, which expanded the EITC, the food/excise credit and the dependent care credit through
TY2027. From TY2028 the EITC returns to 20 percent of the federal credit, still refundable, the
food/excise credit returns to its smaller pre-2023 schedule with lower income limits, and the
dependent care credit’s expense limits fall from $10,000 and $20,000 to $2,400 and $4,800.55
The final report will show refundable credits received by income group before and after the Act
163 expansions end.
Effective marginal rates and cliffs
When a family earns an additional dollar, it does not get to keep all of it. Some of it goes to
taxes, and some is lost because credits and benefits go down as the family earns more. The
effective marginal rate is the share of that dollar the family loses this way. That whole set of

incentives is what matters for people’s behavior, and the income tax is only one part of it. The
phase-out of the EITC and the phase-out of SNAP benefits are not so different from each other,
and we put them in the same calculation.
Figure 2.5 shows these rates for a family of four with one earner and two children, averaged
over the next $1,000 of wages at eight levels of wages. In 2026, at $40,000, federal and state
taxes together with the reduction in the federal EITC and SNAP food assistance take about
76 cents of the next dollar. Most of that comes from federal programs: the gradual reduction
in the federal EITC takes 21 cents and SNAP 36 cents, while Hawaiʻi’s income tax, including
the reduction in its own EITC, takes about 12 cents and federal income and payroll taxes the
remaining 8 or so. SNAP takes more than its 30 percent benefit reduction rate because each
extra dollar of earnings also shrinks the family’s deduction for housing costs.56
At $60,000 the rate is about 80 cents; by $80,000 the family has left these programs and the
rate falls to about 26 cents.57 Acts 46 and 24 help with this, but only a little: at $40,000 the
rate falls by nearly 2 cents, to about 74½ cents in 2027, all of it from the state income tax. The
end of the Act 163 expansions works in the same direction for this family, since from TY2028
the part of the rate that comes from the state EITC’s phase-out falls by half, from about 8 cents
to about 4 at $40,000, while the food/excise steps move to lower federal AGI. The family also
receives less credit. Its marginal rate falls because there is less credit to withdraw as earnings
rise, so the lower rate does not mean the family is better off.
56 Under the ordinary SNAP formula, monthly benefits equal the maximum allotment for the house-
hold’s size minus 30 percent of its net monthly income, subject to rounding and minimum-benefit
rules (7 U.S.C. §2017(a)). SNAP generally deducts 20 percent of countable earned income when
calculating net income, and it also deducts shelter costs above half of income after the other de-
ductions, up to a cap for most households (7 U.S.C. §2014(e)(2) and (e)(6)). While that shelter
deduction is positive and below its cap, an extra dollar of earnings reduces it by 40 cents, holding
shelter costs and other deductions fixed, so net income rises by $1.20 and benefits fall by about 36
cents, which is what the calculator gives at $20,000 and $40,000 for this family, with $18,000 of
rent. Once the deduction is used up, benefits fall by about 24 cents per dollar while they remain

state income tax and credits, the federal EITC, SNAP, federal income tax and credits, and employee
payroll taxes, over the next $1,000 of wages. From February 1, 2025, Hawaiʻi’s Department of Hu-
man Services stopped applying SNAP’s net income eligibility test to households qualifying under
broad-based categorical eligibility, which uses receipt of certain other assistance to establish eli-
gibility, though net income still determines their benefit (Department of Human Services, SNAP
eligibility page). Our calculations include this change, so the family still receives $6,831 of SNAP at
$60,000 in 2026, loses $240 of it over the next $1,000, and faces an effective marginal rate of about
80 cents there (78 cents in 2027). For comparison, the Congressional Budget Office (CBO) projected
an average combined rate of 31 percent in 2016 for low- and moderate-income US workers, with
78 percent facing at least 20 percent (CBO 2015, pp. 1 and 4).

Some of the credits end in steps instead of phasing out, and each step is a cliff. A cliff occurs
when a large benefit is suddenly withdrawn when income exceeds some threshold, even if
by only $1. A family of four filing jointly loses $280 of food/excise credit when its federal
42

===== page 45 =====
Hawaiʻi Tax Review Commission · Draft report 2. Individual income tax
adjusted gross income reaches $60,000, and smaller amounts at each lower step.58 For the
family in Figure 2.5 that loss comes exactly at $60,000, so it is not in the bar for the next
$1,000. A different family, with two earners, two children in care and $20,000 of child care
expenses, can lose up to $1,000 of dependent care credit just above $50,000 of Hawaiʻi AGI.
The renters’ credit ends entirely at $30,000 of Hawaiʻi AGI.
The problem with these credits is not that they fall with income. This ensures that the credits
provide cost-effective assistance to the families they are aimed at: if the state paid the full
food/excise credit to every family of four, whatever its income, its cost would be much greater.
Rather, the problem is the way in which the credits are reduced: via cliffs.
For a family whose only income is wages, with no adjustments to AGI, the food/excise credit
cliff leaves it with less to live on at $60,000 of wages than at $59,999, and it is unlikely that
most people think the family with one dollar more of income is $280 less deserving. That is a
cost in fairness, which a cliff carries even if no one changes what they do because of it.
Furthermore, cliffs do change what families do: researchers interviewing low-income families
in Colorado and California who faced a cliff in a child care subsidy found that many took
steps to keep their income below it, and some turned down extra hours or even turned down
raises.59 A self-employed parent can do it on paper, for example by rescheduling some business
expenses so they occur this year instead of next year, and a study of the federal EITC finds
that the reported incomes of people who report their own income to the IRS pile up at the
point where the credit reaches its maximum, while those of wage earners do not.60
A cliff can be removed without giving up targeting, by reducing the credit by a fraction of each
additional dollar earned. To keep the cost the same, the state has to start the reduction below
the old cutoff, which takes money from families there; any extension above the cutoff, which
gives money to families above it, must be paid for by starting earlier still. Either way, gradual
credit reductions still decrease the extra income that more earnings bring, so families in the
new phase-out range keep less of each extra dollar than before. Several modest phase-outs
that overlap can leave a family with little of its next dollar, which is how the rates in Figure
2.5 arise.
The EITC and the dependent care credit pull a family’s second earner in opposite directions,
which affects the labor force participation of second earners. The EITC depends on the couple’s
combined earnings, so when a second job puts those earnings in the credit’s phase-out range,
the credit shrinks. After federal EITC expansions from 1984 to 1996, participation in the labor
force fell by an estimated 1.1 percentage points among married women in couples with less
58 HRS §235-55.85(b). With four qualified exemptions in a joint or other non-single return, the credit
falls by $80 at $15,000, $120 at $20,000, $25,000 and $30,000, $80 at $40,000 and $50,000, and
$280 at $60,000. A single filer loses $110 per exemption at $40,000. The dependent care step is
$20,000 times the drop of 5 points in the rate above $50,000 of Hawaiʻi AGI (§235-55.6), for a

Eligible and claimed
A credit that is not claimed does not help. In TY2021, UHERO and DOTAX estimated that
about 158,000 eligible people did not receive the food/excise credit, either by claiming it
themselves or by being claimed by someone else, and that they missed about $15.3 million of
the roughly $42 million the credit was worth that year.65 About 17,800 of them were on tax
returns that did not claim the credit, and they missed about $1.3 million. The vast majority
were people who did not file a state tax return at all, most of whom were likely in very low-
income households, since otherwise they would have to file.
These credits can help eligible families only if they claim them. When a credit goes un-
claimed, the money never reaches the family it was meant to help. Existing filers can claim the
food/excise credit on their N-11 return, yet some do not claim the benefit.66 For the federal
EITC, which the state EITC builds on, the IRS estimates that 83.3 percent of eligible Hawaiʻi
individuals and families, including those who did not file, claimed it in TY2022. We estimate

Research suggests people do not claim because they do not know about a credit, or find
claiming confusing or time-consuming.68 In one IRS experiment, simpler letters to filers who
had not claimed the EITC raised the share who responded from about 14 to 23 percent.69
DOTAX’s simplified N-11SF form is aimed at the people who do not file. It is limited to filers
with zero federal and Hawaiʻi adjusted gross income, so a household with $1 under either
income measure has to use another form even though it is eligible for the same credit and
owes no tax.70 The credits are also paid once a year, while food and rent are paid every month,
which chapter 3 discusses for the grocery exemption. Not everyone has savings or is able to
borrow money. In a US study, EITC recipients spent about 30 cents out of every dollar of the
refund at retailers within two weeks of getting it.71 For families living paycheck to paycheck,
taking money at the register and giving it back in April changes what they can buy. Refunds
claimed on Form N-11SF also come only by paper check, with no direct deposit.72

claiming other tax-based payments.73 The effect of a reminder may also need to be renewed:
an IRS reminder raised filing in the year it was sent but not the next year, and a fresh reminder
raised filing again.74 Drawing people into filing can pay off for years. Low-income people who
filed to get a federal stimulus payment went on to claim more EITC and earn more, and were
less likely to be poor in later years.75

2.5 Capital gains
If a person buys a stock or something, and then sells it for more, this is their capital gain. In
this state, the tax on a long-term gain is either the person’s marginal income tax rate or 7.25
percent, whichever is lower,76 and then there are federal capital gains tax rates on top of that.
Other states treat gains differently.77 Capital gains taxes are, generally speaking, only levied
when you sell the asset.
Not all forms of income are tied to a person’s location in the same way. It is much easier to
avoid paying capital gains taxes, for one thing because of step-up in basis at death, and for
another thing because a resident can move, realize the gains in some other state that has zero
capital gains tax once the move is genuine, and there is nothing to stop them.
DOTAX notes that “the capital gains are heavily concentrated in the high end of the income
distribution” and that “the higher the income of taxpayers the greater the share of capital
gains in their taxable income.” In TY2022 residents reported $3.0 billion of gains eligible for
the cap on 43,443 returns, 7.4 percent of resident taxable income, and the 4,188 returns with
Hawaiʻi adjusted gross income of $400,000 or more reported $2.21 billion of it, about 74
percent. For those residents, gains eligible for the cap were 20.9 percent of taxable income,
and from TY2027 the gap between the top rate and the cap is 5.75 points.78
These numbers count only the gains people declare for the cap. Filers whose top rate is below
7.25 percent gain nothing from claiming the capital-gains cap.79 People with large gains might
not declare all of their capital gains, only enough to get the preferential treatment. So when
we look at the data from Hawaiʻi about capital gains, we do not know how much capital gains
people actually have.
If a person has enough income that is characterized as capital gains, they can get a very
favorable tax treatment. An owner’s work building up a business can be reflected in a capital
gain when the business is sold. Carried interest, the share of an investment fund’s profits paid
to the people who manage it, is another example, since it can be taxed as a capital gain under
partnership rules.80 DOTAX notes that gains partially explain “why the average effective tax
rates for this income group stay two to three percentage points below the marginal tax rate
76 HRS §235-51(f). Strictly, the tax is limited by an alternative calculation: ordinary tax on the larger
of taxable income less net capital gain and the income taxed below 7.25 percent, plus 7.25 percent
of the rest. It applies to net capital gain (net long-term gain less net short-term loss), not to sale
proceeds.
77 California Franchise Tax Board, “Capital gains and losses”; New York Tax Law §612 (New York
income starts from federal adjusted gross income); New Jersey Division of Taxation, “NJ Income
Tax: Capital Gains” (March 2026); Oregon Department of Revenue, “Personal Income Tax”; Min-
nesota Department of Revenue, “Net Investment Income Tax” (December 2025); Massachusetts
Department of Revenue, “Tax Rates” (December 2025). Minnesota adds 1 percent on net invest-
ment income above $1 million. Massachusetts taxes most long-term gains at its 5 percent rate and
short-term gains at 8.5 percent, plus a 4 percent surtax on very high incomes.
78 DOTAX, Individual Income Tax Statistics, Tax Year 2022, Table 21 and text, p. 39. The 74 percent
is $2,210.3 million of $2,995 million, our calculation. DOTAX’s remark on average rates refers to
residents and nonresidents with $400,000 or more.
79 HRS §235-51(f).
80 Marples (2020), summary and p. 3. Carried interest is a share of an investment fund’s profits paid

of 11%.”81 For a single filer in TY2027 with $700,000 of taxable income, the $200,000 above
$500,000 is taxed at 13 percent ($26,000) if it is wages, and at 7.25 percent ($14,500) if it
is a long-term gain (total tax about $70,400 against $58,900, or 10.1 against 8.4 percent of
taxable income).82
Because gains are taxed only when they are realized, people can choose when to pay. A
prospective capital-gains tax increase can bring asset sales forward and raise revenue before
it takes effect.83 That is important for a tax authority trying to forecast revenue, because it
also means revenue in the year after the reform might be substantially lower than expected,
and not just because the higher rate discourages people from having capital gains.
People moved forward the moments when they were going to sell: a person can just sell an
asset a year early, take the tax hit at the old rate, and even buy the same asset again. There is
also a lock-in effect: people respond to a higher rate by delaying the sale of assets they would
otherwise sell. The short-run response in timing is much larger than the lasting one (Burman
and Randolph 1994),84 and the lasting response is smaller but real. Six to ten years after a
state changes its tax rate, realized gains fall by about 0.4 percent for each 1 percent increase
in the tax rate among people who stay, and by about 1 percent once people who move to
other states are counted (Agersnap and Zidar 2021).85 Our estimates use 0.4 and leave out
moves, which the final report will add. These studies differ in how much of the response they
attribute to a change in the timing of sales, and how much to a change in the amount sold
over longer periods.86
Inflation, the incentive to hold on to assets, and corporate income tax already paid all provide
reasons to tax gains at a lower rate. Arguments against include who benefits (three quarters
of residents’ gains eligible for Hawaiʻi’s cap went to residents with Hawaiʻi AGI of $400,000
