Gains on Hawaiʻi real estate are different from gains on stocks. Nonresidents owe Hawaiʻi
income tax on them, and the buyer holds back 7.25 percent of the sale price toward that
tax.88 Nonresident and part-year returns reported $1.43 billion of gains eligible for the cap in
TY2022, about a third of all such gains, so a change in the rate on gains reaches nonresident
filers more than the rest of the income tax does. How much of that is Hawaiʻi real estate has
not been counted.
Step-up in basis at death means that when a person dies, their unrealized gains are untaxed.
If someone owned a bunch of shares in a company that is traded publicly, and they had ac-
cumulated a lot of value over the course of their life, and when they die the estate sells some
of those shares, does anyone pay capital gains tax on the value accrued over their life? No,
not at all.89 Chapter 7 takes this up from the estate tax side, including a 2026 bill that would
have taxed unrealized gains at death.
The final report will estimate taxing long-term capital gains as ordinary income paired with
lower top income-tax rates, and exempting long-term capital gains paired with higher top
income-tax rates. We may be able to provide estimates for a higher or lower capital-gains tax
cap in the final report.
2.6 Business income and retirement income
Business profits that pass through to their owners are taxed on the owners’ returns, and chap-
ter 6 takes up most of how to tax them. Pension income and Social Security are mostly un-
taxed.
Business income on owners’ returns
Self-employed people report business profits on their own tax returns, and S corporations
and partnerships pass their profits through to their owners, who pay the individual income
tax on them. In the US much of that income goes to people at the top (section 2.3). Since
TY2023, partnerships and S corporations can instead elect to have the business pay Hawaiʻi
tax itself, now at 9 percent, with a credit for the owners.90 Business income is mixed in with
other forms of income on these returns. Linking pass-through income to owners’ total incomes
would help show who a higher top rate would affect. We leave the main discussion of how to

Pensions and Social Security
Hawaiʻi excludes qualifying public and employer-funded pension income, as well as Social
Security, from Hawaiʻi AGI. In TY2022 residents subtracted $4.74 billion of pension income
and $2.33 billion of Social Security from the income they reported on their federal returns.91
Withdrawals from individual retirement accounts (IRAs) and employer retirement savings
plans known as 401(k) plans are generally taxed when they come from employees’ own con-
tributions made before tax and the earnings on them.92 Most states do not tax Social Security
either, but California, Oregon and Massachusetts generally tax pensions paid for by private
employers.93
Commissions recommended taxing pension income in 1995-97, 2001-03, 2005-07, 2015-17
and 2020-22.94 The most recent one kept Social Security exempt. For contributory pensions,
such as those of the Employees’ Retirement System, whose members had already paid tax on
their contributions, it would have excluded the first $25,000 a year per person for 25 years,
and it estimated that its design would raise $50 million in its first year.95
A retiree with a large exempt employer pension can have low Hawaiʻi AGI despite substantial
income to spend, while a worker receiving the same amount in wages pays tax on most of
it, and the exclusion is worth more to people with larger pensions. But retirees made their
choices about saving and where to retire under the current rules. They are also not tied to
a job here, though Conway and Rork do not find a consistent effect of states’ tax breaks for
older people on where they move.96 For people who stay, taxing pensions could lead some
to put off retiring. Older workers do respond to the reward from working longer: in one US
study, a larger Social Security reward for more years of work made workers aged 52 and older

(IRC §86) still does not apply after Act 35 (2026); HRS §235-54 (exemption).

We do not yet have estimates of the cost and distribution of the pension and Social Security

2.7 Changes to the income tax
Who bears each change, not only who files, decides the gains and losses to Hawaiʻi residents
and the totals counting everyone, including visitors and nonresidents (chapter 1). Which of
these to lead with is a policy choice that we do not make.
Take the tax cuts at the top. Say the state is giving those people $10,000. Actually, we expect
that they are going to earn more also, so it does not cost $10,000. It costs something less than
that, which chapter 1 calls budget payback, and we make those sorts of statements throughout
the income distribution. Whatever the state’s equity preferences, it still has to deal with the
constraints of how people’s behavior changes when tax policy changes. If the state tells us its
objectives, we can tell it how to achieve them, but we cannot tell it what its objectives should
be. The final report will estimate taxing long-term capital gains as ordinary income paired
with lower top income-tax rates, a higher top income-tax rate paired with lower rates at the
bottom or a larger food/excise tax credit, and exempting long-term capital gains paired with
higher top income-tax rates (Chapter 10).

3. General excise tax
Residents bear the cost of the GET through its effects on prices and incomes. If businesses
increase prices, consumers pay some of the tax. If the tax reduces wages or profits, people
who receive this income bear some of the cost. The same is true of income from property: a
business may be willing to pay less in rent because it has to pay the tax. The GET also applies
to sales between businesses, so it can be paid at each step of production, before a good or
service reaches its final user.
The GET provides a large share of tax revenue in Hawaiʻi because of its broad base. The tax
applies to services as well as goods, and to purchases by tourists as well as residents. There
are different reasons for changing different parts of this base. A reduction in taxes on inputs,
the goods and services businesses buy to produce what they sell, can affect the way goods are
produced. An exemption can lower the prices households pay, depending on how businesses
pass it on. A tax credit can depend on income, but only households that claim it get the money.
Knowing the tax rate does not tell us how a reform will affect households. We need to know
what is taxed, how prices and incomes change, and who ends up better off. Housing is one
example. The GET taxes rent, but not the housing that owners provide for themselves (section
3.3). We also need to be clear about what we mean by regressivity. The GET burden of a
household as a share of annual income can be quite different from its share of expenditure.
Both comparisons are useful, but they measure different things. In either case, a reduction in
revenue must be paid for, and we need to include the effects of the policy used to pay for it.
3.1 The GET base
The GET applies to the revenue of a business, including revenue from other businesses. Unlike
an income tax, the GET does not generally allow deductions for the costs of the business.1 A
small profit does not imply a small tax liability.
Most activities are subject to a state tax rate of 4 percent. For manufacturing, producing and
qualifying wholesale transactions, the rate is 0.5 percent. The rate for licensed insurance
producers is 0.15 percent of their commissions. In all four counties, a surcharge of 0.5 percent
also applies to covered activities subject to the 4 percent state tax.2
A business is not necessarily eligible for the wholesale rate on all of its purchases. The lower
rate is available for purchases for resale within the scope of the law, and for services only
1 HRS §237-13, opening and subsections (1)–(3), (6), (9); DOTAX, Tax Facts 37-1, May 2025, p. 1,
questions 1–3. This describes the general tax base, subject to the deductions and exemptions in the
law.
2 HRS §§46-16.8, 237-13 and 237-8.6(a), (d); DOTAX, GET FAQ (2026), and county surcharge guid-
ance. Exceptions apply to particular transactions.
56

===== page 59 =====
Hawaiʻi Tax Review Commission · Draft report 3. General excise tax
under certain conditions. A contribution to the general costs of the business is not sufficient.3
Goods brought into Hawaiʻi are subject to use tax, which complements the GET on business ac-
tivity within the state. Imports for qualifying wholesale, manufacturing and resale are treated
differently from property used for other purposes.4 A comparison that includes the GET but
ignores the use tax may overstate the tax advantage of purchasing goods from outside the
state.
Businesses can charge more than the combined state and county tax rate. At a rate of 4.5
percent, the maximum charge is 4.712 percent, because the charge is itself subject to GET.5
The calculation applies to one sale; it does not account for taxes at earlier stages of production.
GET and use tax provided $4.63 billion, or 40.4 percent of the tax collections reported by
DOTAX.6 An exemption reduces revenue for both governments if the activity is subject to both
taxes. Since January 2026, Act 47, SLH 2024, has exempted qualifying receipts of specified
healthcare providers from Medicare, Medicaid and TRICARE, the military health program.
Only the specified providers and payments are covered.7
Most states tax sales at higher rates than Hawaiʻi, but few of them raise as much revenue
per person from these taxes. With the county surcharge, the average rate in Hawaiʻi was 4.5
percent in July 2026, compared with 7.68 to 9.57 percent in California, Washington, Nevada
and New Mexico (Table 3.1). In FY2024, however, state and local governments in Hawaiʻi
raised $3,670 per person from general sales and gross receipts taxes, more than twice the US
amount. Washington was the only state to raise more, and the median for the 50 states and
DC was $1,631 (Figure 3.1).8

The reason is the base. In FY2024 the base implied by revenue and the tax rate, as calculated

comparison is based on the income of residents.
A comparison of the GET rate with retail sales tax rates in other states is therefore incomplete.
The GET also taxes goods and services bought by businesses, and the tax paid on them can
become part of the cost of the final good or service. The tax in the final price can therefore be
higher than the rate on that sale, as we discuss in section 3.2. Washington and Oregon also

tax the gross receipts of businesses.10
3.2 Business purchases and pyramiding
A tax on business revenue raises two questions for policymakers: how it changes what busi-
nesses do for themselves and what they buy from others, and how much of it is passed on to
final prices. On the first, taxing inputs creates an incentive for a business to do work itself.
Take two firms, one with its own distribution network and the other without. The firm that
buys distribution services from another business pays a price that can include the distributor’s
GET, whether it appears as a separate charge or is built into the price. The firm with its own
network makes no taxable sale to itself, so it avoids that tax, even if the other business could
do the work at lower cost. A restaurant faces the same choice when it decides whether to
employ people to do work or buy the same service from a business. The structure therefore
favors businesses that can vertically integrate.
Figure 3.2 gives a simple example with three firms in a chain of production, each selling to
the next, for $100, $200 and $400. At a hypothetical tax rate of 2 percent on each sale, total
tax liability is $14. By contrast, if one firm does all the work and only its final sale of $400 is
subject to tax, the liability falls to $8.
10 Washington, Chapter 240, Laws of 2025, §109: service and other activities rates of 1.5 to 2.1 per-
cent from October 1, 2025, depending on receipts in the previous year (Washington Department of
Revenue, Service and Other Activities Rate Changes). These are base rates, before any surcharges
that apply, including the surcharge of 0.5 percent on Washington taxable gross income above
$250 million a year, subject to exemptions, from January 1, 2026 through December 31, 2029
(RCW §82.04.288; Washington Department of Revenue, High-Grossing Business Surcharge). ORS
§§317A.116 (imposition), 317A.125 (rate) and 317A.119 (subtraction), 2025 edition: $250 plus
0.57 percent of taxable Oregon commercial activity over $1 million, after subtracting 35 percent of
the greater of qualifying labor costs or cost inputs (Oregon Department of Revenue, Corporate Activ-
ity Tax). Nevada taxes gross revenue over $4 million at rates of 0.051 to 0.331 percent, depending
on the industry, and Ohio taxes gross receipts over $6 million at 0.26 percent (Nevada Department
of Taxation, Commerce Tax; Ohio Department of Taxation, CAT 2023-01). Both also have retail sales
taxes (Tax Foundation 2026a). A low rate of tax on gross receipts does not imply a low rate of tax
on profits.
60

===== page 63 =====
Hawaiʻi Tax Review Commission · Draft report 3. General excise tax
Figure 3.2. Separately taxed production stages can increase total liability
Note: Illustrative. Receipts are held fixed. The hypothetical tax is 2 percent of each taxable receipt; it is not Hawaiʻi’s
rate schedule. The integrated case assumes internal stages involve no taxable sale. Source: UHERO illustration,
adapted from Colby (2020), p. 23.
This cascading effect is known as tax pyramiding. Because the tax is collected at each transac-
tion, the total tax paid in producing a good can exceed the tax implied by the rate on the final
sale. Nor does the tax reach final prices evenly. Two goods sold for the same price may carry
different amounts of tax, depending on the taxes paid on their inputs along the way. A lower
tax rate on goods bought by businesses reduces the difference, but some difference remains.
If businesses change their decisions in response to the tax, the tax itself shapes local supply
chains in part. Tax collections alone cannot show how much.
There is some evidence of this. Washington used to tax cannabis at each stage (the farm,
the processor and the store), and in 2015 replaced these taxes with a single tax at the store.
Relative to the trend, it then became less common for one firm to both grow and process
cannabis. In China, a new law let manufacturing firms claim a credit for the tax on services
bought from other firms. Those that used more of these services started to outsource more.11
When inputs were taxed less, firms in both places started to buy more from other firms.
Production done outside the state has a similar advantage. An input made in Hawaiʻi in several
steps can carry GET from each of them, while the same input made elsewhere pays Hawaiʻi
11 Hansen, Miller and Weber (2021), pp. 2–3 (PDF pp. 4–5), describe the Washington reform and use
an interrupted time series. Xing, Bilicka, Hou and Raei (2024), pp. 1–2 and 22–23 (PDF pp. 3–4
and 24–25), §6.1 and Table 5 (p. 40, PDF p. 42), report the evidence from China. The comparison
is between firms in manufacturing with different connections to the services affected by the reform.
We discuss the direction of these responses without applying their estimated sizes to Hawaiʻi.

===== page 64 =====
Hawaiʻi Tax Review Commission · Draft report 3. General excise tax
use tax at most once, when it is brought in.12 The use tax narrows the tax advantage of buying
from outside the state (section 3.1), but it does not reach the earlier stages of production, so
the more of those stages that move out of state, the less pyramiding there is.
These incentives would disappear under a tax that applies only to final purchases, as long as
the distinction between inputs and final use can be enforced. A value-added tax (VAT), used
in 37 of the 38 OECD countries, reaches the same result another way (OECD 2024, p. 16).
Like the GET, it is charged on sales at every stage, but each business subtracts the tax it paid
on its own purchases, so in effect it is taxed only on the value it adds. The tax on inputs does
not pile up along the chain, so there is no pyramiding. The reform in China described above
replaced a tax on the gross receipts of service businesses, much like the GET, with a value-
added tax. Both approaches require rules about which purchases are eligible, documentation
and refunds, and a policy to replace lost revenue.
Hawaiʻi already provides some relief from pyramiding in several ways: a lower rate on sales for
resale (the wholesale rate), exemptions, and the capital-goods excise tax credit. Each lowers
the tax on business purchases and moves the GET part of the way toward a value-added tax.13
The credit is a refundable income tax credit equal to 4 percent of the qualifying cost of eligible
equipment and other physical business property used in the state, apart from real estate, that
tax rules treat as depreciable, allowing deductions for its cost over time. Businesses claimed
$34.6 million of it on 2023 tax returns.14 The credit ends for taxable years that begin after
December 31, 2027, under Act 24, SLH 2026.15
DOTAX estimated that the GET, after the capital-goods credit, amounted to 4.72 percent of
final taxable consumption statewide. That is 18 percent more than the 4 percent statutory
rate. This does not mean that 18 percent of the final price is tax. DOTAX estimated higher
rates for food processing (6.28 percent) and agriculture (5.79 percent) than for retail trade
(4.32 percent).16 Pyramiding varies considerably across industries.
We do not yet have estimates of GET pyramiding; the final report will provide them.

them, 0.5 percent when a retailer imports them for resale, and nothing when a licensed wholesaler
imports them for resale or a manufacturer makes them into a product sold at wholesale. Imported
services follow similar rules under HRS §238-2.3.

3.3 Who bears the GET
A household with low annual income may pay a larger share of its income in GET than a
household with higher income. This can be true even if they pay the same share of expenditure.
To interpret this result, we need to distinguish low income in a particular year from a persistent
lack of resources. These differences do not make it any less important that some households
have difficulty paying their current bills.
We need to measure the household’s tax burden before comparing it with income or expendi-
ture. The tax reported on a bill may be only part of that burden. Input taxes can be reflected
in higher prices, even when they do not appear as a separate charge. We must account for
these possibilities without counting the same tax twice, as both a cost to consumers and a loss
of income to business owners.
For example, suppose that three households each spend $40,000 this year, including $1,600
in consumption tax. Their annual cash incomes before tax are $20,000, $40,000 and $80,000.
The tax is 8 percent, 4 percent and 2 percent of income, respectively, but 4 percent of expen-
diture for each household (Figure 3.3). The household with the lowest income must finance
the difference between income and expenditure, while the household with the highest income
can save.
Figure 3.3. The same tax and spending can produce different annual-income burdens
Note: Illustrative households, not Hawaiʻi estimates. Each spends $40,000, including an assumed $1,600 tax bur-
den, which is 4 percent of spending and not a statutory GET rate. Other taxes and transfers are omitted. Source:
UHERO arithmetic.
A household that saves pays the tax when it spends the money, not when it earns it. A com-
parison using one year of income can therefore make the tax look more regressive. Poterba
compares the burden of federal excise taxes as a share of income and as a share of expendi-
ture. If households base their spending on the resources they have over time, spending tells
63

===== page 66 =====
Hawaiʻi Tax Review Commission · Draft report 3. General excise tax
us more than income in one year.17 A retired household living on its savings, for example, has
low income, and it will not earn more later.
Spending is still only a proxy for lifetime resources. Savings may be spent outside Hawaiʻi, or
never spent by the person who saved them. Some purchases are lumpy: a household can buy
a car in one year and use it for years after. Data covering more than one year help with this.
Income in one year matters too. A household whose business income falls for a year can draw
on savings, while one with the same income and no savings may have to cut back.
The GET treats renters and owners differently. People who rent their homes pay a business
for the use of the property, and the business pays GET on the rent. People who own their
homes receive housing services without making an equivalent payment, so they pay no GET
on those services, although construction and other inputs may still be taxed. This favors owner
occupation in the tax code.18 What it means for the distribution of the tax burden depends
on who bears the GET on rent. If renters bear it, exempting owner-occupied housing tends
to favor higher-income households, who are more likely to own. If landlords bear it through
lower rental income, we need to know the incomes of those owners and whether they live in
Hawaiʻi.
Whether renters or landlords bear the GET on rent depends on which side can adjust more
easily. DOTAX assumed that property owners bear it.19 Land-use regulation in Hawaiʻi ranks
as the heaviest of any state, which could make housing supply slow to respond to prices.20
When landlords have little scope to rent out fewer units after a tax increase, much of the tax
can come out of what they receive. Over a longer period, less building and maintenance can
push more of it onto renters. Evidence on property taxes fits this pattern. In Germany, about a
third of an increase was passed on to rents at first and about four-fifths after three years, but
hardly any where housing supply was most restricted.21 For now, this is an overview of the
literature. The final report will consider appropriate assumptions about who bears the GET
on rent in Hawaiʻi and explain them in more detail.
Not all GET revenue represents a burden on residents. Past estimates suggest that nonresidents

pay around 30 percent of the GET.2 2 Nonresidents include more than just visitors. To measure
gains and losses to Hawaiʻi residents, we need to know how the residents’ share is spread
across households. If we count everyone, including visitors and other nonresidents, their losses
count too. Visitor spending may also affect the wages and profits residents receive (chapter
4).
The amount of GET a household pays also needs to be distinguished from its burden after cred-
its. What matters for a household’s resources is the food/excise tax credit it actually receives,
not the one it is eligible for.
We do not yet have estimates of household GET burdens after refundable credits, relative to
income and spending. The final report will include them.
3.4 Exemptions and refundable credits
A refundable tax credit can provide assistance based on income and household size, even when
a household has no income tax liability. An exemption provides benefits to those who make
the purchases it covers. A credit can therefore provide relief to households with little or no
taxable income while leaving the purchases of other households subject to tax. The benefits
depend on what households actually receive. To evaluate a credit, we need to account for
eligibility, whether households claim it, and when it is paid.
The grocery exemption illustrates the difference. We estimate that exempting groceries would
reduce state and county revenue by $267.4 million before allowing for additional spending
out of the savings. Assuming stores reduce prices by the full amount of the tax removed,
residents would save $214.1 million on groceries and visitors $53.3 million (Figure 3.4).23
Of the $267.4 million, $29.7 million is county revenue and $237.7 million is state revenue.
When residents spend their savings, the state recovers about $4.7 million in GET, leaving the
$233.0 million state revenue loss reported in Figure 10.1. The visitor savings likewise include
about $47.3 million of state GET and $5.9 million of county surcharge. These amounts are
rounded separately. The household savings in Figure 10.1 include both taxes and subtract the
GET paid on the additional spending.

income excluded from AGI, but leaves out benefits paid in kind, such as SNAP and WIC food
assistance. It does not measure a household’s accumulated savings or future earnings, so it
describes only part of the resources available over its lifetime.
Households with higher cash incomes receive more dollars from the exemption on average
because they spend more on groceries subject to GET. Before GET on additional spending,
the top 10 percent receive $748 per year, compared with $195 for those between the 10th
and 20th percentiles. Relative to cash income, however, the benefit is generally greater for
households with lower incomes.27 Figure 10.1 shows these household effects after GET on
additional spending. A larger dollar benefit need not represent a larger share of income. As
in the overall GET analysis, we should distinguish a comparison based on income from one
based on expenditure.
For the same total amount of relief to households, a credit can provide more to residents
with low incomes and less or nothing to those with high incomes, while keeping purchases
by visitors subject to tax. Even if it matched the average benefit of an exemption at each
income level, individual households would receive different benefits. Households with the
same income may differ in size, grocery expenditure and use of exempt benefits. We need to
consider these differences before concluding that residents have been compensated.
Some households do not claim credits for which they are eligible. The UHERO–DOTAX study
of TY2021 estimated that food/excise tax credits worth about $1.3 million were not claimed
for roughly 17,800 people on tax returns, including filers, spouses and dependents.28 People
who did not file a return were also an important source of unclaimed credits. The results show
that being eligible is not the same as receiving the credit. Nor do they tell us what take-up of
a new credit would be today.
Under the expanded schedule, the food/excise credit is worth up to $220 per eligible person
and falls as income increases. Single filers are no longer eligible at federal adjusted gross
income of $40,000; for other filers, the threshold is $60,000. The earlier schedule is due to
return after 2027, but the credit does not end.29 The year of the reform therefore matters
for the comparison. So does the difference between households that are eligible and those
assumed to receive the credit.
Even if a household receives a tax credit every year, it needs to pay for food throughout the
year. The state could pay more frequently, but it would need to know who is eligible and be
able to pay them, accounting for changes in income and household circumstances. A simpler
tax form is not enough. Hawaiʻi’s TY2025 N-11SF, for example, is available only to specified

Removing the GET on rent does not necessarily reduce the amount renters pay by the amount
of tax removed. If the burden falls partly on the income of property owners, an exemption
may increase that income instead. A credit for renters provides relief to them, if they are
eligible and claim it. The exemption can also affect the supply of rental housing. To compare
the policies, we need to consider both who bears the tax in the short run and the response of
housing supply over time.
If the full exemption is paid for by residents, they must also replace the tax revenue from
visitors. Other ways of paying for it have different effects. The final report will estimate the
grocery GET exemption paired with higher income-tax rates or with a reduction or elimination
of the food/excise tax credit.
We have not yet compared grocery and long-term rent exemptions with refundable credits
that cost the same. We expect to make that comparison in the final report.
3.5 Different rates on final purchases
The presence of tourists provides a reason for taxing some goods at higher rates than others.
However, the share of demand accounted for by tourists does not tell us which goods should
face higher taxes. Tourists can substitute spending at home for some of their purchases in
Hawaiʻi, or choose not to buy a particular good at all. Purchases outside the state do not
generate GET revenue. Thus, an increase in the tax rate on goods that tourists can easily buy
elsewhere may reduce demand more than a tax on other goods.
Moore (2025) develops a framework for this problem in which changes in the income tax
can compensate residents. Lower taxes should be imposed on goods that tourists can more
easily substitute with spending outside the destination. The response of residents to changes in
relative prices affects the size of the differences in rates. Other things equal, goods consumed
disproportionately by tourists who are more likely to cancel a visit as its cost increases should
also face lower taxes.31
These results are relevant to proposals for higher taxes on luxury goods. Goods that appeal to
wealthy tourists may also be goods that they can easily buy in other places. By contrast, some
services can only be consumed in Hawaiʻi. Tourists who visit may have few alternatives to
these services, but they can still choose not to visit if the trip becomes too expensive. Chapter
4 considers these responses and their effects on residents’ incomes. The fact that a good is
a luxury, or that tourists account for a large share of demand, does not tell us how much
demand will change.
A proposal must also specify which goods are taxed and how residents would be compensated.
Businesses need to be able to distinguish goods subject to the higher rate from other products
when reporting sales, while consumers may respond by purchasing other goods. These re-
30 DOTAX, N-11SF and instructions, revision 2025, p. 1 of each. Qualifying single or joint filers can
use the form if they claim only the food/excise credit. This does not make payment automatic for
all eligible households.
31 Moore (2025), abstract (p. 1); section 2.2 (pp. 10–11) for the rule on goods tourists can buy else-
where, and section 2.3 (pp. 12–15) for visitors who stay away when prices rise.

sponses matter for both tax revenue and the effects on residents.32 Estimating revenue would
require a definition of the tax base and evidence on these responses.
3.6 Changes to the GET and their offsets
The effects of a GET reduction depend on the policy used to replace its revenue. Consider a
reduction in taxes on inputs financed by a higher tax on final sales. Some products would
benefit from the removal of taxes at earlier stages of production, while others would face
higher taxes. The effects on prices, earnings and production also matter for the distribution
of benefits. Payments under the existing tax system are not enough to identify the effects of
the combined reform.
The same is true of an exemption paid for through income taxes. Some households benefit
from the exemption; some pay more income tax. Even if the average at each income level
stays the same, individual households may be worse off. At a given income, spending can
differ, and some households may claim credits that others do not. The comparison between
policies needs to account for these differences.
The comparison depends on when the reform is implemented. The capital-goods credit ends
for tax years that begin after 2027, and the food/excise credit returns to its earlier schedule.33
We also need to consider the effects on state and county governments separately. Replacing
the revenue lost by the state does not mean that counties will receive as much as before. The
final report will estimate two of the pairs in Chapter 10: a grocery exemption paid for by
higher income-tax rates, and a higher GET rate paid back through lower income-tax rates.

===== page 75 =====
4. Visitors and tourism
DOTAX describes Hawaiʻi’s transient accommodations tax (TAT) as a tax borne largely by
nonresident visitors.1 PFM estimated that visitors paid about one-fifth of the general excise
tax (GET).2
Hawaiʻi can’t levy an income tax on the tourists, whose income was earned elsewhere, so
taxes on what they buy while they are here are one of the main ways the state can tax them.
For many residents of Hawaiʻi, taxing the tourists as much as possible is an attractive idea.
But that idea is predicated on our being able to make residents whole for what they pay on
the same purchases, on the tax not costing residents their jobs or profits, and on the rate not
being so high that it drives away enough visitor spending to lower revenue.
We know more about which goods to tax more heavily than about how high to set the rates.
Moore (2025) develops a framework for a destination that can compensate residents for their
share of a visitor tax. In it, the rates that raise the most from visitors are highest on purchases
that must be made here, such as the hotel room and the rental car.3
Section 4.2 explains what that compensation involves. Compensating residents for higher
prices is fairly easy. Compensating them for effects on jobs and wages is much harder. A cal-
culation made before Hawaiʻi’s hotel room tax of 1987 and a study of the tax afterward both
suggest that most of the tax fell on visitors, at a combined rate about half of today’s.4 Section
4.3 considers an alternative to the percentage TAT, a charge for each night.
4.1 The taxes on a visitor’s trip
Visitors pay three kinds of tax on their stay, and residents pay them too. The state TAT is 11
percent of the room charge. Act 96 (2025) raised it from 10.25 percent on January 1, 2026,
and under current law it falls to 10 percent after 2030.5 The act also extended the TAT to

3 Moore (2025), pp.1, 10-11. The result assumes that residents can be fully compensated for what a

same 11 percent (HRS §237D-2(c), (e)).

state TAT raised $816.2 million in FY2025, which makes it the third-largest state tax, after the
GET and the individual income tax.8 The county TATs raised about $245 million.9
Visitors also pay the GET on what they buy, at the same rates as residents, 4 percent for the
state and 0.5 percent for the county surcharge. A hotel may add up to 4.712 percent of the
room charge for the GET, slightly more than 4.5 percent because the GET is also due on the
GET the guest pays.10 The GET and use tax, with the county surcharges, raised $5.20 billion
in FY2025 (Table 1.1). Car rental companies pay a surcharge of $7.50 a day in 2026,11 and
the surcharge on rental, tour and shared vehicles raised $118.2 million in FY2025.12
The hotel operator is required to pay the TAT and the GET, and it legally owes both whether or
not they appear on the bill.13 One question we need to ask, then, is to what extent the burden
of these taxes is paid by the guest and to what extent it is paid by the hotels once room prices
and wages adjust, and section 4.2 takes up that question.
Although it is commonly called the hotel room tax, the TAT applies to any room, house or
condominium rented for less than 180 consecutive days. The state taxes vacation rentals as
well as hotel stays, including any resort fees the guest is required to pay. People staying in
timeshares, even if they own them, pay tax on a statutory “fair market rental value,” calculated
from half the owner’s gross daily maintenance fees.14

Hawaiʻi Tax Review Commission · Draft report 4. Visitors and tourism
its Special Enforcement Section responsible for them. In FY2022 the law gave the section the
authority to examine any sector, and it now cites operators whose ads do not show a tax
registration number.15
Some TAT receipts are earmarked by law for particular uses.16 But the TAT is not a user
charge: it is not a payment for a taxpayer’s use of a particular public service. Visitors’
payment of the tax does not by itself establish a claim for tourism-related spending on the
proceeds. The law says where earmarked revenue goes, but does not say which spending is
best for residents. We look at tourism-related spending like any other use of public funds, at
its benefits, costs and effects on public budgets.
Visitors spent $21.91 billion in Hawaiʻi in 2025. Of this, $10.03 billion, or 46 percent, was
spent on lodging, the largest category. Spending on food and drink was $4.72 billion, on shop-
ping $2.15 billion and on rental cars $1.48 billion.17 The lodging category includes somewhat
more than the TAT base, but the tax on hotel rooms still covers close to half of total visitor
spending, and it can be collected in one place for each stay.
The GET applies to the remaining purchases, at the same rate that residents pay. Nonresidents
pay a much smaller share of the GET than of the TAT (see the start of this chapter), and the
visitor share of the GET rises and falls with the number of visitor days.18 Chapter 3 covers the
GET itself.
We couldn’t find a ranking of lodging taxes across states that is up to date,19 so we compare
the taxes on a $400 night in a Hawaiʻi hotel with the taxes in three places on the mainland
under the rules in force on October 1, 2026 (Table 4.1). Hawaiʻi’s taxes on that night come to
$74.85, which is $24.85 more than in Orlando, $21.33 more than on the Las Vegas Strip and
$10.85 more than in Portland, including Portland’s separate 3 percent tourism assessment.
That is what a visitor would see on the bill, which doesn’t tell us how much a higher rate
would discourage visitors from coming here or to what extent the burden of the tax is paid by
the hotels if they reduce room rates when the tax goes up (section 4.2).20 The table includes
the GET in Hawaiʻi and the sales taxes in Orlando. It leaves out flat charges per night. Georgia

4.2 Visitor responses and compensation for residents
Residents and tourists pay the same GET, and the same TAT if they stay in hotels, so a tax on
tourists also has a cost to residents. Moore (2025) considers a benchmark in which the state
can fully compensate every resident for that cost. Under the model’s assumptions, the state
should then raise as much revenue from visitors as it can.24 Giving residents a tax cut equal
to what they pay in total can still leave some households worse off. Fully compensating each
resident requires more than that. How much of a tax cut the visitor tax can pay for depends
on whether the government is to collect the same revenue overall.
Suppose we increase a broad tax on purchases and reduce the income tax at each income
level enough to keep every resident’s purchasing power the same.25 If all purchases face the
same rate and pre-tax prices stay the same, residents face the same budgets and pay the
same total tax as before. Any additional revenue comes from visitors. This result assumes that
Hawaiʻi policymakers care only about the well-being of residents and give no weight to that
of visitors.26
Wages and prices can also change in the model. Residents then need compensation for any
loss of earnings as well as the higher prices. The income tax can do this in the model because
wages change in the same way across sectors, and residents also pay tax on imports, as they
do under Hawaiʻi’s use tax on goods purchased outside the state.27 The common wage change
matters. A tax cut based only on income cannot generally compensate two workers with the
same income for different losses in earnings.
The model assumes that firms serving tourists earn no profits above their costs. If the tax

well as lodging, so it is not a measure of hotel taxes. The Census figures for local governments are

===== page 80 =====
Hawaiʻi Tax Review Commission · Draft report 4. Visitors and tourism
reduces profits in the tourism sector, full compensation would in principle cover resident
owners as well. Lost profits are a cost to residents only when residents own the hotel. If the
owners live outside Hawaiʻi, they bear that part of the tax, just as visitors bear the part they
pay.28 If the owners are a family in Hilo, it falls on residents.
We would have a hard time compensating everyone in practice. We can predict price changes
more easily than changes in jobs and wages, and can partly compensate people for higher
prices before they pay them. If tourists stay away, though, workers in tourism may lose hours
or earn less. It is much harder to predict those losses and compensate workers before they
happen.
Whether a household files a return, when the credit arrives and how much the household loses
all matter for compensation. A household pays GET throughout the year, including when it
buys groceries, but has to file a return and wait until the year is over to receive a refundable
credit. A tax cut based on income need not cover each household’s losses: some spend more
on taxed purchases than others with the same income, and some, such as a housekeeper at a
Kāʻanapali resort, may also lose hours of work. Section 2.4 discusses whether Hawaiʻi’s credits
reach the people eligible for them.
We therefore need to compare the tax cut each household receives with what it loses through
higher prices or lower earnings. Equal totals for residents’ tax increases and tax cuts do not
establish that everyone has been compensated.
Changes in the income tax are not the only way to compensate residents. The state could also
exempt or subsidize things that only residents buy. Long-term rent is one example. It is subject
to the GET, and it is paid by residents rather than visitors (chapter 3 discusses how much of
the GET on rent falls on renters). Property tax is another. Maui, for example, taxes a principal
home at lower rates than a second home (chapter 5).29 Whether an owner or tenant lives in
a home is fairly easy to establish, whereas residency is hard to check at the point of sale.
The final report will estimate a GET increase paired with proportional reductions in income-
tax rates across the board (Chapter 10). We do not yet have an estimate for a higher TAT
paired with compensation for residents, but the final report will include one.
When setting the overall level of the tax, the main way the state loses revenue is that visitors
choose not to come, or stay fewer nights (Figure 4.1). In chapter 1’s terms, the base shrinks:
if a visitor goes to Cancún instead, or stays home, we lose the tax revenue from everything
they would have bought here. The base can also move. Some tourists will still come but buy
goods at home that they would otherwise have bought at Ala Moana.

profits also matters.

Figure 4.1. Two ways visitor spending leaves Hawaiʻi’s tax base
Note: Illustrative diagram of the mechanism in Moore (2025), sections 2.3–2.4, pp.7, 12–15. It carries no magni-

The possibility of buying goods outside Hawaiʻi also matters for how rates should differ across
goods. The model starts from a benchmark in which, without tourists, the same tax rate on all
goods is best for residents. Tourist demand gives a reason to depart from equal rates. If visitors
can easily buy handbags at home but must buy their Hawaiʻi hotel nights here, the model calls
for a higher rate on hotel nights than on handbags.30 This tells us which rate should be higher,
without telling us whether to raise or lower either rate from where it is today. Visitors can still
buy fewer hotel nights when the price goes up.
Residents’ purchases matter for how much the rates should differ. Even with an income tax
cut to compensate for higher prices, residents change what they buy when goods are taxed
at different rates. The state loses revenue as a result, and has more reason to keep rates close
together when residents buy a larger share of the goods or respond more to prices. A larger
resident share therefore reduces the difference between the rates, without changing which
30 Moore (2025), pp.6–7, 10–11. The equal-rate benchmark rests on the model’s assumptions about
resident preferences and its ability to compensate residents through the income tax. The comparison
here is the section 2.2 result; section 2.3 also allows rates to differ when goods are bought by visitors
with different responses in their decision to visit. The paper’s own example is a visitor who buys “a
luxury handbag from a retailer at home” (p.7). It places lower taxes on goods which tourists can
more easily substitute for spending outside the destination, and visitors’ and residents’ responses
to each good’s own price influence only the magnitude of the difference. Moore notes that the rule
“provides no rationale for taxing goods with relatively high tourist demand more heavily” (p.11).

===== page 82 =====
Hawaiʻi Tax Review Commission · Draft report 4. Visitors and tourism
rate should be higher. These results can help explain why many US states tax hotel rooms
more heavily than other goods. Florida and Nevada also impose separate rental-car charges
(section 4.1).31
The Hawaiʻi evidence on who bears the hotel tax is very old, but it points toward visitors
bearing most of it. Before the tax existed, Fujii, Khaled and Mak (1985) calculated from their
own estimates using Hawaiʻi data that about two-thirds of a hotel room tax would be borne
by tourists and the rest by hotel operators, through lower receipts.32
Bonham, Fujii, Im and Mak (1992) studied the TAT that took effect in 1987, when the com-
bined tax on a hotel room, GET included, rose from 4.16 to 9.41 percent. Their results sug-
gested that the tax was almost fully passed on to visitors in higher prices and that demand
for hotel lodging may change very little when its price rises.33 One reason they offered is that
a 5 percent rise in lodging spending raises the total cost of a typical Hawaiʻi vacation, airfare
included, by less than 1.5 percent.
These studies do not tell us much about the tax today. They concern a lower tax rate, before
vacation rentals were offered through online platforms and before hotel rooms were booked
online, and cover only the first three years after the tax took effect.
Over a longer period, hotels can change the number of rooms available, the number of staff
needed to look after their guests, and the amount they spend on keeping rooms clean and well
maintained. These changes can cause workers and suppliers to bear some of the tax. Thus,
the share that tourists paid at a combined rate of 9.4 percent may differ from the share they
pay at 18.7 percent. By contrast, a recent study of hotel taxes in California cities finds little
effect on hotel sales or employment, but that evidence comes from a different market.34
We can’t just look at a chart of tax rates and arrivals (Figure 4.2) and expect it to show how
visitor spending responds to today’s lodging taxes.35 Evidence from other places covers only
the rates they have tried, so a much larger increase would remain uncertain. We do not yet

want to know what would have happened to arrivals in the absence of each increase. Arrivals can go
up after a rate increase if airfares go down, exchange rates change or mainland incomes rise, and
the pandemic mattered too. With only Hawaiʻi’s rate changes it is unclear how to separate these
effects.

4.3 A per-night charge as an alternative design
The TAT is proportional to the room price, so upgrading the room increases the tax. A fixed
charge per night would work differently: for a visitor staying the same number of nights, an
upgrade would leave the tax unchanged. Visitors would then have less reason to save on taxes
by choosing a cheaper room. Both taxes can change total spending through the cost of the
trip and who chooses to visit. Charging a fixed amount for each extra night could also change
how long visitors stay. We do not yet have an estimate of that response for Hawaiʻi.36
A per-night charge also makes sense for two other goals: making sure visitors make some
minimum contribution toward the costs they impose on residents, and managing the number
of visitors. Do we want to reduce the total number of visitors to Hawaiʻi, or the number who
are present on any given day? Arguably, the costs to residents are more closely related to
the latter. A visitor who stays ten nights adds to the crowding on beaches, trails and roads
ten times as long as someone who stays one night, but paying $900 for a room rather than
36 Collins and Stephenson (2018) found that after Georgia began charging $5 per room per night,
hotels in the state rented about 2.7 percent fewer rooms, while the fall in the price hotels received
was “imprecisely estimated.” The study covers only the first six months of the fee, so, like much of
the evidence on hotel taxes, it is suggestive rather than firm.
81

===== page 84 =====
Hawaiʻi Tax Review Commission · Draft report 4. Visitors and tourism
$200 does not add anyone to the crowd on the Diamond Head trail.37 A charge for each guest
tracks visitor numbers more closely than a charge for each room, since several guests can
share a room.38 Still, visitors differ in the costs they impose, so a fee at the crowded beach or
trail would be closer still. Hawaiʻi already charges such fees at Hanauma Bay, Diamond Head,
Hāʻena and Waiʻānapanapa.39
A per-night charge has costs too. Because the tax does not vary with the price of the trip, it
is a larger share of the cost of a budget trip than of a luxury one. If budget visitors are more
likely to cancel when the trip costs more, the charge would discourage more visits for each
dollar of revenue. It would also burden residents staying at hotels, as the TAT does now, so
their share would need to be part of any compensation (section 4.2).40
A per-night charge is more closely tied to the number of visitors present than a percentage
room tax. A percentage tax has an advantage for raising revenue, however, because visitors in
expensive rooms pay more.41 The state could use both taxes, choosing how much to charge
for another night independently of how much to charge for a more expensive room. Visitors’
responses would determine the resulting revenue and change in crowding.
4.4 Comparing visitor tax designs
The largest revenue question in this chapter is how high to set the percentage TAT. We do not
yet know how much room there is to raise it before the revenue lost as visitor spending falls
outweighs the additional tax collected from those who still come. Nor is additional revenue
by itself enough to establish a gain for residents. We have to compare the benefit from the
taxes it replaces with any losses through prices, employment, wages and profits.
The evidence from 1987 describes hotel demand when the combined rate was 9.4 percent
(section 4.2), well below today’s 18.7 percent. It does not tell us whether demand responds

===== page 88 =====
5. Property and real estate
Chapter 1 orders taxes by how much people can change what they do to avoid them. Taxes on
labor income can lead people to work less, or to live and work somewhere else, while taxes
on business income raise the cost of capital and can discourage investment here. The supply
of land is fixed. A tax can reduce its value but cannot make it disappear. When people move
away, on the other hand, the state loses the tax they would have paid on their earnings. If
we want Hawaiʻi to be more competitive while raising the same revenue, moving part of the
burden from income taxes to property taxes is an attractive direction. That does not mean
property taxes are better in every respect.
Property taxes can also make Hawaiʻi less competitive. Honolulu taxes commercial property
at several times the rate on a home (section 5.2), and high taxes on business and industrial
property raise the cost of operating here. High taxes on homes can make Hawaiʻi a less at-
tractive place to live, and in principle some people would leave. Taxes that depend on how
a property is used can also go too far, and the extreme case is an outright ban. UHERO esti-
mated that phasing out transient vacation rentals in Maui County’s apartment districts could
add up to 6,127 units to the long-term housing stock, but could also reduce visitor spending
by about $900 million a year and cost about 1,900 jobs.1 A higher property tax rate on these
rentals is a middle ground between a ban and the status quo. Maui already taxes short-term
rentals at higher rates than long-term rentals. A further increase in the tax would make the
least profitable rentals no longer worth operating. The more profitable rentals would remain
in business and pay county taxes. Unless the tax were very high, fewer homes would become
available than under a ban, but the loss to tourism would be smaller and the county would
collect more revenue. A very high tax on these rentals or on vacant homes could have many
of the costs of a ban.2
Property owners can change what they build, how they maintain it, whether they rent to
tourists or to residents, and when they sell, even when the supply of land is fixed. The supply
of land is an argument for a tax on its value only if what owners pay is independent of these
choices. County property taxes also fall on buildings, with rates that vary by use, and the
state’s conveyance tax falls on sales, so neither one gets the full benefit of that argument.
1 UHERO (2025), An Economic Analysis of the Proposal to Phase Out Transient Vacation Rentals in
Maui County Apartment Districts, report prepared for the Hawaiʻi Community Foundation, March
31, executive summary, report p. 1 (PDF p. 3). The spending and job figures are for the baseline
scenario, in which visitor spending falls by 15 percent. The report also projects that county property
tax revenue could be about $60 million a year lower by 2029 because of changes in tax class and
lower values (2023 dollars; report p. 1 and Table 10, p. 15), and it discusses higher property tax
rates on these rentals as an alternative (report p. 17).
2 Moore (2024), “Why Not Just Tax Them? A Middle Ground on Addressing Short-Term Rentals,”
UHERO Insights, July 29. Maui Resolution 26-69, FD2, p. 1, gives the county’s rates for short-term
and long-term rentals. Moore also notes that some former short-term rentals would become vacation
homes or sit empty under either policy, so a higher tax on those uses may be needed as well.

Hawaiʻi Tax Review Commission · Draft report 5. Property and real estate
5.1 A statewide property tax as an alternative
A new state property tax could replace part of the income tax or GET. It would take a change
in the constitution (section 5.2), and the state would receive the revenue from the new levy to
finance the cut in state taxes. The state must decide what to tax and what to exempt. We need
to know whether to include buildings or only land, and whether to exempt homes occupied
by their owners.
The distribution of gains and losses depends on these choices. A retiree who owns an expensive
home but has little taxable income would pay more property tax with little benefit from an
income tax cut. Renters can benefit from a reduction in GET.
5.2 The county system and the state’s role
A state property tax would require a constitutional amendment. Hawaiʻi’s constitution gives
the counties exclusive authority to tax real property, except in Kalawao, while the state can tax
conveyances and income from real estate.3 The proposal in section 5.1 would add a separate
state levy, with the state keeping its revenue.
Comparing county rates alone tells us little about what a household pays. The county first
assesses the property’s value, subtracts any exemption, and applies the rate for its tax class
to what remains. Both the exemption and the class can depend on how the property is used.
Even after calculating the bill, we need to know the household’s income to assess whether it
can afford it.
Consider a single-family home with an assessed value of $2 million for the land and improve-
ments together. Its owner is 50, lives in the whole property, and qualifies for the full ordinary
home exemption in fiscal year 2027. In Honolulu, the exemption reduces the taxable value to
$1.88 million. Applying the Residential rate of $3.50 per 1,000 dollars gives an annual bill of
$6,580.4
For the same assessed value in Maui, $1.7 million remains after the exemption. The first $1.5
million is taxed at $1.65 per 1,000, and the remaining $200,000 at $1.80. The annual bill
is therefore $2,835. The comparison holds the assessment fixed and allows the full principal-
home exemption in each county. These amounts are not what homeowners pay on average.
The same assessment in the two counties does not mean the homes are the same or have the
same market value.5
For private second homes without an exemption, the rates are Residential A in Honolulu and

the same pattern in Honolulu. In 2025, the tax on a home with the median value was 0.304
percent of its value, the lowest of the 53 cities in the study. The rate on commercial property
was 3.46 times the rate on that home, the fifth highest ratio.9
Hawaiʻi finances its public schools statewide, while the counties levy property taxes. We there-
fore combine state and local governments, which makes the revenue shares more comparable,
although the way schools are financed still affects the mix of taxes. Even so, these figures do
not tell us who bears the tax or how large it is relative to household income, as section 5.4
discusses.
5.3 Land and buildings
When the supply is fixed, a tax on the value of land can affect the price. Buyers decide how
much they are willing to pay in light of the tax payments they expect to make. If they expect
higher taxes, they will be willing to pay less. The loss in value would be borne by the owners
at the time expectations change. Buyers who come later may pay a lower price, so the amount
of tax they pay need not reflect the burden they bear.10
This does not mean that the tax imposes no burden. The advantage is that it can raise revenue
without the same incentives to reduce work or investment. Even if property owners sell, the
value of land is still subject to tax. If the tax does not depend on what the owner does with
the land, doing less does not reduce the tax. The owner still loses the amount of revenue
the government gets. What is missing is the little bit extra, what we call efficiency costs or
deadweight loss.
Buildings are different. The property is the thing that’s being taxed and it can’t move, but
the owner can respond by investing less in maintaining it. In practice, the tax includes the
value of anything you’ve done to fix up the property, and so you can avoid property taxes,
not necessarily by moving, but just by not making the property as nice as it could be. Higher
property taxes can therefore reduce the value of existing buildings and the amount invested
in the future. We would miss that if we treated all real estate as fixed.
Like most property taxes, Hawaiʻi’s county taxes apply to both land and buildings. So they
combine a base that can’t go anywhere with one that can respond. Someone who wants a
home in Hawaiʻi has to build or buy it in Hawaiʻi, while a firm choosing where to put a new
plant has other options. A higher tax on buildings can reduce how much is built and how well
it is kept up.
9 Lincoln Institute of Land Policy and Minnesota Center for Fiscal Excellence (2026), 50-State Property

and Table 6a, pp. 104–105. The authors calculate what the tax would be on a home with the local

each state (two cities in Illinois and New York, and DC). Honolulu is not the whole state (for Kauaʻi,

The price effect depends on expected future taxes. The chapter considers buildings, valuation and
the timing of development. We use these concepts without adopting its UK recommendations or
treating them as estimates of a response in Hawaiʻi.

===== page 94 =====
Hawaiʻi Tax Review Commission · Draft report 5. Property and real estate
A land-value tax has a base consisting only of land. A split-rate system taxes land and buildings
at different rates.11 A higher tax rate for the former and a lower rate for the latter would
change the return to investment. The amount of construction depends on its cost and on
whether it is allowed. A change in taxes will not make it possible to build where additional
housing is prohibited. Even if it is allowed, construction must be worthwhile.
This comparison requires a distinction between the value of land and the value of buildings.
That is a difficult task when the two are sold together. A new building is supposed to be
factored into the new valuation of the property, but if it also raises the assessed value of the
land, the owner pays more tax for building, even under a land tax. A lower tax on land that
has not been developed can also discourage development. So whether the tax affects what
gets built depends on how the assessment is made, as much as on its name.
Renters may also bear part of the burden. Rents may go up when property taxes go up, but
that depends on the other options renters have. The tax does not change the amount of land,
but some homes that might have existed won’t be built, and owners may not look after the
property, so it deteriorates. That takes time, so what renters pay a few years later can differ
from what they pay in the year the tax is introduced.
5.4 Assessments and fairness
Homes are not like stocks. The assessor has to come up with a way of imperfectly measuring
the value of each home. Suppose two homes have the same value and the assessor puts a
higher value on one of them. Then that home is taxed more heavily at the same rate, before
any differences in exemptions or classification. That is an inaccurate assessment, and it is a dif-
ferent problem from deciding which households should get relief, like low-income households
or owners in their principal homes.
One check is to compare the assessed value with the price the home sold for. If that ratio is
higher for cheaper homes, the same tax rate is a higher effective rate on those homes. This is
called assessment regressivity. We would be overestimating the value of lower-value housing.
The ratio is about the value of the property, and household income isn’t in it, so it can’t tell us
what share of income goes to property taxes.
Christopher Berry looked at US residential property sales and found that, within the same
jurisdiction, less expensive homes tend to have a higher ratio of assessed value to sale price.12
That is a reason to look at this in Hawaiʻi. It is not a Hawaiʻi estimate, and it doesn’t tell
us how the burden is spread by income or race here. In a later study, Berry also looked at
sales in Urban Honolulu. Median tax bills there were 0.37 percent of sale price for the lowest-
priced fifth of homes and 0.31 percent for the highest-priced fifth, a comparison that includes
exemptions and classifications as well as assessments.13
11 Mirrlees et al. (2011), p. 379.
12 Berry (2021), abstract and §IV (pp. 9–10). His result is about homes at different prices. There are
also related papers on racial bias in assessments, such as Avenancio-León and Howard (2022), The
Assessment Gap, but we do not use their estimate.
13 Berry (2024), De jure and de facto property tax rates in large US cities, Public Budgeting & Finance
44 (4): 5–27, Table 3, https://doi.org/10.1111/pbaf.12382. The rates are median tax bills divided
by sale prices, by price quintile within the city, for residential sales in 2021. They describe final tax
bills; they do not separate errors in assessment from the effects of exemptions and classification.

===== page 95 =====
Hawaiʻi Tax Review Commission · Draft report 5. Property and real estate
Owners who think their assessment is wrong can appeal it.
Even if the assessed value is right, a household may not be able to pay. Home values go up,
and so property tax payments go up, but a retired owner on a fixed income doesn’t actually
have more money, and the bill doesn’t drop when income drops. We can reduce the tax, or let
it be paid later. One way is to let the owner defer the tax until the home is sold or the owner
dies, with the deferred tax and interest secured by a lien on the home. Oregon does this for
homeowners aged 62 or older with household income of $70,000 or less. The state pays the
county, charges 6 percent interest a year, and generally requires repayment after the owner
dies, sells or moves.14 Texas lets owners aged 65 or older defer the tax on their home, with
interest at 5 percent a year, until they no longer own and live in it.15 British Columbia runs a
similar loan program for owners aged 55 or older.16 In effect, the owner borrows against the
value of the home to pay the tax. If the state charges interest, the owner still pays about as
much, just later, and can stay in the home until it is sold or passes to heirs. Choosing among
these policies is a decision about relief, separate from correcting an error in assessed value.
5.5 Rates by owner and use
When tax rates differ across uses of a property, taxes can affect how that property is used.
This is different from a tax that applies equally to land regardless of its use. For example, an
increase in property taxes on short-term rentals reduces their profitability relative to other
uses. Some property owners may respond by providing long-term rentals instead. Others may
continue to rent to tourists and pay the higher tax. The effects on housing and revenue depend
on the extent of each response.
A decline in short-term rentals need not translate into an equal increase in long-term rentals.
Property owners can also choose to live in a home themselves, use it as a vacation home, keep
it empty, or put it up for sale. In the last case, its use depends on the decision of the buyer.
Counting the properties that disappear from a booking platform would therefore be insuffi-
cient to measure the increase in housing for residents. What matters is how the properties are

Hawaiʻi Tax Review Commission · Draft report 5. Property and real estate
used after the change. A study of Florida counties found slower growth in residential rents
after Airbnb began collecting the county tourist development tax from hosts, although it did
not establish how many vacation rentals became long-term homes.17
In Maui, several dwelling classes depend on actual use and exemptions. Owner-occupied prop-
erty must be the owner’s principal residence, with the home exemption. For the long-term
rental class, the property must have a rental exemption and be rented to the same person for
at least 12 consecutive months. For transient vacation rentals, the stay is less than six con-
secutive months. Homes that do not qualify for the other dwelling classes are classified as
non-owner-occupied.18 These rules make the tax depend on the use of a property, even when
there is no change in ownership.
Is it a resident or a non-resident who owns the home? Is it a vacation home? A home owned
by someone who lives out of state may be rented to people here, and a resident may own
a vacation home that sits empty for most of the year. Where the owner lives doesn’t tell us
whether residents live in the home. Nor does it tell us who pays the tax, which depends on
how prices, rents and investment respond. A change in policy has to account for both the use
of housing and the burden.
In Vancouver, a home counts as vacant if the owner says so, if a city audit finds it vacant,
or if the owner did not file a declaration. The number of homes reported vacant by owners
has fallen. But the count would change with reporting and audits, and with the supply of
and demand for housing, whether or not there was a tax. So we can’t say how much housing
the tax made available to residents, or how much of the decline is a response to the tax.19
A French study found that taxing vacant homes reduced recorded vacancy rates by about 13
percent, mostly among homes that had been vacant for a long time, and increased the share
recorded as primary residences, although it did not track whether each formerly vacant home
became occupied.20
Some homes are empty for reasons a tax cannot change. Many people in Lahaina had their
homes destroyed by the wildfires. Maui temporarily exempts certain unoccupied reentry-zone
properties there from property taxes because infrastructure prevents occupancy, the property
was completely destroyed and remains vacant, or permitted repairs or rebuilding await final

inspection.21 If the owners can’t live in the home, they can’t respond to the tax, and raising it is
a burden without any change in behavior. So we need to look at the exceptions, the evidence
needed to qualify, and how the base is defined.
A change in how housing is used also has effects on tourism. Fewer short-term rentals could
lead to fewer tourists, resulting in less income for residents who work in tourism or sell to
visitors, and reduced revenue for both the county and state. If visitors choose hotels instead,
the effects would be different. The taxes paid by the rentals that continue operating are only
part of the calculation. To estimate the change in housing and in total revenue, we need
evidence from Hawaiʻi on how properties are used and on how visitors respond.
5.6 The conveyance tax
The conveyance tax is collected when real estate is transferred. It therefore creates an in-
centive to delay a sale: the supply of land is unchanged, but there is no transaction to tax.
In FY2025, the state collected $96.0 million from this tax, compared with $97.4 million in
FY2024.22 This is a separate source of revenue from property taxes collected by counties and
from income tax on gains from the sale of real estate.
The conveyance tax applies to the full amount paid for the property, legally called considera-
tion, rather than the seller’s profit. This amount includes applicable liabilities and payments
other than cash. Under the ordinary schedule, the rate is 0.10 percent below $600,000 and
reaches 1 percent at $10 million or more. A higher schedule applies to a single-family res-
idence or condominium if the buyer does not qualify for a county homeowner exemption.
Rates in this schedule run from 0.15 to 1.25 percent.23 The relevant distinction is eligibility
for the exemption, rather than simply whether the buyer is a resident.
A higher rate applies to the full consideration at the threshold. For example, a taxable cash
sale just below $1 million faces the ordinary rate of 0.20 percent, giving a tax of about $2,000.
If the price is $1 million, the rate is 0.30 percent and the tax becomes $3,000. For the higher
schedule, the corresponding tax goes from $2,500 to $4,000. Figure 5.3 illustrates the result:
the increase in liability can exceed the increase in price.

ocumentCenter/View/157109/Ord-5913. Qualifying Lahaina reentry-zone parcels that are not
occupied are exempt from property tax from July 1, 2026 to June 30, 2028, including exemption
from the minimum tax. Figure 5.1 does not include this relief.

Buyers and sellers have an incentive to avoid this jump by agreeing on a price below the
threshold. Sellers may accept a lower price if the reduction in tax exceeds the loss in sale
price. The tax can also prevent transactions. If the gap between what a buyer is willing to pay
and what a seller is willing to accept is smaller than the tax, no price makes a sale worthwhile
for both. The number of sales below the threshold does not capture those that never occur.
Kopczuk and Munroe study the million-dollar mansion-tax threshold in New York and New
Jersey. They find sales clustered just below the threshold and fewer sales just above it than
would otherwise be expected. The extra sales below the threshold do not account for all
the missing sales above it. Bargaining helps explain these responses; their evidence does not
support evasion as the explanation in the setting they study.24
Best and Kleven study a temporary reduction in the tax. During a 16-month period in 2008–
2009, the UK removed a 1 percent tax for a particular range of housing prices. Their estimate
for homes in the affected price range, after accounting for sales clustered around the tax
thresholds, is that the number of transactions increased by about 20 percent. Less than half of
24 Kopczuk and Munroe (2015), pp. 214–215, 242–243, 250–251. The setting and sample are different
in New York and New Jersey. We cannot assume that the findings about bargaining and evasion also
apply in Hawaiʻi.
96

===== page 99 =====
Hawaiʻi Tax Review Commission · Draft report 5. Property and real estate
the increase was offset in the next year.25 This does not establish the effect of a similar change
in Hawaiʻi, particularly outside the recession in which it occurred.
A higher rate could apply only to the part of consideration above a threshold. This would
remove the jump, while leaving a tax on the sale. There would still be an incentive to delay
a sale or not sell at all. Unless rates or some other tax were adjusted, the change would also
affect revenue.
5.7 Design choices and tradeoffs
For each reform, we need evidence on the choices it affects. Comparing land and improve-
ments requires values for each and information about the scope to develop. If the tax depends
on the use of a property, we need evidence on how use changes. For the conveyance tax, the
issues are bargaining, the timing of sales, and the decision to sell at all. These responses can-
not be inferred from the fixed supply of land. They must be considered when evaluating the
effects of a reform.
The choice of tax relief also depends on the problem it is meant to address. If the assessment
is wrong, we can correct it. If a household cannot pay, a change in the timing of payment may
help. Changing rates, exemptions or other taxes can change the distribution of the burden.
These are different choices about who receives relief and who pays for it. In evaluating a
reform, we should identify the problem and account for both the revenue and the losses to
residents, including the effects of changes in behavior.

6. Business taxes
The corporate income tax raises a small share of Hawaiʻi’s revenue, but can affect the income of
people who never pay it. A reduction in investment can affect wages and employment, as well
as the value of land used by businesses. For a business that remains in the state, the burden
can also fall on business owners through a reduction in income, or on consumers through
higher prices. These effects matter for Hawaiʻi because the owners, workers and consumers
need not live in the same place. Whether they live here determines how much of the gain or
loss falls on Hawaiʻi residents.
The corporate income tax may raise much less revenue, on net, than it collects. The tax collects
revenue directly, but it can also shrink the bases of other taxes. If it discourages investment
here, it lowers wages, employment and the value of land, and with them the collections from
the income tax and the GET, which raise far more than the corporate tax does. The corporate
base itself is narrow. Much business income is taxed on the returns of owners instead, credits
remove much of the liability on corporate returns, and a business in several states can change
how much of its income is assigned to Hawaiʻi. Section 6.2 reviews evidence that a lower
rate could raise total revenue, including a model calculation using 2010 Hawaiʻi data and
responses estimated across the US. Who would gain from a cut also matters.
6.1 The corporate income tax
Not all business income in Hawaiʻi is subject to corporate income tax. C corporations pay the
tax on their profits. That describes who pays in a legal sense, the statutory incidence of the
tax. It is not the same as who bears the cost. A corporation can pass the tax on to its workers
through lower wages, to landowners through lower rents, or to its customers through higher
prices, and section 6.2 shows that much of the burden may fall on them. For partnerships
and S corporations, income is generally taxed as part of the income of their owners. Hawaiʻi
also allows these businesses to pay at the business level and provides a credit to the owners.1
Thus, a change in the corporate tax rate has different effects on different types of businesses.
Changes in individual income taxes can affect businesses as well as workers, because they
affect the tax paid on income from businesses owned in these other forms.
The ordinary corporate tax rates are 4.4 percent on taxable income up to $25,000, 5.4 percent
on the next $75,000, and 6.4 percent above $100,000. They apply to profits, not to the amount
of sales. A business can have high revenue but pay little tax if it also has high costs. Net
capital gains are subject to an alternative calculation, and banks and certain other financial
1 HRS §235-51.5(a), (b) and (e); DOTAX, “Pass-Through Entity Taxation”; see section 6.5.
100

institutions pay a separate tax on their income, called the franchise tax.2 By contrast, the GET
applies to receipts. It can therefore impose a tax on a business even if there is no taxable profit.
Chapter 3 considers its effect on production.
DOTAX reports corporate collections of $400.4 million after refunds, about 3.5 percent of its
reported total for tax revenue.3
In Figure 6.1, net collections fall by 17.3 percent from FY2024 to FY2025, while gross pay-
ments increase by 5.4 percent. The difference is due to refunds, which more than doubled.
Refunds adjust the amounts previously paid to reflect the tax liability after credits. Conse-
quently, the decline in revenue is not sufficient to establish whether there was a reduction
in investment or in the profits reported by businesses. That would require information from
their returns, including the years to which the payments apply. Payments and refunds in a
given year need not reflect only that year’s business activity.

Hawaiʻi Tax Review Commission · Draft report 6. Business taxes
We can also compare these rates and collections with those in other states. Table 6.1 shows
Hawaiʻi with California, Oregon, Alaska and New Mexico, together with national figures. The
top rate of 6.4 percent in Hawaiʻi is close to the median of 6.5 percent for the 44 states with
a general corporate income tax. Figure 6.2 shows the top rate in all 50 states and DC. If we
count the six states without the tax as the lowest, Hawaiʻi is 25th of 51, and the median is
6.0 percent. The tax raises less here than in the US as a whole. State corporate collections in
Hawaiʻi were $287 per resident, compared with $480 in the 50 states. They were 3.7 percent
of state tax revenue, compared with 10.7 percent.4 Collections per resident do not measure
the burden on residents. Part of the burden can fall on visitors and on owners in other states,
and the amount collected in a given year also depends on refunds.
An apportionment formula divides a multistate business’s income among states for taxation.
Using sales alone means that the share of income taxed by a state depends only on the share of
sales assigned there. Combined reporting calculates income across related corporations that
operate as one business.
