Hawaiʻi Tax Review Commission · Draft report 6. Business taxes
6.2 Investment, profits, and who bears the tax
Business taxes can affect the level of investment and employment, and the distribution of
income between owners, workers and consumers. They can also affect how much income
is reported in a state without an equivalent change in production. For example, a business
could expand outside the state instead of increasing its activity here. It could reduce the rate
at which existing operations grow, or change where profits are reported without changing
production at all. All of these responses can reduce the tax base. However, they have different
implications for the incomes of Hawaiʻi residents.
To see how the tax can affect workers, consider a business deciding whether to make an
investment in Hawaiʻi. The business compares the income it would receive after tax with the
cost, taking into account the return it could earn elsewhere. If the tax makes this investment
less attractive, the business may decide to wait or invest somewhere else. If the investment
would have increased workers’ productivity, they may receive lower wages as a result. Thus,
the tax can affect the income of workers even if the business continues its existing operations
in the state.
Some businesses may be able to earn more than is needed to keep them in a given location.
The business may have a valuable location, or customers who are willing to pay more for its
product. In either case, it can earn income above the return needed to remain there. A tax on
this additional income can therefore reduce the income of the owners with a relatively small
effect on that decision. However, the state must be able to tax that income. A business with a
valuable brand could earn additional profits while also having opportunities to report income
elsewhere. An inability to move production does not necessarily imply an inability to move
reported income.
Giroud and Rauh examine the effects of state taxes on business activity in the US. An increase
of one percentage point in the corporate tax rate leads to a reduction of about 0.4 percent in
employment at existing C-corporation establishments. Approximately half of the effect is offset
by a change in activity within the same firms in other states.11 For Hawaiʻi, attracting activity
can be valuable even if there is no increase in the number of jobs in the US as a whole. However,
the firms in this study are large businesses with operations in several states.12 A business
serving local consumers from a single location in Hawaiʻi may have fewer opportunities to
respond in this way.
The effects on workers are not limited to employment. Fuest, Peichl and Siegloch examine
changes in municipal business taxes in Germany. Among firms subject to the tax, they estimate
11 Giroud and Rauh (2019), pp.1265–1266 and 1287–1289, Table 3; for the offset in other states,
p.1265 and p.1290, Table 4. The sample includes firms with at least 100 employees and operations
in at least two states over 1977–2011 (p.1265). A one-percentage-point increase in the rate reduces
employment at existing establishments by approximately 0.4 percent for C corporations and 0.2
percent for pass-throughs. This is different from a 1 percent proportional increase in the tax rate.
12 Sapollnik and Swonder (2025) study a different response: the entry of new employer businesses.
Five years after substantial corporate tax cuts, they find that entry is roughly 10 percent higher,
although the new businesses tend to have lower predicted growth potential. Their sample covers
the contiguous states, so it does not include Hawaiʻi.

that about half of the burden falls on workers in the form of lower wages.13 The estimated
share of the burden is smaller when the calculation includes firms not subject to the tax. Thus,
workers as well as the owners of a business can bear the tax. Applying the estimate to Hawaiʻi
would require assumptions about the businesses and workers in the state and how wages are
determined.
Using US data on wages, rents and business location, Suárez Serrato and Zidar estimate that
roughly 40 percent of the burden of state corporate taxes falls on business owners, with the
rest on workers and landowners.14 Further work by the same authors, using new evidence
on hiring by existing firms and on local productivity, also finds that owners bear a substantial
portion of the burden.15 A tax cut can raise average productivity in two ways. The firms
already there can become more productive, or the state can become more attractive to highly
productive firms. The average can be the same in both cases, but the implications for the
income of workers and landowners are different. To interpret these estimates for Hawaiʻi, we
would need to know both the response to the tax and the types of businesses responsible for
it.
Suárez Serrato and Zidar used a model estimated across US local labor markets to calculate
the corporate tax rate that would raise the most revenue in each state. The Hawaiʻi calcu-
lation combines national estimates of how businesses and workers respond with 2010 data
on the state’s revenue mix, share of US business establishments and apportionment formula.
Counting only corporate revenue, they put the rate for Hawaiʻi at 28.6 percent, far above the
actual 6.4 percent. The answer changes when the calculation includes income and sales taxes
lost when wages fall and workers leave. In those data, Hawaiʻi raised 57 times as much from
these taxes as from the corporate tax, more than three times the ratio in the median state.
Including revenue from these other taxes, the 2010 analysis put the rate that maximizes total
revenue in Hawaiʻi at 0.7 percent, or 1.0 percent after allowing for the weight on sales in the
apportionment formula.16 This is a calculation of how the rate affects total revenue, includ-
ing effects on other taxes. It does not measure how Hawaiʻi responded to changes in its own
corporate tax. The GET and individual income tax now raise about 20 times as much as the
corporate tax, rather than 57 times, which would push the rate that maximizes revenue up,

Hawaiʻi Tax Review Commission · Draft report 6. Business taxes
holding the model’s other inputs fixed. Even so, the calculation gives reason for concern that
Hawaiʻi’s rate is too high and that a cut could raise total revenue.
An increase in workers’ income does not necessarily mean that a corporate tax cut mainly
benefits low-income households. Kennedy and coauthors find that the gains from the federal
corporate tax cut went mainly to owners and highly paid workers. The increase in earnings
within firms was confined to the highest-paid tenth of workers, with especially large gains for
executives. There was no increase for the other nine-tenths. Since the workers who gained
pay individual income tax, part of the corporate revenue loss can be recovered from their
higher earnings. The authors suggest that taxing labor income could have smaller economic
costs than taxing corporate income. This supports considering whether to tax high incomes
directly instead of collecting the same revenue through corporations.17
A corporate tax can reduce what the state collects from other taxes. Lower wages reduce
income-tax and GET revenue, and lower land values reduce property-tax revenue, so the
corporate tax may add much less to total revenue than its own collections suggest. At the
federal level, most evidence finds that the recovery through other taxes pays for only a small
part of a corporate tax cut. Chodorow-Reich, Zidar and Zwick find that the federal corporate
tax cut increased investment, but they estimate only a small recovery of revenue through
individual income taxes. The response to a state tax could be larger, since firms and workers
can choose another state without leaving the country.18 In Hawaiʻi, even a small percentage
change in the income-tax and GET bases can be large relative to corporate tax collections,
because these taxes raise so much more revenue.
In Hawaiʻi, a substantial cut in the corporate tax rate could raise total revenue once its effects
on the bases of other taxes are counted.19 The analysis of 2010 data, considered alongside
the broader literature, gives reason for concern about the cost of Hawaiʻi’s corporate tax and
for further investigation.20
The final report will revisit this calculation to estimate the revenue effects of a corporate rate
cut, including the resulting changes in income-tax and GET revenue.

Hawaiʻi Tax Review Commission · Draft report 6. Business taxes
A lower rate increases the income of the owners of assets already in place. It may also affect
whether a business chooses to make an investment. An increase in deductions for investment
may provide a more direct incentive to do so, subject to the treatment of losses and the rest
of the tax base. Giving more weight to sales in the formula used to assign income to Hawaiʻi
can also affect investment. These options must be compared together with the taxes used to
finance them.
The corporate income tax is not the only thing that discourages business investment in Hawaiʻi.
The costs of doing business here and the rules businesses must follow matter too, and it would
be a mistake to think that changes in business taxes alone could solve the state’s competitive-
ness problems. The wider business climate is beyond the scope of this report. Still, the tax
deserves attention. Capital moves more easily than almost anything else we tax, which is why
business capital is among the most responsive bases in the ordering in Figure 1.4. A tax on it
can send investment elsewhere, and workers and landowners here bear part of the cost.
6.3 Apportionment
Hawaiʻi uses an apportionment formula to determine the share of a business’s income taxed
by the state. Under the general formula, this share depends on property and payroll as well
as sales, even if total profit is fixed. For a business in several states, it is the average of three
shares: property in Hawaiʻi as a proportion of total property, payroll here as a proportion of
total payroll, and sales assigned here as a proportion of total sales.21 If each share is one-tenth,
one-tenth of business income is generally assigned to Hawaiʻi. These shares determine how
much income is assigned to the state; we do not need to estimate the profit earned by each
office.
Consider a business with activity in several states. Suppose it hires one more person in Hawaiʻi
and nothing else changes. Its share of payroll or property in the state goes up, and more of
its income is taxed here, even though the tax rate is the same. In some sense, the three-factor
formula is a tax on the jobs and buildings that the state wants to attract.22 If we only use sales,
that problem goes away. Customers are harder to move than workers or buildings. A firm can
sell to people here from somewhere else, but the people who buy from it stay in Hawaiʻi. This
is the main argument for a formula based only on sales, now used by most states, and we think
it is probably the right formula for Hawaiʻi (Mazerov 2005, p.2; ITEP 2012; Tax Foundation
2026c).
Figure 6.3 illustrates why some firms would gain while others lose. Both firms are hypothetical.
For the first, Hawaiʻi accounts for half of property and payroll, but one-tenth of sales. Under
a formula based only on sales, the income share assigned here falls from about 37 percent to

factors, but do not attempt to describe how the formula differs across industries or the alternative
methods that may be used. A full discussion of the connection a business must have with a state for
the state to tax it, and of special industry rules, is beyond the scope of this chapter.
22 The evidence on jobs is less settled. Goolsbee and Maydew (2000) find that reducing the weight
on payroll in the formula increased manufacturing employment. A replication by Merriman (2015)

Even with the same rates, sales only would raise the tax on some businesses and lower it on
others. It goes up where a business’s share of sales in Hawaiʻi is bigger than its share of property

elsewhere that sell here without many workers or buildings in the state. Businesses based in
Hawaiʻi that sell mostly elsewhere would pay less, and the throwback rule limits the cut on
goods sent to places where the seller is not taxable.23 In DOTAX’s statistics, corporations that
apportion income among states report most of the net income of C corporations in Hawaiʻi.24

lose revenue where the base is dominated by large plants selling outside the state, and it
can gain revenue where the base is dominated by businesses elsewhere selling to the state’s

whose share of sales here exceeds their share of property and payroll, even with the statutory
rate unchanged. A reform could pair it with a lower rate.
6.4 Investment deductions and losses
Tax deductions affect investment through their timing as well as their amount. The cost of
an investment is paid when it is made, but the associated deductions may be spread over
several years. The deductions have a higher value if they are received earlier, because the
tax savings can be used in the meantime. This depends on the return available during that
time and whether there is sufficient taxable income to use them. Changing their timing can
thus reduce the cost of additional investment without reducing the tax on income from all
investments already in place.
Federal rules for deducting investment costs over time generally apply in Hawaiʻi, but there
are important exceptions. For tax years beginning after December 31, 2025, Act 35 of 2026 up-
dated the reference to federal law without adopting bonus depreciation, which allows larger
deductions in the year an investment is made, or the new allowance for certain buildings used
in production. The state retains the section 179 limit of $25,000 for investment eligible for
immediate deductions, reduced when qualifying investment exceeds $200,000.26 Nor does
Act 35 adopt the new federal provision allowing immediate deductions for domestic research
expenses.27 The state and federal taxes can therefore treat the same investment differently,
and a comparison of statutory rates leaves out these differences in the base.
The argument for expensing starts with an investment that just breaks even before tax. Ex-
pected revenue is equal to its cost, after taking into account the time and risk involved. With
expensing, the cost is deducted from taxable income immediately. Suppose that future rev-
enue is taxed at the same rate, and that the deduction is worth the full amount even if the
business makes a loss. The tax then reduces both sides by the same proportion. The invest-
ment still breaks even after tax. Thus, under these assumptions, the tax does not affect the
decision to undertake this investment.28 The result depends on the timing of deductions, the
25 Mazerov (2005), p. 29. He adds that a gain is unlikely in states without a throwback rule. States
that made a sales-only formula mandatory after allowing firms to choose it, such as California in
2012, gained revenue, but that is a different change from the one Hawaiʻi would make.
26 Act 35, SLH2026, §2 and amendments to HRS §235-2.4, printed pp.1, 23–25 (physical PDF pp.2,
24–26); DOTAX Announcement 2026-06, pp.2–3. Sections 168(j), (k), (m) and (n) are inoperative
for Hawaiʻi purposes. HRS §235-2.3(o), as set out in Act 35, SLH2026, p.24, retains the section 179
cap of $25,000 and the $200,000 phaseout threshold, with additional state exceptions. The Act’s
general conformity update applies to taxable years beginning after December 31, 2025.
27 Act 35, SLH2026, printed pp.7 and 24: IRC §174A is inoperative; §174 remains operative in its form
as of December 31, 2024. This describes the law as enacted. It does not cover all transition choices
or the treatment of each type of research expenditure.
28 Rousslang and Chow (2017, pp. 31–32) supported expensing for this reason, and the 2015–2017 Tax
Review Commission recommended it, noting its revenue cost in the short run (Hawaiʻi Tax Review
Commission 2018, §5.1.3, p. 22). The 2005–2007 commission had recommended eliminating the
corporate income tax (Hawaiʻi Tax Review Commission 2007, printed pp. 19–20).

===== page 113 =====
Hawaiʻi Tax Review Commission · Draft report 6. Business taxes
treatment of losses, and the tax rate being the same.29
There are also arguments against expensing. The result is only true for an investment that just
breaks even. Where a business expects to earn more than it needs to, its choice of location
depends on the tax on all of its profits, and expensing leaves that tax in place. If interest is also
deductible, an investment paid for with debt can face a negative tax rate.30 A state deduction
is also not tied to investment in the state. Hawaiʻi starts from business income and uses the
apportionment formula to assign a share to the state. So faster deductions for a machine
bought in another state lower Hawaiʻi tax just as much as for one bought here, holding fixed
the share assigned to Hawaiʻi.31 Finally, the state would lose revenue at first. Businesses would
still write off what they bought before the change one year at a time, and write off new things
up front, so for a while the state would be paying for both. Denying the remaining deductions
on past purchases to avoid this cost would raise the tax on investments already made.
A business also might not have the income to use a deduction right away. Then the deduction
is worth less, and if it never makes any money, it may be worth nothing. In Hawaiʻi, losses after
2017 can generally reduce taxable income in later years, with no time limit, but the deduction
cannot exceed 80 percent of that year’s taxable income. They generally cannot reduce tax on
earlier years, except that farming losses can be applied to the preceding two years.32 The
state takes a share of the profits right away, but it only shares in the losses once the business
is profitable. So a business losing money at first can pay more tax than one that earns the
same amount in a steady way. Expensing has to be considered together with how losses are
treated.
6.5 Pass-through businesses
Businesses can respond to individual income taxes as well as corporate taxes. For partnerships
and S corporations, business income is generally taxed as income of the owners. The rate on
an additional dollar therefore depends on their other income as well as profits. This is why
a change in the individual tax can affect the incentive to undertake investment in a business.
However, not every response to an individual tax change represents an effect on investment.
Owners can also change their work, move to another jurisdiction, or change the amount of
29 This is an illustration of an economic argument, not a statement of Hawaiʻi law. Let the cost of
investment be I, the value of future receipts in today’s dollars V, and the constant tax rate t. With the
full deduction available immediately, the net present value after tax is (1−t)(V−I). An investment
that just breaks even before tax still breaks even at V=I. What matters is that a dollar of costs reduces
the tax by as much as a dollar of revenue increases it. This assumes that the rate used to convert
future receipts into today’s dollars takes into account the risk and that the financing is treated the
same way. A separate deduction for financing would change the calculation, so full expensing need
not give the same result.

Act 27, SLH2018; DOTAX (2025), Form N-30 Instructions, p.2. The instructions distinguish losses
for tax years ending after December 31, 2017 from the limit applying to years beginning after that
date. For a loss after 2017, the general rule limits the deduction to 80 percent of taxable income in
a later year. It cannot be used for earlier years under this rule, but there is no limit on the number of
later years in which it may be used. Farming losses may also be applied to income in the preceding
two years. Other special provisions, including the transition rules, can affect the treatment of a loss.

===== page 114 =====
Hawaiʻi Tax Review Commission · Draft report 6. Business taxes
income that they report.33
Giroud and Rauh find different effects for different types of businesses. Employment at ex-
isting pass-through establishments falls by about 0.2 percent following an increase of one
percentage point in personal income-tax rates. It responds to these rates rather than corpo-
rate tax rates.34 The businesses are large and have operations in several states, providing
opportunities to respond by moving activity. Businesses that depend more heavily on a local
market may have fewer such opportunities. This identifies the tax relevant to each legal form,
but an estimate of the employment effect for Hawaiʻi requires assumptions about the types of
businesses affected.
Studies give different answers on how much of a tax on business owners passes to their work-
ers, and the answer depends on the setting. Risch studies the 2013 increase in the top federal
income tax rate, which raised the tax on the owners of many S corporations and partnerships.
For each dollar of additional tax on owners, their employees’ earnings grew by 11 to 18 cents
less. Nearly all of this effect fell on the highest-paid 30 percent of workers across the affected
firms.35 Most of the burden stayed with the owners, but not all of it. This is the same pattern
as with the 2017 corporate tax cut (section 6.2). Some of the burden of taxes on business in-
come falls on employees, at least at the federal level, and probably in the states.36 Kindsgrab
looks at the same 2013 increase by comparing local economies where top earners had larger
or smaller shares of income. He finds no detectable effect on the wages of other workers, and
Risch’s estimates are small enough to be consistent with that.37 That doesn’t mean changing
Hawaiʻi’s top income tax rates would have a big effect on revenue that way. Many people with
high incomes own businesses, but business owners are mixed in with other taxpayers. A higher
top rate falls on all of them, and only part of the response works through the businesses.
In Hawaiʻi, partnerships and S corporations can choose to pay the elective pass-through entity
tax. This is an annual choice. The rate is 9 percent on the Hawaiʻi taxable income allocated
to eligible owners, including payments to partners that do not depend on the partnership’s
profits, compared with 11 percent in TY2023. A member receives a credit for their share of
33 Goodman, Lim, Sacerdote and Whitten (2024) study the federal deduction for pass-through busi-
ness income introduced in 2018. They find an increase in reported business income eligible for the
deduction, and partnerships paid their owners less in forms that do not qualify. They find little evi-
dence of changes in real economic activity, as measured by investment, employment or wages paid
to non-owners.
34 Giroud and Rauh (2019), p.1287 and Table 3; see note 11.
35 Risch (2024), pp.669–670 and Figure V. Employment did not change. Almost all of the effect was on
the top 30 percent of earners among workers in the exposed firms, and most of the burden stayed
with the owners.
36 Risch (2024); Suárez Serrato and Zidar (2016); Giroud and Rauh (2019).
37 Kindsgrab (2022), abstract, p. 1, and pp. 18–19. Kindsgrab reports a 95 percent confidence bound
of 0.051 for the elasticity of other workers’ wages with respect to the share of an additional dollar
of income that a top-rate taxpayer keeps after tax, and places Risch’s estimates within this bound
(pp.18–19). Smith and coauthors (2019), as cited by Kindsgrab (2022, p.2), report that about 70
percent of top-one-percent earners own and actively manage small or medium-sized businesses that
operate locally or regionally.

Hawaiʻi Tax Review Commission · Draft report 6. Business taxes
the payment.38 So the money the business pays is not just an extra tax on top of what its
owners pay. To see the effect, we have to look at both tax returns, including the credit and
when each payment is made.
The credit is nonrefundable. From TY2024, though, it can be carried forward when it is more
than the member’s tax for the year, which was not possible for TY2023 credits. That matters
for a taxpayer whose credit is greater than the individual income tax they would otherwise
pay. From TY2025, members who claim the credit must also add their share of the entity tax
back into their Hawaiʻi taxable income.39
One reason to choose this option is federal. Under IRS guidance, when the business pays a
qualifying state income tax, the owners have less income passed through to them for federal
purposes.40 Since 2018 there has been a cap on the federal deduction people can take for state
income taxes, but these payments are not subject to it. How much the owners save depends
on federal rules and on the owner. For the state, the election can move some of the tax from
the owners’ returns to the business. The state gets more from the business and less from the
owners, who claim the credit. To find out whether it comes out ahead, we need to compare
the two with what the owners would have paid without the election.
Official TY2023 statistics show 1,421 S corporations paying $146.8 million in elective pass-
through entity tax and 296 partnerships paying $38.6 million.41 Together that is $185.4 mil-
lion, a substantial amount of tax paid through businesses in the first year of the election. It is
not an estimate of how much the state gained, because the members claim credits for these
payments, sometimes in later years, and the rest of their tax can change as well. The total also
does not tell us who the owners are, and we would need to know that to estimate the effects
on different households.
6.6 Real estate investment trusts
The final report will discuss how Hawaiʻi taxes real estate investment trusts (REITs).
38 HRS §235-51.5(a)–(b), as amended; DOTAX, “Pass-Through Entity Taxation,” sections “Eligibility,”

levied on the qualified members’ Hawaiʻi taxable distributive shares and guaranteed payments. The
9 percent rate does not apply to all of the business’s receipts across the country.
39 HRS §235-51.5(e), with the 2024 amendment history (Act 50, SLH2024, §§2 and 4), for the credit

of tax overpayments. Members must add back the tax for years beginning after December 31, 2024.
The business itself can get a refund of estimated payments above the tax due, but that does not

7. Wealth and estates
Couldn’t Hawaiʻi have a wealth tax and use it to pay for things? It’s a popular idea, but Hawaiʻi
already taxes wealth in several ways. The most direct is the estate tax, which the state levies
once on everything a person owns at death, for large estates, and it is the main subject of this
chapter.1 You’ll hear people say the estate tax is a death tax, or double taxation (section 7.1).
Think of a family with a home in Honolulu, some publicly traded stock, and a family business
that has never been sold. The county taxes the home every year, based on its estimate of what
the home is worth. The estate tax is a tax on everything they own when they die, but only
once, and only if the estate, after debts and deductions, is worth more than the exclusion (the
amount the tax leaves alone, $5.49 million for a single person with no earlier taxable gifts).
An annual wealth tax would have to come up with a value for all of it every year. The business
is the hard part.
There’s no tax more easily avoided than a tax on a base that the government doesn’t have the
capacity to measure, or on people who can leave. Basically, Hawaiʻi is already taxing the part
of wealth that’s easy for it to tax. The property tax is a tax on one kind of wealth, land and
buildings, and they pass both tests. The county can put a value on them, and the owner has to
pay the tax where the property is. The GET also reaches the wealth of people who earned their
income somewhere else and moved here, when they spend it here (section 7.2). The estate
tax only has to measure the value once, at one point in time, and Hawaiʻi does it with federal
rules and the federal return form. Collections swing so much from year to year that much of
its revenue seems to come from a few estates, whose owners can move or plan around the
tax.
The estate tax turns on how readily the wealthy move. A published study with estimates for
Hawaiʻi, which models a 2017 tax with a similar exclusion, finds that the income tax lost
when they leave would exceed the estate tax gained if they moved as readily as billionaires
do, and fall short of it if they moved half as readily. An annual tax on all other wealth would
have to pass both tests every year, and the evidence makes it unlikely that such a tax, levied
by Hawaiʻi alone, would raise much revenue (section 7.3).
7.1 The estate tax
Hawaiʻi’s estate tax largely follows federal definitions, with its own exclusion and rates. The
exclusion, the part of an estate that is not taxed, is $5.49 million, the federal amount for a
death on December 31, 2017, and because the law fixes it at that date it does not rise with
1 The 2001-2003 Tax Review Commission treated the estate tax as a question of conformity with
federal law, and no earlier commission recommended raising it (Karacaovali 2021, pp. 9-10). Most
of the evidence on mobility and on wealth taxes discussed in this chapter has appeared since.
118

===== page 121 =====
Hawaiʻi Tax Review Commission · Draft report 7. Wealth and estates
inflation.2 The rates rise from 10 percent on the first $1 million above the exclusion to 20
percent on the part of the estate more than $10 million above the exclusion, a bracket that
has applied to deaths since 2020 (Act 3, SLH 2019).3
The federal exclusion is $15 million in 2026. Like other states, Hawaiʻi relies very heavily on
the way the federal government has calculated the estate in order to levy its own tax. For a
single person with no earlier taxable gifts, as in Table 7.1, a taxable estate between $5.49
million and $15 million owes tax to Hawaiʻi and nothing to the federal government. It still
fills out a federal return, just for Hawaiʻi, and doesn’t send it to the IRS.4
Above $15 million the federal tax changes what the Hawaiʻi tax costs, because the estate can
deduct state estate taxes from its federal taxable estate, so each dollar of Hawaiʻi tax lowers
the federal tax by up to 40 cents (the top federal rate).5 On a taxable estate of $30 million,
the Hawaiʻi tax in Table 7.1 is about $4.3 million, but it adds only about $2.6 million to what
the estate pays in total. There is no federal tax to lower below $15 million, so the estate pays
the whole Hawaiʻi tax.

does not have to file a federal return fills out a computed federal Form 706 for Hawaiʻi. Deductions

The revenue is small in most years and large in a few. In the twelve fiscal years before FY2025,
collections ranged from $12.1 million to $58.1 million, and they could more than double or
fall by more than half from one year to the next (Figure 7.1). In FY2025 they were $405.8
million, including a one-off receipt of $315 million in September 2024.6 Swings like these
suggest that a few estates pay much of the tax.7 This is typical of state estate taxes, where
a single death can bring in more than many ordinary years together. When Bud Walton, a
co-founder of Walmart, died in 1995, Arkansas’s estate tax revenue rose the next year from
$34.8 million to $183.2 million, in 2017 dollars.8 We learn little about what this tax can raise
from a few years when it brings in little, and even less from one year when it brings in a lot.
Assuming FY2025 will repeat itself would give us a very misleading picture. The Council on
Revenues attributed part of the decline it forecast for FY2026 to the unusually high FY2025
base.

1annual/). The FY2025 receipt is described by DOTAX as a one-off. Council on Revenues, report of
January 9, 2026, p. 1, on the FY2026 forecast. Collections are what was received in each fiscal year,

the median is 16 percent.10 These are the amounts in the law. It’s hard to say from them how

credits, deductions and earlier gifts (and above $15 million, on the federal deduction in Table
7.1).
Hawaiʻi also raised more death and gift tax per resident in FY2025 than any other state (about
$283, against $22 for the country as a whole). Most of that was the one-off payment of $315
million, 78 percent of the total that year (Figure 7.1 shows how much collections swing).11

The usual objection to the tax is that it taxes savings that were already taxed. Gale and Slemrod
argue that what matters is the combined tax burden and the cost of collecting it, rather than
the number of times wealth is taxed. The objection also leaves out gains that were never taxed
at all. Savings out of income were taxed when the income was earned, but the gain on an asset
someone holds until they die is generally never taxed as income. This is what’s called step-up
in basis at death, which means that when you die, your unrealized gains are untaxed, and
Hawaiʻi’s income tax follows the same rule.12
Suppose, for example, someone bought stock in a company like Google many years ago, before
it became big. They are rich on paper, but they haven’t sold the stock, and they can finance
their lifestyle by borrowing against it, which is something you can do if you’re rich. When they
die, the estate sells some of the stock to pay down the debt, and there’s generally no income
tax on the gain that built up over their life, only on any gain after the date of death. So for
assets like these, the estate tax is the one tax that reaches that gain, and only for estates above
the exclusion.
Do the rich really move because of the tax? The most direct evidence is a paper by Moretti
12 Gale and Slemrod (2001), “Rhetoric and Economics in the Estate Tax Debate,” Claim 10, pp. 21–22.
26 U.S.C. §1014(a); HRS §235-2.3. The rule can also step the basis down, and there are exceptions,
including income in respect of a decedent (§1014(c)), such as balances in traditional retirement
accounts. Gains that accrue after the heir inherits are taxed when the heir sells. Chapter 2 discusses
capital gains.

===== page 126 =====
Hawaiʻi Tax Review Commission · Draft report 7. Wealth and estates
and Wilson on where the Forbes 400, the richest Americans, choose to live in response to state
estate taxes. Before 2001, a federal credit meant their estate tax was the same wherever they
lived. After the federal tax reform of 2001 phased out the credit, their estate tax was higher
if they lived in a state that had one. The number of Forbes 400 members living in estate-tax
states then fell by about 35 percent relative to other states, and the response was stronger
among older billionaires.13 This is fairly compelling evidence that the richest people move in
response to the tax. Against this, the death of the average billionaire brought in an estimated
$165 million in state estate tax in the years after the death. Moretti and Wilson weigh the two
for each state: the estate tax a state collects when a wealthy resident dies, against the income
tax that residents who move away would have paid over the rest of their lives. In their model
of a 2017 tax on billionaires alone, the cost for Hawaiʻi is 1.12 times the benefit, a net loss
of about $20 million in present value, that is, with future amounts discounted to today. This
calculation rests on one person, the single Forbes billionaire living in Hawaiʻi in 2017.
Of the eleven states with an estate tax and at least one billionaire in 2017, Hawaiʻi was the
only one where the cost was larger than the benefit. The authors attribute this mainly to its
high income tax rate, which makes it more costly to lose a resident’s income tax.14 The authors
also model a broader 2017 tax with an exclusion near $5.5 million, close to Hawaiʻi’s, and
an assumed average rate of 10 percent. Its result depends on how readily the merely wealthy
move. If everyone above that level moves as readily as billionaires, the cost for Hawaiʻi is 1.18
times the benefit, a loss of $472 million in present value. If instead they are half as mobile,
the ratio is 0.52, a gain of $1.45 billion.15
Bakija and Slemrod had looked earlier, before the 2001 reform, at much smaller estates. They
found that some of the wealthy did leave states with high estate taxes, or at least changed
their address on paper. Still, the estate tax the states lost was only about 6 to 14 percent
of what they would bring in by keeping their own estate tax after the federal credit ended.
If you also count the income, sales and property taxes that the people who moved would

have paid, the loss could be as much as a third.16 Other studies find little response among
the elderly as a whole.17 None of these studies tells us whether the merely wealthy move
as readily as billionaires. Moretti and Wilson’s is the more recent analysis, but neither it nor
Bakija and Slemrod’s can fully account for the investment that may or may not leave with
wealthy people who move, such as a firm that moves with its owner.18
For a US citizen or resident who becomes domiciled outside Hawaiʻi, ordinary financial assets
generally fall outside the Hawaiʻi estate tax. Hawaiʻi real estate and tangible property located
here are taxed, with the taxable estate and the exclusion apportioned for nonresidents. Moving
doesn’t get you out of the tax on a house or land you own here. That part of the base remains
even after the owner leaves, though once you’ve moved, you can avoid the tax by selling
before you die. For a resident, selling does not help, since the estate tax reaches the proceeds,
and selling has a cost of its own, because the gain is then taxed as income instead of escaping
income tax at death.
For the purpose of the estate tax, your residence depends on domicile, roughly the permanent
home you plan to return to. Spending time in another place is not enough (chapter 1, section
1.3). The law assumes that someone with property in Hawaiʻi at death was a resident, and
the estate has to prove otherwise.19 Owners can also lower the tax without moving, and there
are a lot of things they can do, from gifts to their kids and trusts to the way a family business
or property is owned and valued.20 There is no gift tax in Hawaiʻi, but adjusted taxable gifts
reduce the exclusion at death. Giving away assets therefore may not reduce the tax, although

it can keep the later increase in their value out of the estate. People give to their children and
set up trusts years before they die. It can take a long time to see the effects in the data, making
this harder to study than moving.
The tax may also change what heirs do. Older workers in the United States who receive an
inheritance tend to retire sooner than they expected, especially if they hadn’t expected the
inheritance.21 A study using federal tax returns found that single people who inherited more
than $150,000 were about four times as likely to leave the labor force as those who inherited
less than $25,000.22 If a smaller inheritance keeps an heir working, some revenue comes back
through the income tax, the budget payback discussed in chapter 1 (section 1.5).
Another concern is that the heirs of closely held businesses might have to sell the business to
pay the tax.23
For the family in our example, this raises questions about both what the business is worth
and how to get the money to pay. Since the business has never been sold, there’s no market
price to use. It has to be valued under federal rules, though only once. Even if it’s worth a
lot, the business may not have enough cash to pay the tax, and selling a minority share of a
small family company may be difficult. For farms, at least, the evidence suggests that cash is a
problem for some estates but not most. In the Congressional Budget Office’s study of federal
returns filed in 2000, at most 138 of the 1,659 farm estates that owed estate tax did not have
enough cash and assets that could readily be sold to pay it.24 Among estates that claimed the
federal deduction for family-owned businesses, at most 164 of the 485 that owed estate tax
lacked enough cash and assets that could readily be sold to pay it.
Hawaiʻi follows the federal rules on valuation and how long people have to pay. If a closely
held business makes up more than 35 percent of the estate after debts and expenses, the tax
on it can be paid in up to ten annual installments, with the first payment of principal delayed
by up to five years. Some farm and business real property can be valued at what it’s worth in
its current use instead of market value, but the heirs have to keep using it that way for ten
years without selling it or repay the tax saved.25

A deduction for family businesses would go further. It would exempt qualifying business in-
terests whether or not the estate had trouble paying the tax. It would also narrow the base,
since on taxable federal estate tax returns filed nationwide in 2024, closely held stock, farm
assets and other business assets were about 19 percent of gross estates.26 And owners could
avoid the tax by holding more of their wealth in a form that qualifies. The federal govern-
ment had such a deduction for deaths from 1998 to 2003, and 1,470 returns filed in 2000
claimed it.27 Washington and Minnesota now allow capped deductions for family businesses
and farms, and Maryland excludes up to $5 million of farm property.28 Minnesota estimates
that its deduction costs about $19 million to $20 million a year.
7.2 The property tax and the GET as taxes on wealth
The property tax is the part of a wealth tax that Hawaiʻi can collect every year. What makes it
attractive is that it is easy to measure and hard to avoid. The assessor can estimate the value
of land and buildings from sales of similar properties, and neither can move, although taxes
on buildings can reduce construction and upkeep (chapter 5).29 This is why chapter 1 puts
land at the least responsive end of its ordering, and wealth at the most responsive end. The
tax also applies to property owned by people who live somewhere else. If the owner sells, the
buyer pays the tax, but the property is still taxed, and expected taxes can lower what a buyer
is willing to pay.
As a wealth tax, the property tax has limits, since it misses financial wealth and most of the
value of businesses. It is based on the market value of the property, so a mortgage does not
reduce it.30 Values are estimated by the assessor, and chapter 5 (section 5.4) discusses how
accurate they are.
A common complaint in Hawaiʻi is that the value of a home has gone up, and with it the
property tax, but the owner does not have more money. This is the same difference between
value and cash that arises for family businesses at death. The owner is richer and could sell
the house or borrow against it, but the tax has to be paid in cash every year, whatever the
owner’s income.
The GET is another tax on wealth that is not called a wealth tax. People who move to Hawaiʻi

with savings they earned somewhere else while nonresident generally have not paid Hawaiʻi
income tax on that income, but they pay the GET when they spend the savings here, and so
do visitors. The GET does this without anyone having to put a value on what people own.31
A permanent increase in the GET is also like a one-time tax on wealth. An increase in a tax
on spending that people did not expect lowers what their savings can buy, so in this sense it
falls on wealth people already have as well as on income.32
7.3 An annual wealth tax
An annual tax on net wealth is a popular idea. Economists such as Saez and Zucman have
made the case for one in the United States.33 The evidence makes it unlikely that such a tax,
levied by Hawaiʻi alone, would raise much revenue at all, partly because wealth is so difficult
to measure and partly because people respond strongly to the tax, by reporting less wealth or
by moving and taking their income tax with them.
Wealth is very hard to measure. Publicly traded stocks are easy, since we just need to look
at today’s market price. A painting might be sold once in twenty years, and a closely held
family business that has never been sold has no price at all. Kopczuk emphasizes that wealth
taxes depend on valuations that cannot always be checked against an actual sale between
independent buyers and sellers.34 In our example, the county assessor puts a value on the
home and the market puts a value on the stocks, but no one puts a value on the business until
it is sold or its owner dies. The wealth of the very wealthy is the hardest to measure, since as
we go up the wealth distribution, more of what people own is privately held businesses and
other assets that are hard to value.35
Most countries that had an annual wealth tax have given up on it. Twelve OECD countries
had one in 1990 and four still did in 2017, and apart from Switzerland, those that kept it
raised little revenue from it.36 Reported wealth also responds strongly to the tax. Across Swiss
cantons, a one percentage point cut in the wealth tax rate raised reported taxable wealth

34 Kopczuk (2019), p. 518. Advani, Chamberlain and Summers (2020), pp. 52 and 56–57, describe

Table 9, p. 102). These are projections for the United Kingdom, not observed costs. The commission
preferred a one-time wealth tax to an annual one (p. 106). A one-time tax faces the same problem
of valuing assets, though only once.

Spain brought back its wealth tax in 2011, every region except Madrid levied it, and wealthy

other regions lost when these taxpayers moved was six times the wealth tax they lost from
them.38
Unlike the property tax, a tax on net worth is about the person instead of the asset, and a
person can move.
7.4 Design choices and tradeoffs
Keeping the exclusion fixed at $5.49 million would bring more estates into the tax as prices
and asset values rise. The state could instead index it to inflation or follow the federal exclu-
sion, which is $15 million in 2026 and indexed after that.39 Following the federal amount
would exempt estates like those in Table 7.1 between $5.49 and $15 million. Larger estates
would also pay less, because the exclusion is subtracted before the rates apply: Hawaiʻi’s tax
on the $30 million estate would fall by about $1.9 million. The state could also rely more on
the federal government’s review of returns.
Rates determine how much we collect from large estates. As section 7.1 explains, their owners
may respond to higher rates by moving or changing their estate plans. Above $15 million,
each added dollar of Hawaiʻi tax from a higher top rate costs the largest estates as little as 60
cents because of the federal deduction (Table 7.1). We may be able to estimate the effect of
repealing the estate tax in the final report.
For closely held businesses, Hawaiʻi could keep the current rules, cap a deduction as Wash-
ington and Minnesota do, or deduct the full value. A cap would limit the revenue lost to the
deduction. Either way, DOTAX would have to check that people qualify. How much tax they
can avoid by moving or giving away assets depends on the rules for residence, gifts and other

fifth came from higher house prices. Saving explains little of the rest, which the authors interpret

basis it had for the person who gave it, with no step-up (26 U.S.C. §1015(a)). It also matters that
federal estate-inclusion rules can reach property transferred with specified retained interests or
powers (26 U.S.C. §§2036, 2038), as well as certain transfers and gift-tax payments within three
years of death (§2035). Gifts included in the gross estate are excluded from adjusted taxable gifts
under §2001(b).
42 HRS §236E-8(c)(1)-(2), compilation p. 5; Form M-6 instructions, pp. 4-5. The credit applies when
the other state’s tax is “not qualified by a reciprocal provision allowing the property to be taxed in
the state of the decedent’s domicile”. It is the smaller of the death tax actually paid to the other
state and the Hawaiʻi tax multiplied by that property’s share of the gross estate.

8. Tax credits
This chapter draws heavily on analyses first prepared by Branden Hing, a former
UHERO research assistant.
Tax credits are sometimes called tax expenditures, because they are spending that happens
through the tax code by providing tax relief to people. To a tax economist, there is not that
much daylight between a credit and just spending money on a program. When a household
on Oʻahu claims $5,000 for the solar panels on its roof, the state collects $5,000 less in tax
(or provides a refund), and the questions to ask are the ones we would ask of any program:
what does the credit cost the state on net, and what does it cause to happen that would not
have happened anyway?
In TY2023, Hawaiʻi taxpayers claimed $621.3 million in tax credits.1 DOTAX sorts them into
three groups by purpose. About $243 million went to credits intended to avoid taxing the
same income twice or taxing business inputs again as goods pass from one business to the
next (pyramiding; chapter 3). About $218 million went to credits that give relief to targeted
groups, like the earned income tax credit and the food/excise tax credit, and $152 million
to credits intended to change what certain households and businesses do, like the renewable
energy technologies income tax credit and the film production credit.
A relief credit works if the money goes to the households it was meant for, even if nobody
does anything differently. For an incentive credit, money to people who would have done it
anyway is close to just giving them cash, and what it causes has to be worth its cost. Section
8.1 describes each group and compares Hawaiʻi’s credits with those of other states. Section
8.2 sets out seven questions to ask of any credit, and sections 8.3 to 8.5 take up the solar
credit, the film credit and the other business credits.
8.1 Credits as spending
Figure 8.1 shows the credits in each of DOTAX’s three groups. Expired credits and amounts
not shown separately make up the remaining amount of about $8 million.2 We use these
groups, except for two credits (the capital goods credit and the low-income housing credit),
for reasons given below.
1 DOTAX, Tax Credits Claimed by Hawaii Taxpayers, Tax Year 2023, Table 4, PDF p.14 (printed p.10).
Amounts claimed on all returns.
2 Our sums of the rows of DOTAX’s Table 4 (PDF p.14): “Tax Credits to Avoid Double Taxation or
Pyramiding of Taxes” $242.8 million (pass-through entity $133.8 million, tax paid to another state
$74.4 million, capital goods excise $34.6 million); “Tax Credits to Promote Social Welfare” $218.4
million; “Tax Credits to Encourage Certain Industries or Economic Activities” $152.4 million among
active credits. Amounts DOTAX does not publish separately total $3.5 million.

The pass-through entity tax credit ($133.8 million) and the credit for income tax paid to
another state ($74.4 million) prevent the same income from being taxed twice. The first gives
owners of a partnership or S corporation credit for the tax their business has already paid on
their share of its income. The second does the same for a resident who also earned income
on the mainland. Both are part of the definition of the income tax base, so counting them as
134

===== page 137 =====
Hawaiʻi Tax Review Commission · Draft report 8. Tax credits
spending would overstate spending through credits by a third of the $621.3 million.3
The earned income tax credit ($77.1 million in TY2023) and the food/excise tax credit ($64.0
million) provide cash support through the tax return. Both are refundable, so a family can
receive them even if it owes no income tax. Since tax year 2023, the state earned income
credit has equaled 40 percent of the federal amount.4 These payments help a low-income
family whether or not it changes its work or purchases: each dollar received provides a dollar
of relief. Chapter 2 considers which families receive the credits, why some eligible families do
not claim them, and how the loss of eligibility affects a family whose income rises.
The child and dependent care credit ($14.8 million) serves both purposes.5 By lowering what
it costs parents to go to work, it is intended to change their behavior, but it also provides
targeted tax relief to families. The earned income credit has both purposes as well, since it
requires earned income.6
The capital goods excise tax credit ($34.6 million) is intended to reduce tax pyramiding. The
credit returns 4 percent of what a business spends on qualifying machinery or equipment,
approximately covering the GET on the purchase. Chapter 3 discusses why taxing these pur-
chases can cause pyramiding and how credits compare with exemptions. Under Act 24 (SLH
2026), the credit ends for tax years beginning after December 31, 2027.7
We treat the low-income housing tax credit ($60.9 million) as an incentive, although DOTAX
includes it among the social welfare credits. It pays developers and investors to build or keep
affordable rental units, so its value depends on how many units exist because of it, which is the
incentive test.8 The renewable energy technologies credit ($100.1 million) and the film credit
($43.5 million) are the two largest in DOTAX’s group of credits that encourage industries or
activities.

Other states have credits for many of the same purposes (Table 8.1). Hawaiʻi is one of 32 states
with an earned income credit and one of 28 where the credit is refundable.9 At 40 percent
of the federal credit, Hawaiʻi’s is the same as New Jersey’s and larger than New York’s (30
percent, less any household credit) or New Mexico’s (25 percent). Hawaiʻi’s rate is tied for
5th among the 48 jurisdictions in Figure 8.2, and if we count the states with no credit as zero,
the median is 10 percent.
Hawaiʻi’s rate of 35 percent for rooftop solar is the highest in the table, but its $5,000 cap is
the same as New York’s. So for an example $20,000 system, a household would get the same
$5,000 in Hawaiʻi as it would in New York (if it can use the full credit). It would get less in
New Mexico ($2,000), and much less in Arizona ($1,000). Hawaiʻi now also has a limit on
the total credit of $40 million a year. That is $10 million higher than the limit New Mexico
has for solar, but the Hawaiʻi credit also has to pay for wind and other kinds of systems.10
The film credit pays 22 to 32 percent of qualified costs, which compares with California (35
percent), New Mexico (25 percent, and up to 40 percent in some cases) and Georgia (20 or
30 percent). The states differ most in their limits. Hawaiʻi allows $60 million of film credit
a year. California allows $750 million, which is 12.5 times as much, and Georgia has no
annual limit. A limit is only the most the state is willing to pay, though. The rates are not
directly comparable either, because states differ in whether the credit is paid as cash,11 in

8.2 Seven questions for any credit
Seven questions give a rough-and-ready way of assessing any credit (Figure 8.3), one that can
be applied quickly to a specific case even without a lot of evidence. A full evaluation would
answer them with data.
14 Connecticut Department of Revenue Services, 2025 Form CT-1040 instructions, pp. 3 and 17: 40%

===== page 142 =====
Hawaiʻi Tax Review Commission · Draft report 8. Tax credits
Figure 8.3. Seven questions for any tax credit
Note: Conceptual figure. Source: UHERO.
What is the goal?
What counts as a benefit depends on what we are trying to accomplish, and for whom. Relief
for working families, cleaner electricity, jobs and cultural goals each call for different, mea-
surable evidence. If the credit is meant to reduce emissions, for example, we would want to
know how many tons of carbon dioxide it avoids.
Who benefits?
Who actually benefits from a credit is a question of fact, and the answer often reaches be-
yond the people the credit is meant for. Part of each dollar of solar credit goes to installers
through higher prices, for example, and most of its climate benefit goes to people who live
outside Hawaiʻi (section 8.3). It is not for economists to say whether the gains of nonresidents
should count as much as those of residents. That is for the Commissioners, policymakers and
ultimately the people of Hawaiʻi to decide, so we report the results separately for Hawaiʻi
140

===== page 143 =====
Hawaiʻi Tax Review Commission · Draft report 8. Tax credits
residents and for everyone.
Who actually responds?
For a credit meant to incentivize or change behavior, we need to know who would have done
the same thing regardless. Consider two households claiming the solar credit. One would
have installed panels without it. For this household, the credit is like free money, and the
state gets no extra installation or reduction in emissions. The other household would not
choose to install without the credit, so its installation is what the state is paying for. The gains
and losses can also reach other people, including other electricity customers and everyone
affected by a changing climate.
Whether a credit changes behavior, and by how much, is a critical part of the analysis, and
it is often not given the serious treatment it needs. Bartik concludes that a typical incentive
changes the location decision of between 2 and 25 percent of firms receiving it.15 Even at the
top of that range, for each firm an incentive brings in, three others receive it for doing what
they would have done anyway. Households buying panels and productions choosing where
to film make different decisions, so sections 8.3 and 8.4 look at the evidence on those.
What is the net gain?
For a household that would not install without the credit, the gain is less than the amount it
receives. For this household, the credit is not like receiving cash. There is something it was
not willing to do, but is now willing to do because of the discount, which means the panels
are worth less to the household than their full price. Part of the credit covers that gap, and
the household gains only what is left.
A resident film-crew member hired by a production is in much the same position: her gain is
what the job pays above what she would have earned in other work. Thus, counting all of the
spending or payroll as a benefit overstates what the credit buys.
What is the cost to the state?
When we ask what a credit costs the state, we should ask what it costs on net. We need to
include the credit itself, administration and any other public costs, and subtract the extra
revenue and savings in spending that it causes. An installation or production that would have
happened anyway would already have paid GET and income tax, so we can count only the
taxes on activity caused by the credit against its cost. This budget payback need not cover the
whole credit. In the film example in section 8.4, it falls well short.
Is there a better alternative?
A natural benchmark for a credit is just having the government send out checks to people
(probably not literally checks; direct deposit is more efficient). A dollar spent this way gives a
dollar of benefits to whoever receives it. We can then ask whether, for each dollar of net cost,
the credit gives the people we are counting more or less than they would get from the checks.
Getting more usually requires a benefit that the checks do not provide, such as reducing
pollution that markets leave alone. A ratio of $1.10 of benefits per dollar of net cost does
15 Bartik (2018), PDF pp.3, 5 and 12–13. The 2–25 percent range is his conclusion from 34 estimates,
not their full range; he judges a majority of those estimates likely to be too high.
141

===== page 144 =====
Hawaiʻi Tax Review Commission · Draft report 8. Tax credits
not mean that the government gets $1.10 back in revenue. It just means $1.10 in benefits is
generated for someone.16
What could change?
Finally, we can ask which of our answers, had it been different, would have changed the
conclusion. The point is to see what the conclusion rests on. We would like to think we can
intuit our way to the answer, but sometimes we might be led astray. For most incentive credits,
the answer that matters most is how much of the activity the credit causes. For film, DBEDT
estimated that 60 percent of production spending was due to the credit, and section 8.4 sets
this beside evidence from other states.17
Answers to the other questions can matter just as much, including those about the goal and
about who benefits. Take the film credit. Pay to nonresident cast and crew qualifies for the
credit, and nonresidents received nearly half of the wages that qualified for the credit in 2023
(section 8.4). Payments to nonresidents can help bring a production here and support jobs
for residents. But if the goal is to raise residents’ earnings, we should ask whether the same
expenditure would do more if the credit paid a higher rate on their wages and on Hawaiʻi
purchases. The answer depends on how productions respond to the different incentives.
Some of these missing facts could be collected, and chapter 11 discusses how.
8.3 The renewable energy technologies income tax credit
The solar credit’s benefits depend heavily on whose benefits we count. By our estimates, each
dollar of net state expenditure produces about 71 cents of benefits for Hawaiʻi residents and
$1.95 for everyone, including the global benefit of lower emissions.18 These estimates are for
household rooftop solar under the law before 2026, without the federal credit, and they do
not evaluate the new cap and income limits.19
Taxpayers claimed $100.1 million of the credit in TY2023 ($58.3 million on individual returns,
$38.6 million on corporate returns and $3.2 million on other returns).20 We analyze the solar
panel component for households, because it is the part that is easiest to analyze. The corporate
claims and wind systems are not included.
A household can claim 35 percent of what a solar system costs, up to $5,000 on a single-
16 Hendren and Sprung-Keyser (2020) use this ratio of benefits to the government’s net cost. A simple
transfer, like the checks, has a ratio of one (p. 1211). Revenue that comes back to the state counts

family home, so the cap limits the credit on any system costing more than $14,285.71, and
on a typical $20,000 system the credit covers 25 percent of the cost.21 The credit used to exist
alongside a 30 percent federal credit, which ended for expenditures made after December 31,
2025. On a system below the cap, the household’s out-of-pocket cost used to be 35 cents on
the dollar, and moving forward it is 65 cents on the dollar.22
Act 24 (SLH 2026) changed the credit. Claims must now be certified by the Hawaiʻi State
Energy Office, and the total credit is limited to $40 million a year, with each claimant receiving
a share in proportion to the amount claimed. Beginning in TY2027, the credit is no longer
available to individuals with Hawaiʻi adjusted gross income above $175,000, or joint filers
above $350,000, and it ends after TY2029.23 The estimates in this section are for the law
before these changes.
We do not yet have an estimate for the solar credit under current law. We expect to provide one
in the final report.
Benefits per dollar of state expenditure
The denominator includes credits for installations that would have happened anyway and for
those caused by the credit. We currently assume no administrative cost or change in other
tax revenue, so net state expenditure in this calculation equals total credits paid. These are
benefits per dollar of that expenditure; the dollar itself has not been subtracted from them.
Figure 8.4 shows how the benefits divide among the people affected. Households that would
have installed panels anyway receive 55 cents per dollar of net state expenditure. For them, the
credit is like free money rather than a reason to install. Households that install only because
of the credit gain 11 cents, less than the credit they receive, because part of it covers the
gap between the cost of the system and what it was worth to them. Installers gain 16 cents,
because part of the credit shows up as higher installation prices.24
Utility shareholders and other ratepayers together lose 11 cents in the calculation. As more
people install panels and buy less electricity, the utility saves on generation but still has to
pay for its poles, wires and power plants. It can recover those remaining costs through higher
prices for other customers, or its profits can fall. Adding these gains and losses gives 71 cents
of benefits to residents per dollar of net state expenditure, counting installers and utility share-

Our estimate of gains and losses to Hawaiʻi residents counts none of the climate benefit, be-
cause the benefits of reduced carbon emissions go primarily to people who do not live in the
state. This is just the nature of the policy, and it is not a commentary on whether the credit
is worth doing. The estimate that counts everyone values the global benefit at the Environ-

that accrue to nonresidents reduce the resident benefit. Utility losses borne by nonresident share-
holders increase it, because those losses no longer fall on residents. Losses recovered through higher
electricity bills still fall on the customers who pay them. We have not estimated these ownership
shares or how the utility loss divides between shareholders and customers.

mental Protection Agency’s estimate of the damage done by a ton of carbon dioxide.26 How
much we value the benefits to each group is a value judgment. Depending on that choice, the
benefit is below or above a dollar per dollar of net state expenditure. The resident comparison
is therefore 71 cents of benefits against a dollar available for another use; it is not a 71-cent
gain after paying for the credit.
Installations caused, ratepayer costs and grid emissions
The result depends on how many households install panels only because of the credit. We
assume about 46 additional installations for every 100 that would have happened without
it, based on research on California.27 Mainland evidence does show that subsidies cause in-
stallations: in California, rebates account for an estimated 53 percent of installations made
between 2007 and 2012.28
A stronger response raises climate benefits and lowers residents’ benefits, because more of
each dollar goes to households whose own gain is small (Figure 8.5). With a response to
price a little over a third or about three-quarters as strong as the one assumed (about 15 or
33 extra installations per 100), residents’ benefits are 88 or 78 cents per dollar of net state
expenditure, and benefits counting everyone are $1.39 or $1.74.29 In each case the benefit
to residents stays below a dollar per dollar of net state expenditure, and the benefit counting
everyone stays above it.

Figure 8.5. The assumed demand response and the benefit per dollar of net state expenditure
Note: Benefits per dollar of net state expenditure in the model, state credit alone; the cost assumptions are as in
Figure 8.4. The horizontal axis gives the percentage increase in installations associated with a small 1 percent fall
in the price households pay. A value of 1.14 means a 1 percent price decrease raises installations by about 1.14
percent for a small price change. The dashed line marks the response we assume, about 46 extra installations per
100 that would happen without the credit. The two solid lines mark the lower responses described in the text,
about 15 and 33 per 100. Source: UHERO calculations; Appendix J.
The cost to other ratepayers is the most Hawaiʻi-specific and least certain number in the anal-
ysis. It shows up as a cost either through reduced profits to the utility and its shareholders
or as higher rates for other ratepayers. To approximate the loss, we subtract avoided genera-
tion costs from the revenue lost on each kilowatt-hour no longer sold, then apply the utility’s
allowed return on equity, the regulator-approved rate of return to shareholders. This gives
the 11-cent cost to shareholders and other ratepayers together. We do not have an estimate
of the share of lost revenue borne by shareholders and other customers. Using the allowed
return for this purpose is an approximation. If the utility recovered the entire loss from other
customers, they would bear a cost many times larger. A Hawaiʻi estimate would require ev-
idence on the fixed costs recovered through electricity rates and the costs the utility avoids
when households generate their own power.
146

===== page 149 =====
Hawaiʻi Tax Review Commission · Draft report 8. Tax credits
The climate benefit depends on which plants produce less power when households install
solar. To keep it simple, we use one emissions rate for the whole state and the life of the
system, from the Oʻahu plants that go up and down with demand. To the extent the utility
can decarbonize the grid, a panel installed today would replace cleaner power as it ages, and
the climate benefit would be smaller.30
Claimants and alternatives
Most of the credit goes to higher-income households. DOTAX’s report on the credits claimed
in TY2023 shows that of the $58.3 million claimed on individual returns, 72 percent went to
filers with income of $100,000 or more, and 44.6 percent to those with $200,000 or more. The
income measure is Hawaiʻi adjusted gross income for residents, and worldwide income under
Hawaiʻi tax rules for nonresidents and part-year residents.31 This is probably not so surprising:
higher-income households are more likely to have a home suited to panels and to be able to
finance the installation. The national figures look similar. Among the four federal clean energy
credits for households from 2006 to 2012, including rooftop solar, the top fifth of earners
received about 60 percent of the money and the bottom three-fifths about 10 percent.32
Hawaiʻi households with Hawaiʻi adjusted gross income of $20,000 or less ($40,000 for joint
filers) can receive the state credit as a refund. Other households can also take a refund, but
must accept a credit 30 percent smaller. In TY2023, individuals claimed $13.4 million of
the credit as refundable and $44.5 million as nonrefundable.33 The income limits starting in
TY2027 will exclude the households at the top.
What could the state do with the revenue if it ended the credit? One possibility is to have
the utility buy large solar installations supplying the electricity grid, which might be a more
cost-effective way to do the same thing. Studies of climate policies in the United States and
Europe put the cost of avoiding a ton of carbon dioxide through solar subsidies at about $140
to $2,100. Part of the difference is that a large array costs much less per kilowatt to build than
panels on a house.3435 In Hawaiʻi we would also need to account for land, transmission lines
and reliability, and ask whether the savings would reach ratepayers.

8.4 The film production credit
The film credit refunds part of what productions spend in Hawaiʻi. This section sets out its
rules, the questions its design raises and the evidence so far on what it achieves.
Rates and rules of the film credit
The credit refunds 22 percent of qualified production costs incurred on Oʻahu and 27 percent
of those incurred in the other counties. Act 185 (SLH 2026) adds 5 percentage points for pro-
ductions whose workforce is at least 80 percent local hires, for costs incurred after December
31, 2025, raising the rates to 27 and 32 percent.36
The same act raised the total credit allowed each year from $50 million to $60 million, raised
the limit per production from $17 million to $20 million (with no limit for productions spend-
ing at least $60 million) and extended the program from January 1, 2033, to January 1, 2038.
The Council on Revenues estimates that the credit changes will cost the General Fund $10
million a year.37 In TY2023, 24 returns claimed $43.5 million of film credit.38
The film credit can finance nonresident salaries and purchases from businesses outside
Hawaiʻi. Pay to nonresident cast and crew for work in Hawaiʻi qualifies for the credit.39 These
wages are taxable here, as other wages earned in Hawaiʻi are, so some of the credit comes
back in income tax. But the credit still pays 22 to 32 cents of every dollar a nonresident earns
for this work. To qualify for the new local-hire bonus, at least 80 percent of the people in
a production’s workforce must be Hawaiʻi residents. This counts people, regardless of how
much of the payroll they receive.40
For purchases from businesses outside the state to qualify, a production has to show that it
tried to find comparable inputs in Hawaiʻi and could not.41 Money paid to nonresidents and
out-of-state businesses is not part of the income of Hawaiʻi residents, although it can still
support local jobs, purchases and tax receipts.
Not every film credit pays for work done by nonresidents. Ontario’s film credits count only
spending on Ontario residents and businesses. Its credit for Canadian productions pays 35
percent of wages paid to Ontario residents, and its credit for other productions pays 21.5
36 HRS §235-17(a); Act 185 (SLH 2026), §1 and §7; DOTAX, Tax Information Release 2026-03, p.1.
The credit is refundable.

Benefit and Fiscal Impact Analysis of Hawaiʻi’s Film Tax Credit in 2023, pp.7 and 22 (PDF pp.9 and
24).

and a local hire is a resident “during the calendar year for which the Film Credit is being claimed.”

work for its own residents and sales by its own businesses.
Evidence from other states and from Hawaiʻi
DBEDT estimates that the state collects between 30 and 50 cents in tax revenue for each dollar
of film credit, a net fiscal loss of $19 million to $27 million. DBEDT also finds that “there is
little evidence that the film credit has generated a local film industry that can continue at the
same scale without the ongoing support of credits.”4445
Several studies of film incentives find little or no lasting gain in film jobs or wages, even though
credits affect where some productions shoot. Button’s study of state film incentives across the
United States finds that adopting an incentive raised the number of television series filmed
in a state, with no meaningful effect on feature films or on employment, wages or business
establishments in the film industry and related industries.46 California allocated its first film
credit partly by lottery, and a third of the projects that applied and lost, and were eventually
made, filmed in California.47 State evaluations that compare tax revenue with credit cost,
each with its own method and years, find returns of 7 cents per dollar of credit in Florida,
15 to 19 cents in Georgia, and 20 cents in Virginia.48 An evaluation prepared for Georgia’s
Department of Audits and Accounts estimated large effects from its film credit. We do not find

these estimates credible.49
Some of the gain to one state comes from productions moving from another. How much
production the credit attracts is a different question from how much would leave if it ended.
Crews, suppliers and studios that have grown up around it may stay for a while, or may follow
productions elsewhere. Studies of states that ended their credits find falls in film employment,
but the estimates are too imprecise to give a share.50
DBEDT’s data show Hawaiʻi residents received about 53 percent of the wages that qualified
for the credit in 2023. The gap is largest at the top of the payroll: of the 2023 wages paid
to principal cast, directors, producers and writers, residents received about 15 percent ($5.3
million) and nonresidents $30.8 million.5152
Average private-sector pay in Hawaiʻi’s motion picture and video production industry was
$58,912 in 2024, against $122,861 nationally. DBEDT puts Hawaiʻi’s average wage across
all industries at $67,356. Film work in Hawaiʻi pays less on average than work across all
industries here, and far less than film work nationally. These are industry averages, which do
not separate residents from nonresidents or account for hours worked.53
One possible benefit of the film credit is that it promotes tourism, and this is an indirect
effect that is very uncertain. To the extent it is true, we would want to count the extra wages
of residents working in tourism who would not have been working otherwise, and also any
costs, if some residents feel that more tourism makes their lives worse.
DBEDT attributes $1.05 billion of 2022 visitor spending (5.3 percent of the total) to film
and television made in Hawaiʻi, a figure that reflects “the cumulative effect of years of televi-
sion program and movie filming,” and it leaves tourism out of its cost-benefit analysis of the
credit.54

One option for the film credit is to limit eligible spending to wages for Hawaiʻi residents
and purchases from Hawaiʻi businesses, raising the credit rate to keep total state expenditure
approximately unchanged. This would give productions more reason to hire and buy locally,
though how much work residents gain depends on whether local workers and suppliers can
replace those brought in from elsewhere. It would also change which productions receive the
most support, and keeping the state’s total cost unchanged would not necessarily keep the
number of productions unchanged. To judge whether this is a better use of the credit, we
would need to estimate the additional employment and earnings for residents, as section 8.2
explains.
If part of the purpose of the credit is cultural, then we would want culturally specific criteria
applied in allocating it, favoring productions that are about Hawaiʻi or that tell stories that
are important to Hawaiʻi, which probably does not include every movie that has ever been
shot here. The credit’s rate currently depends on the county where the cost is incurred and,
since 2026, the share of residents hired, but neither the production’s subject nor its creators’
cultural ties to Hawaiʻi affects the rate.
Table 8.2 applies the seven questions of section 8.2 to the solar and film credits.55 For film,
the evidence does not answer several of the questions.

8.5 Renewable fuels and other business credits
The remaining business credits raise the same questions, and 2026 law moved them in both
directions: Act 24 ends the renewable fuels, research and high technology credits, while other
acts extend the enterprise zone and low-income housing credits.56
The renewable fuels production tax credit pays per unit of qualifying fuel sold in Hawaiʻi, for
fuels whose emissions over their production and use are below those of fossil fuels.57 Claims
were $69,000 in TY2023, and the credit ends after TY2028.
The research activities credit ($7.0 million in TY2023) and the high technology business in-
vestment credit are repealed as of January 1, 2029. The high technology credit was closed to
new investment in 2010, yet Pew reports that the State Auditor found in 2015 that the state
still faced obligations of hundreds of millions of dollars from credits that could be carried
forward to later years without limit.58
56 Act 24 (SLH 2026), §§3–7 and 9; Act 182 (SLH 2026), §7; Act 205 (SLH 2026), §§2–3.
57 HRS §235-110.32, as enacted in Act 24 (SLH 2026), §4, including subsection (o), which ends el-
igibility after TY2028. Producers must make at least 2.5 billion British thermal units a year, total
credits are limited to $20 million a year, and claims need Hawaiʻi State Energy Office certification.
58 Research-credit repeal: Act 261 (SLH 2019), Part II, as amended by Act 24 (SLH 2026), §7; high-
technology-credit repeal: Act 24 (SLH 2026), §§5 and 9(4). Pew Charitable Trusts (2017), PDF pp.11
and 55, reports the State Auditor’s finding on carryforward obligations. Claims for the research and
fuels credits: DOTAX, Tax Credits Claimed, TY2023, Table 4, PDF p.14.
