===== page 155 =====
Hawaiʻi Tax Review Commission · Draft report 8. Tax credits
The enterprise zone credit ($639,000 in TY2023) targets businesses in designated areas, and
Act 182 (SLH 2026) extends the period over which it can be claimed from seven to nine
years.59
The low-income housing tax credit is the largest of these ($60.9 million in TY2023). Act 205
(SLH 2026) lets a partner or member that is a partnership or limited liability company (LLC)
sell an allocated credit once to another taxpayer, and keeps the 2016 expansion of the credit
in force through December 31, 2032. The Council on Revenues estimates that the act will cost
the General Fund $57.1 million in FY2028, rising to $72.9 million in FY2033.60
The closest thing we have found to an estimate of what the credit causes is Georgia’s evalu-
ation of its state credit.61 It attributes about 21 percent of the low-income rental units made
available for occupancy from 2000 to 2009 to the credit. Taking out units that would have
been built anyway, it finds the credit’s net effect on state output and revenue is negative.62
The final report will say more about the evidence on business tax credits more broadly.

===== page 159 =====
9. Other matters
This chapter will be completed in the final report. It will cover administrative issues, like how
DOTAX handles people who come forward on their own and pay taxes they did not pay. It may
also look at revenue volatility, which is how much Hawaiʻi’s tax revenue goes up and down
from one year to the next. This is an important thing to keep in mind when you’re thinking
about the reforms in this report, so we may look at whether each one would make it more or
less stable. Other things that do not fit in other chapters will go here too.
157

===== page 160 =====
10. Joint tax reforms: revenue and
household effects
Before allowing for changes in behavior, a tax cut reduces revenue and a tax increase raises it.
People’s responses can change both amounts, including the revenue from other taxes. We can
combine tax cuts and increases in many different ways. Our Hawaiʻi household model, built on
PolicyEngine, looks at both changes at once.1 Estimates for the paired reforms require further
model refinement and will appear in the final report. Figure 10.1 reports our estimate of the
grocery exemption on its own and provides an example of the summaries that will accompany
the paired reforms in the final report. None of these reforms is a recommendation.
The model starts from a public Census survey of Hawaiʻi households, pooling three years of
responses. The survey does not ask about capital gains, among other things, so we take IRS tax
records for households like them and use those to fill it in. Spending gets filled in the same
way, from a national survey, and then adjusted to match what Honolulu households spend.
After that we reweight the data so that it matches what we know about Hawaiʻi as a whole,
like the population, total income at each income level, how many people claim each credit,
and how many are on SNAP and Medicaid. Some people who qualify for a credit never claim
it, and the model allows for that. UHERO forecasts of income and population carry it forward
to 2027.
For each household, the model works out state and federal taxes and benefits under current
law, and then again with both changes in place. People can respond to income-tax changes.
They can report more or less income, sell assets sooner or later, and decide whether to work. At
this stage, nobody moves to or from Hawaiʻi and visitors keep spending the same. For the GET,
the model includes the spending of residents’ grocery savings but otherwise holds purchases
fixed. We add separate estimates of what visitors and nonresident filers pay. That will give us
the effect on revenue, and it will tell us who would be affected and how. Each pair will be set so
that State General Fund revenue in 2027 is unchanged. Appendix C describes the household
model in more detail, Appendix A explains how revenue is compared, and Appendix L will
give the detailed household results.
10.1 An example: exempting groceries
We assume that stores pass the full grocery GET exemption through to shoppers in lower
prices. Before changes in spending, the state and counties would lose $267.4 million in 2027:
$237.7 million for the state and $29.7 million for the counties. Residents spend their savings
and pay GET on those additional purchases, returning about $4.7 million to the state and $0.6
1 PolicyEngine is an open-source model of federal and state tax and benefit rules in the United States,
maintained by the organization of the same name. Our Hawaiʻi household model is built on it,
with Hawaiʻi data and adjustments. The tax cut and the tax increase in each pair use the same
assumptions.
158

===== page 161 =====
Hawaiʻi Tax Review Commission · Draft report 10. Joint tax reforms
million to the counties. The resulting revenue losses are $233.0 million and $29.1 million. Of
the initial grocery savings, visitors receive $53.3 million: $47.3 million from the state tax
and $5.9 million from county surcharges, with totals differing slightly because of rounding.
Chapter 3 discusses the evidence on how much of a tax cut stores pass through.
After the GET on additional spending, the exemption would benefit almost all Hawaiʻi house-
holds, by about $419 a year on average. Higher-income households save more in dollars, since
they spend more. The saving is larger relative to income at the bottom, however, because these
households spend a greater share of their income. It amounts to about 1.3 percent of income
in the bottom tenth, compared with 0.1 percent in the top tenth. Households are ranked by
cash income before taxes, including pensions, Social Security and other cash benefits, but
excluding benefits such as SNAP and WIC.2
Figure 10.1 reports state revenue effects, while household savings include both state and
county taxes. It shows the exemption on its own, before any tax increase or credit reduction
to pay for it.

spending. The revenue bridge in the text uses separately rounded figures.

For a simple illustration of what financing changes, suppose a household saves $400 on gro-
ceries but pays $500 more in income tax. Its resources fall by $100. A household with the
same grocery savings and a $200 income-tax increase gains $200. These are hypothetical
households, not estimates from the model. The final report will estimate those differences for
the paired reforms.
10.2 Estimates for the final report
Each pair below holds State General Fund revenue in 2027 unchanged:
• Exempt groceries from the GET, and raise all income-tax rates by the same proportion.
• Exempt groceries from the GET, and reduce the food/excise tax credit.
• Tax long-term capital gains as ordinary income, and lower the top three income-tax
rates.
• Raise the top income-tax rate by one percentage point, and lower the bottom three rates
or enlarge the food/excise tax credit.
• Exempt long-term capital gains, and raise the top three income-tax rates.
• Raise the GET rate by half a percentage point, and lower all income-tax rates by the
same proportion.
The final report will also estimate:
• a cut in the corporate income tax rate (chapter 6)
• a statewide property tax and, if possible, property tax rates that differ by owner and
type of property (chapter 5)
• a higher TAT paired with compensation for residents (chapter 4)
• GET pyramiding (chapter 3)
• migration responses to income-tax changes (chapter 11)

11. What we cannot know yet, and how
Hawaiʻi could know more
There are things in this report that we cannot know right now, or that are highly uncertain,
but which we think Hawaiʻi could know much better given additional research and better use
of information it already has. Our estimates of revenue and of who gains and loses depend on
how people and businesses respond to taxes. Most of the evidence on these responses comes
from other places.1 It helps show how taxes affect work, investment, prices and where people
live. But a large share of purchases in Hawaiʻi are made by visitors, housing costs are among
the highest in the country, and moving to another state requires crossing an ocean. Responses
here could differ from those on the mainland.
Our household model combines several sources, and income the household survey does not
collect (capital gains, for example) comes from national tax data. Its results give us a starting
point, with considerable uncertainty about the amounts.
Take migration. The final report will add estimates of how many people might move in re-
sponse to the reforms. These may have to rest on research from other places, and will be
more uncertain than the other estimates.
Hawaiʻi data could sharpen those estimates and show where responses here differ. We will
use Hawaiʻi estimates where we can, and expect the model to become more precise over time.
Other gaps are harder to close. Taxes change alongside other things in the economy, and it can
be hard to tell how much of what happens is due to the tax. Research often gives us a range of
possible responses. Which of these is most relevant for Hawaiʻi depends on the circumstances.
Much of the information we would want is already in the records of DOTAX and other state
agencies. DOTAX has tax returns, and other agencies have records of wages and government
benefits such as SNAP. Researchers in government and outside it have difficulty using these
records together. We recommend linking tax returns with records of earnings and benefits
for the same households over time, and interagency data sharing under clear rules for how
the records can be used. Researchers should also have easier access to DOTAX’s tax-return
data and other data on the economic lives of Hawaiʻi households, including their incomes
and government benefits.
Some of our questions can only be answered with tax records linked to other agencies’ records.
Researchers in California, for example, linked tax-filing, wage and benefit records to estimate
1 The evidence cited in this report does not provide comparable Hawaiʻi estimates of tax-induced
migration and reported income, how much of the GET businesses pass on through prices, luxury-
goods demand, lodging-tax responses by hotel class, construction responses to property taxes, solar
and housing activity caused by credits, the division of solar subsidies between buyers and installers,
inheritances’ effects on work, or future tax revenue and savings on benefits as children grow up.

which families getting food or cash assistance were likely to receive the child tax credit.2 The
same kind of work here would show how many eligible Hawaiʻi families miss out on credits
because they do not file.
This can be done while respecting the legal and privacy obligations of each agency. Names
and other identifying details can be removed before the records are linked, only trained re-
searchers would be given access, and the data would be kept on secure servers. Results would
be checked for personal information before they are released.3 Hawaiʻi could then use more
of its own experience to assess tax changes, with less need to draw on evidence from other
places.

Technical appendices
A. Data, current law and revenue reconciliation
The paired changes to the GET, income tax and food/excise tax credit in Chapter 10 are
designed to hold State General Fund revenue constant in 2027 only. County revenue effects
will be reported separately.
In Table 1.1, DOTAX’s FY2025 total of $11.46 billion includes $565.3 million of county GET
surcharges and $87.8 million of county fuel tax that the state collects for the counties (DOTAX,
State Tax Collections and Distribution, June 2025, fiscal year to date, p.1). County property
taxes are the amounts the four counties levied for FY2025: $1,733.4 million in Honolulu,
$585.4 million in Maui, $507.5 million in Hawaiʻi County and $235.1 million in Kauaʻi, or
$3,061.4 million in all (City and County of Honolulu, Real Property Assessment Division,
Statewide Real Property Tax Valuation for Tax Year 2024-2025). These are amounts levied,
not collections. County TATs are for FY2025: $104.9 million in Honolulu and $72.2 million in
Maui1 , from their annual comprehensive financial reports (Honolulu, PDF p. 60; Maui, PDF
p. 47), and $31.0 million in Kauaʻi and $37.2 million in Hawaiʻi County, from the counties’
unaudited continuing disclosure annual reports for their general obligation bonds (Kauaʻi,
Section VIII; Hawaiʻi County, Table 21). Kauaʻi’s figure is the amount received during the year,
so it is not strictly comparable with the others. The state and county rows add the county
surcharges to the state GET and use tax ($4,630.4 million), and the county TATs to the state
TAT ($816.2 million; DOTAX, Annual Report 2024-2025, Table 1.8).
B. Evidence standards and methods
In the final report, this appendix will set out the standards we use to judge evidence and how we
choose among estimates from other places and periods.
We don’t know exactly how people and businesses will respond to a tax change. We give
estimates based on different plausible assumptions, and choose a preferred estimate only
when there is much more evidence for it than for the others. Evidence from past reforms
may be less informative about very different reforms.2 But no matter what method we use,
estimating the effect of a large policy change will always involve substantial uncertainty.
1 Honolulu’s total includes the share for transit. City and County of Honolulu, Annual Comprehensive
Financial Report, Fiscal Year Ended June 30, 2025, PDF p.60, https://www.honolulu.gov/bfs/w
p-content/uploads/sites/62/2026/01/CC-Honolulu-ACFR-FY2025-PDF-A.pdf; County of
Maui, Annual Comprehensive Financial Report for the Fiscal Year Ended June 30, 2025, PDF p.47,
https://www.mauicounty.gov/DocumentCenter/View/157120/FY2025-ACFR-FINAL.
2 Readers may wonder why we do not always give a single number. A study can find an effect that is
not statistically different from zero and still be consistent with a large effect, so a range of estimates
often tells us more than the number in the middle. Responses can vary with what is taxed, how the
tax is enforced and how visible a change is (Saez, Slemrod and Giertz 2012).

C. The Hawaiʻi household model
The final report will add full documentation of the Hawaiʻi household model, its data, how it is
weighted to Hawaiʻi totals, and how it compares with Hawaiʻi data.
The household model has 66,000 synthetic household records that together represent
Hawaiʻi’s resident households. We start with Hawaiʻi households from the Census Bureau’s
Current Population Survey and use other data to add income and spending missing from the
survey.3 We calculate each household’s federal and state income taxes, payroll taxes, GET on
spending and benefits such as SNAP, both under current law and with the proposed change.
The model is designed to reflect key features of Hawaiʻi’s population and economy and to
match 381 statewide totals. It matches all of them. These include the number of people and
households, federal returns, income, deductions and taxes, the Hawaiʻi income tax residents
reported on their 2022 returns at each income level, state tax credits, SNAP cases and benefits,
and Medicaid enrollment. A separate account of the GET matches the GET and use tax DOTAX
collected in 2024.
The grocery estimate starts from projected taxable grocery sales of $5.942 billion in 2027.
Applying the 4 percent state rate and 0.5 percent county surcharge gives revenue losses of
$237.68 million and $29.71 million before changes in spending. We assume that visitors make
19.92 percent of these purchases. Residents then save $214.13 million directly if stores pass
the full exemption on to shoppers. We assign savings to households based on their taxable
grocery purchases and estimate how much additional GET residents pay when they spend the
savings (chapters 3 and 10).
For income-tax changes, we use published research to allow for changes in reported income,
the timing of asset sales and decisions about whether to work. The model does not account for
people moving to or from Hawaiʻi, or for changes in visitor spending. For the GET, it includes
the spending of residents’ grocery savings but otherwise holds purchases fixed.4
D. Response estimates from the literature
The final report will expand this appendix to list the response estimates we borrow, with each
one’s place, period and population, and to set out the formula for the top income tax rate beyond
which a higher rate loses revenue (section 2.3).
A higher rate in the top bracket raises revenue from everyone above its threshold, while chang-
ing the incentive to earn the next dollar only for people in that bracket (section 2.3; Saez
2001). So in theory, and up to a point, different rates at different incomes can raise the same
revenue at a lower cost than one rate on everyone. But there is a limit. At some point we
3 The missing income comes from IRS tax records, and spending from the Bureau of Labor Statistics’
Consumer Expenditure Survey, adjusted to published Honolulu averages. The statewide totals come
from the American Community Survey, IRS Statistics of Income and EITC data, DOTAX’s tables of
income tax returns and credits, and data from the U.S. Department of Agriculture and the Centers
for Medicare & Medicaid Services on SNAP and Medicaid. PolicyEngine US provides the foundation
for the report’s household calculations (PolicyEngine, PolicyEngine US, https://policyengine.githu
b.io/policyengine-us/).

E. Who bears each tax: residents and nonresidents
In the final report, this appendix will explain how we split each tax between residents and non-
residents, and the assumptions about who bears it.

The solar credit cost-benefit model, and how its results change with the assumptions (chapter 8),
will be set out here in the final report.
K. Film credit: summary statistics
The motion picture, digital media, and film production income tax credit (HRS §235-17) is
a refundable credit of 22 percent of qualified production costs incurred on Oʻahu and 27
percent on the other islands.6 This appendix reports summary statistics on the credit from
two public sources. Table K.1 gives the credit claimed on tax returns in each tax year, as
reported by DOTAX, together with the annual limit on total credits. Table K.2 gives figures by
production year from DBEDT’s annual reports on the credit, which draw on the production
reports that productions file with the Hawaiʻi Film Office: the number of productions, qualified
spending and credits, how spending divides between residents and nonresidents and between
Hawaiʻi and out-of-state businesses, hires, and spending by island. The two sources measure
5 Saez, Slemrod and Giertz (2012).
6 HRS §235-17(a), (c). Act 185 (SLH 2026) adds 5 percentage points for qualified productions whose
workforce is at least 80 percent local hires, for costs incurred after December 31, 2025 (Act 185,
SLH 2026 (https://data.capitol.hawaii.gov/sessions/session2026/bills/GM1287_.PDF), §§1 and 7;
DOTAX, Tax Information Release 2026-03, p.1).
